  Psalms 14:4 — close honoring those who fear God: Sirach identifies those fearing the Lord as honored in His eyes; the psalm makes honoring such people a mark of the faithful worshiper, alongside contempt for wicked conduct.

Sirach 10:22
Rich, renowned, and poor alike—their boast is the fear of the Lord.
  Jeremiah 9:22-23 — parallel God-directed boasting above wealth or standing: Sirach gives rich, renowned and poor the same boast in fear of the Lord; Jeremiah rejects boasting in wealth, strength or wisdom and directs it toward understanding and knowing the Lord.

Sirach 10:23
It is not right to dishonor an understanding poor person, and it is not fitting to glorify a sinful man.
  Ecclesiastes 9:15-16 — warning and example concerning despised poor wisdom: Sirach says it is wrong to dishonor an understanding poor person; Ecclesiastes describes the corresponding injustice of a poor man's saving wisdom being forgotten and despised.

Sirach 10:24
A dignitary, judge, and ruler will be glorified, but none of them is greater than the one who fears the Lord.
  Acts 10:34-35 — related divine regard beyond human distinctions: Sirach denies that recognized officeholders are greater than the person who fears the Lord; Peter similarly grounds divine acceptance in fear and righteous conduct rather than partiality, without reproducing the same ranking of offices.

Sirach 10:25
Free people will serve a wise household slave, and a knowledgeable man will not grumble.
  Proverbs 17:2 — close wise servant ruling social superiors: Sirach says free people will serve a wise household slave; Proverbs likewise says an intelligent household servant will rule foolish masters.

Sirach 10:27
Better someone who works and has abundance in everything than someone who walks about enjoying glory but lacks bread.
  Proverbs 12:9 — close useful work contrasted with hungry pretension: Sirach prefers a working person with enough to one displaying glory without bread; Proverbs likewise prefers the dishonored person who serves himself to one clothed in honor but needing bread.

Sirach 10:28
Child, glorify your soul with gentleness, and give it honor according to its worth.
  1 Peter 3:4 — parallel inward honor expressed in gentleness: Sirach urges giving the soul honor with gentleness; Peter likewise identifies the hidden heart's gentle spirit as imperishable beauty precious before God.

Sirach 10:29
Who will justify the one who sins against his own soul? Who will glorify the one who dishonors his own life?
  Proverbs 15:32 — related moral injury against one's own life: Sirach questions justification or honor for one harming his own soul; Proverbs similarly describes rejecting discipline as hatred of oneself and accepting rebuke as love for one's life.

Sirach 10:30
A poor person is glorified for his knowledge, and a rich person is glorified for his wealth.
  Ecclesiastes 9:16 — contrast in the recognition of poor wisdom: Sirach names knowledge as a poor person's ground for honor; Ecclesiastes observes that the poor man's wisdom is nevertheless despised, exposing the social failure to give that honor.

Sirach 11:1
The wisdom of a humble person will raise his head and seat him among dignitaries.
  1 Samuel 2:8 — close elevation of the lowly to seats with rulers: Sirach says humble wisdom raises a person's head to sit among dignitaries; Hannah's song likewise describes God lifting the poor to sit with rulers, without naming wisdom as the means.

Sirach 11:2
Do not praise a man for his beauty or loathe a person for his appearance.
  1 Samuel 16:7 — close rejection of judgment by appearance: Sirach forbids praise or contempt based on appearance; God likewise tells Samuel not to judge by appearance or height, contrasting the face with the heart.

Sirach 11:3
The bee is small among winged creatures, but her product is foremost among sweet things.
  Proverbs 6:8a-8c — close esteem for the small but productive bee: Sirach contrasts the bee's small size with her excellent sweet product; Proverbs likewise praises her useful, esteemed labor despite bodily weakness.

Sirach 11:4
Do not boast in the clothing you wear or exalt yourself on a day of glory. For the Lord’s works are wonderful, and His works are hidden from human beings.
  1 Peter 3:3-4 — parallel restraint of outward adornment's importance: Sirach warns against boasting in clothing; Peter likewise shifts valued adornment from garments and ornaments to the hidden heart's gentle spirit, without repeating Sirach's account of hidden divine works.

Sirach 11:5
Many rulers have sat upon the ground, while someone unsuspected wore a diadem.
  Luke 1:52 — parallel reversal of ruling status: Sirach describes rulers on the ground and an unexpected wearer of a crown; Mary's song likewise contrasts rulers brought down from thrones with lowly people raised up.

Sirach 11:6
Many powerful people have been greatly dishonored, and the renowned were handed into others’ hands.
  Luke 1:51-52 — related humbling of the powerful: Sirach recalls powerful and renowned people dishonored and handed over; Mary's song likewise describes proud people scattered and rulers brought down, without naming the same captors.

Sirach 11:7
Do not find fault before investigating; understand first, and then rebuke.
  John 7:50-51 — close investigation before judgment: Sirach requires investigation and understanding before rebuke; Nicodemus likewise asks whether the Law judges before hearing a person and knowing the facts.

Sirach 11:8
Do not answer before hearing, and do not interrupt in the middle of someone’s words.
  Proverbs 18:13 — close hearing before answering: Both reject answering before hearing; Sirach also expressly prohibits interrupting another person's words.

Sirach 11:9
Do not quarrel about a matter that is no concern of yours, and do not sit together in the judgment of sinners.
  Proverbs 26:17 — close warning against another person's dispute: Sirach warns against quarrels that do not concern oneself; Proverbs likewise compares presiding over another's dispute with grasping a dog's tail.

Sirach 11:10
Child, do not let your activities concern too many things. If you multiply them, you will not escape blame; even if you pursue, you will certainly not catch up, and even by running away you will certainly not escape.
  Ecclesiastes 4:6 — related restraint of excessive toil: Sirach warns that multiplying activities cannot secure escape from blame or frustration; Ecclesiastes similarly prefers a handful of rest to two handfuls of toil, without stating the same pursuit-and-flight paradox.

Sirach 11:11
There is someone who toils, labors, and hurries, yet is all the more in want.
  Psalms 126:1-2 — parallel failure of strenuous toil to secure its hoped-for result: Sirach observes labor and haste ending in greater want; the psalm likewise calls early rising and painful labor vain apart from the Lord's action, providing a related theological account of frustrated effort.

Sirach 11:12
There is someone slow who needs support, lacking strength and abounding in poverty. Yet the Lord’s eyes looked upon him for good, and He raised him from his low estate
  1 Samuel 2:8 — close divine lifting of the helpless poor: Sirach's weak, impoverished person is raised when the Lord looks kindly on him; Hannah's song likewise describes God lifting the poor from the earth and dung heap.

Sirach 11:13
and lifted his head, and many marveled at him.
  1 Samuel 2:8 — parallel public elevation of the lowly: The lifted head continues Sirach's account of the impoverished person's reversal; Hannah's song likewise describes the poor receiving a seat with rulers. It does not expressly report the spectators' marveling.

Sirach 11:14
Good things and bad, life and death, poverty and wealth are from the Lord.
  1 Samuel 2:6-7 — close divine authority over life, death, poverty and wealth: Sirach attributes life's opposing conditions to the Lord; Hannah's song likewise explicitly gives Him authority to kill, enliven, impoverish and enrich.

Sirach 11:17
The Lord’s gift remains with the godly, and His good pleasure will prosper for ever.
  Psalms 36:18-19 — parallel enduring provision for the godly: Sirach says the Lord's gift remains with the godly; the psalm likewise promises the blameless an enduring inheritance and provision in evil times.

Sirach 11:19
when he says, “I have found rest, and now I will eat from my good things,” he does not know how much time will pass before he leaves them to others and dies.
  Luke 12:19-20 — close rich man's promised rest interrupted by death: Both portray a person promising himself rest and enjoyment of stored goods while failing to reckon with death and the goods passing beyond his control.

Sirach 11:20
Stand firm in your covenant, occupy yourself with it, and grow old in your work.
  1 Corinthians 15:58 — parallel steadfast perseverance in faithful work: Sirach counsels remaining in one's covenant and growing old in work; Paul likewise urges steadfast abundance in the Lord's work, grounding it specifically in resurrection hope.

Sirach 11:21
Do not marvel at a sinner’s works, but trust the Lord and remain at your labor. For it is easy in the Lord’s eyes to make a poor person rich quickly and suddenly.
  Psalms 36:3-7 — close trust and perseverance instead of envy at sinners: Sirach rejects amazement at sinners' success while counseling trust and labor; the psalm likewise joins hope in the Lord and good action with refusal to envy the prospering lawless.

Sirach 11:22
The Lord’s blessing is in the godly person’s reward, and in a swift moment He makes His blessing flourish.
  Proverbs 10:22 — close blessing as the source of righteous prosperity: Sirach attributes the godly person's flourishing reward to the Lord's blessing; Proverbs likewise says that His blessing rests upon the righteous and makes rich.

Sirach 11:24
Do not say, “I have enough; what harm can come to me from now on?”
  Psalms 9:27 — close presumptuous denial of future adversity: Sirach forbids saying that sufficient possessions exclude future harm; the psalm depicts a sinner similarly declaring that he will remain unshaken and without adversity.

Sirach 11:25
On a day of good things, bad things are forgotten; on a day of bad things, good things will not be remembered.
  Ecclesiastes 7:14 — related counsel concerning alternating good and bad days: Sirach observes that each kind of day drives out memory of the other; Ecclesiastes likewise pairs days of goodness and evil, urging consideration of God's arrangement rather than assuming present conditions exhaust reality.

Sirach 11:26
For it is easy before the Lord, on the day of death, to repay a person according to his ways.
  Romans 2:6-8 — close repayment according to deeds: Sirach places repayment according to one's ways at death; Romans likewise announces divine repayment according to works, within its description of the day of righteous judgment.

Sirach 11:28
Call no one happy before his death, and a man will be known in his children.
  Ecclesiastes 7:1-2 — related assessment of life in view of its end: Sirach postpones calling a person happy until death; Ecclesiastes likewise gives the day of death priority over birth and makes life's end instructive. The statement about knowing a man through his children is not repeated there.

Sirach 11:29
Do not bring every person into your house, for the deceitful person has many ambushes.
  Psalms 100:7 — parallel exclusion of deceptive house companions: Sirach warns against receiving everyone into one's house because of deceitful ambushes; the psalm likewise excludes arrogance from the house and unjust speech from the speaker's presence.

Sirach 11:30
Like a decoy partridge in a basket is the heart of a proud person; like a spy, he watches for a fall.
  Psalms 55:6-7 — related concealed watching for another's fall: Sirach likens a proud person's heart to a decoy and watching spy; the psalm likewise describes hostile people concealing themselves and watching the speaker's heel, without using the partridge image.

Sirach 11:31
For he lies in wait, turning good things into bad, and will attach blame to what is choice.
  Isaiah 5:20 — close moral inversion of good and evil: Sirach describes an ambusher turning good things into bad and blaming what is choice; Isaiah likewise condemns calling good evil and evil good, without specifying the same ambush.

Sirach 11:32
From a spark of fire a bed of coals grows, and a sinful person lies in ambush for blood.
  James 3:5-6 — close small-fire image applied to growing evil: Sirach joins a spark growing into coals with a sinner's ambush for blood; James likewise uses a small fire spreading widely to describe the destructive tongue. The particular forms of wrongdoing differ.

Sirach 11:33
Beware of an evildoer, for he devises evil, lest he give you lasting blame.
  Proverbs 1:10-19 — parallel caution against planned evil: Sirach warns against an evildoer's plans and lasting blame; Proverbs likewise counsels distance from people planning bloodshed and explains the ruin entailed by their paths.

Sirach 12:2
Do good to a godly person, and you will find repayment; if not from him, then from the Most High.
  Matthew 10:41-42 — parallel reward for receiving the righteous: Sirach promises repayment from the godly recipient or the Most High; Jesus likewise promises reward for receiving a righteous person or giving even a cup of water in a disciple's name.

Sirach 12:3
There will be no good things for the one who persists in evil and the one who gives no alms.
  Proverbs 21:13 — parallel loss of help through refusal of charity: Sirach denies good things to one withholding alms; Proverbs likewise says one who refuses to hear the weak will call without receiving a hearing. Sirach also includes persistence in evil.

Sirach 12:4
Give to the godly person, and do not support the sinner.
  Luke 6:35 — contrast over doing good to sinners: Sirach limits support to the godly and forbids supporting a sinner; Jesus commands good toward enemies and describes the Most High as kind to the ungrateful and evil. The different directions are preserved.

Sirach 12:5
Do good to the humble person, and do not give to the ungodly. Hold back his loaves and do not give them to him, so that he does not use them to overpower you. For you will receive double evils for all the good things you do to him.
  Romans 12:20-21 — contrast over feeding a dangerous adversary: Sirach counsels withholding loaves from the ungodly to avoid being overpowered; Paul instead commands feeding a hungry enemy and overcoming evil with good, rather than presenting the two instructions as identical.

Sirach 12:6
For the Most High Himself has hated sinners and will repay the ungodly with retribution.
  Psalms 5:6-7 — close divine hatred and judgment of wrongdoing: Sirach grounds retribution in the Most High's hatred of sinners; the psalm likewise joins hatred of lawlessness's practitioners with destruction of falsehood and abhorrence of bloodshed and deceit.

Sirach 12:7
Give to the good person, and do not support the sinner.
  Galatians 6:10 — contrast in the extent of benevolence: Sirach says to give to the good and not support the sinner; Paul gives special attention to the household of faith while expressly extending good to everyone.

Sirach 12:8
A friend will not be vindicated in prosperity, and an enemy will not remain hidden in adversity.
  Proverbs 17:17 — related testing of friendship in adversity: Sirach says prosperity does not vindicate a friend and adversity exposes an enemy; Proverbs calls for a friend at all times and brothers useful in need, stating the fidelity such testing seeks.

Sirach 12:9
When a man prospers, his enemies are in grief; when he suffers adversity, even his friend will separate himself.
  Proverbs 19:4 — close poverty and abandoned friendship observation: Sirach observes that even a friend separates in adversity; Proverbs similarly contrasts wealth's many friends with the poor person abandoned by his existing friend.

Sirach 12:10
Never trust your enemy, for as copper corrodes, so does his wickedness.
  Proverbs 26:24-26 — parallel distrust of concealed enmity: Sirach compares an enemy's wickedness with copper's corrosion; Proverbs likewise exposes deceptive lips concealing hostility, without the corrosion comparison.

Sirach 12:11
Even if he humbles himself and walks bowed down, keep your soul alert and guard against him. You will be toward him like someone who has wiped a mirror, and you will know that it has not completely corroded.
  Proverbs 26:24-25 — parallel suspicion of an enemy's conciliatory behavior: Sirach warns against trusting bowed, apparently humble behavior; Proverbs likewise says not to be persuaded by an enemy's tearful entreaty because hidden evil remains. The wiped-mirror image is not repeated.

Sirach 12:13
Who will pity a snake-charmer bitten by a serpent, or all those who approach wild beasts?
  Ecclesiastes 10:11 — close snake-charmer and snakebite image: Sirach asks who would pity a charmer bitten by a serpent; Ecclesiastes likewise describes a snake biting without a charm and no advantage for the charmer, without posing the same question about pity.

Sirach 12:14
So it is with the one who approaches a sinful man and becomes entangled in his sins.
  Proverbs 1:15-19 — parallel danger of joining sinful companions: Sirach warns that approaching a sinner entangles one in his sins; Proverbs likewise commands distance from lawless companions' paths and describes their destructive outcome.

Sirach 12:15
He will stay with you for an hour, but if you turn aside, he will certainly not hold firm.
  Proverbs 19:4 — related instability of conditional friendship: Sirach describes an associate who stays briefly but does not hold firm if the addressee turns aside; Proverbs describes abandonment in poverty, a different stated condition for failed loyalty.

Sirach 12:16
An enemy will make his lips sweet, but in his heart he will plan to throw you into a pit. An enemy will weep with his eyes, but if he finds the occasion, he will not be satisfied with blood.
  Proverbs 26:24-26 — close weeping lips concealing hostile plans: Both depict an enemy whose agreeable, tearful outward speech masks deception in his heart; Sirach additionally specifies a pit and appetite for blood.

Sirach 12:17
If evil things meet you, you will find him there before you; as though helping, he will undermine your heel.
  Psalms 40:8-10 — related betrayal beneath an appearance of help: Sirach depicts an enemy posing as a helper while undermining one's heel; the psalm joins whispered hostile plans with treachery by a trusted table companion, without repeating the helper's pretense verbatim.

Sirach 12:18
He will shake his head and clap his hands, whisper many things, and change his expression.
  Lamentations 2:15-16 — close hostile clapping and head-shaking gestures: Sirach describes the enemy shaking his head and clapping; Lamentations likewise depicts onlookers clapping and shaking their heads at fallen Jerusalem alongside hostile speech.

Sirach 13:1
The one who touches pitch will be stained, and the one who associates with a proud person will become like him.
  Proverbs 22:24-25 — parallel harmful conduct learned through association: Sirach says association with the proud makes a person like them; Proverbs similarly warns that companionship with an angry person teaches his ways, using a different harmful disposition.

Sirach 13:2
Do not lift a burden beyond you, and do not associate with someone stronger and richer than you. What fellowship can an earthen pot have with a cauldron? One will strike, and the other will be shattered.
  Proverbs 23:4-5 — close warning against a poor person's attempt to match the rich: Sirach warns against burdens beyond one's strength and unequal association with the wealthy; Proverbs likewise tells a poor person not to stretch himself to match a rich man. The pottery collision is Sirach's distinct image.

Sirach 13:3
A rich person has done wrong, yet he has added threats; a poor person has been wronged, yet he will add entreaties.
  James 2:6 — parallel coercion by the wealthy against the poor: Sirach contrasts a rich wrongdoer's threats with the injured poor person's entreaties; James likewise calls the rich oppressors who drag others into courts, without describing the same entreaties.

Sirach 13:4
If you are useful, he makes use of you; if you are in want, he will abandon you.
  Proverbs 19:4 — close wealth-dependent companionship: Sirach's wealthy associate uses someone useful but abandons one in need; Proverbs likewise contrasts wealth's many friends with abandonment in poverty.

Sirach 13:6
He needs you, and he will mislead you, smile at you, and give you hope. He will say pleasant things to you and ask, “What do you need?”
  Proverbs 26:24-26 — parallel pleasant speech concealing an exploitative purpose: Sirach exposes smiles and hopeful promises used to mislead someone needed by the speaker; Proverbs likewise exposes agreeable lips and entreaties concealing deceit and enmity.

Sirach 13:7
He will shame you with his food until he has emptied you out twice or three times, and in the end he will mock you. After this he will see you and abandon you and shake his head at you.
  Proverbs 23:6-8 — parallel harmful hospitality from an ungenerous host: Sirach warns that a rich person's food can lead to humiliation and depletion; Proverbs likewise warns against the begrudging host's food and the ruin of good words, without describing repeated depletion in the same way.

Sirach 13:9
When a powerful person summons you, be retiring, and he will summon you all the more.
  Proverbs 25:6-7 — close restraint before a powerful host's invitation: Sirach advises reserve when summoned by the powerful; Proverbs similarly discourages self-placement among rulers and prefers receiving an invitation to come higher.

Sirach 13:10
Do not thrust yourself forward, lest you be pushed away; do not stand too far off, so that you are not forgotten.
  Luke 14:8-10 — close avoidance of self-promotion and public displacement: Sirach warns that thrusting oneself forward leads to being pushed away; Jesus likewise counsels taking a lower place so that the host invites one higher rather than removing one in shame. Sirach's caution against excessive distance is additional.

Sirach 13:12
Merciless is the one who does not keep words to himself, and he will certainly not spare you harm and bonds.
  Proverbs 11:13 — related danger of indiscreet disclosure: Sirach warns about a merciless person who does not keep words to himself; Proverbs likewise contrasts disclosure of plans by the double-tongued with faithful concealment. It does not promise the same harm or bonds.

Sirach 13:17
What fellowship can a wolf have with a lamb? So it is with a sinner and a godly person.
  2 Corinthians 6:14-15 — close incompatible-fellowship question: Sirach asks what fellowship a wolf has with a lamb and applies it to sinner and godly person; Paul likewise asks what sharing righteousness has with lawlessness and a believer with an unbeliever.

Sirach 13:18
What peace can a hyena have with a dog? What peace can a rich person have with a poor one?
  James 2:6 — related antagonism between rich and poor: Sirach questions peace between rich and poor through an animal comparison; James supplies the concrete antagonism of rich people oppressing others and taking them to court, without the hyena-and-dog image.

Sirach 13:19
Wild asses in the wilderness are prey for lions; so the poor are feeding grounds for the rich.
  Micah 3:1-3 — close predatory consumption image for social oppression: Sirach compares the poor as feeding grounds for the rich with wild asses as lions' prey; Micah likewise depicts rulers consuming the people's flesh, turning exploitation into a bodily feeding image.

Sirach 13:20
Lowliness is an abomination to the proud; so a poor person is an abomination to the rich.
  James 2:2-4 — parallel contempt for poverty amid social pride: Sirach describes the poor as abhorrent to the rich; James exposes the corresponding favoritism that gives the well-dressed person a good seat and demeans the poor person.

Sirach 13:21
A rich person who is shaken is supported by friends; a lowly person who falls is pushed away still further by friends.
  Proverbs 19:4 — close wealth and poverty's opposite effects on friends: Sirach contrasts friends supporting a shaken rich person with pushing a fallen poor person farther away; Proverbs likewise contrasts multiplying friends through wealth with a poor person's abandonment.

Sirach 13:22
When a rich person stumbles, many support him. He spoke things that should not be spoken, and they justified him. A lowly person stumbled, and they added rebuke; he spoke with understanding, and no place was given him.
  Isaiah 5:23 — related unjust vindication linked to material advantage: Sirach describes excusing a rich person's improper speech while rebuking a poor person; Isaiah condemns vindicating the ungodly for gifts and removing the righteous person's right. The social deference and explicit bribery are related but distinct mechanisms.

Sirach 13:23
A rich person spoke, and everyone fell silent; they raised his word to the clouds. A poor person spoke, and they said, “Who is this?” If he stumbles, they will throw him down all the more.
  Ecclesiastes 9:16-17 — close disregard for poor wisdom beside powerful speech: Sirach contrasts the audience's reverence for rich speech with contempt for poor speech; Ecclesiastes likewise says poor wisdom is despised and unheard, while preferring quiet wise words to rulers' shouting.

Sirach 13:24
Wealth is good when no sin accompanies it, and poverty is evil in the mouth of the ungodly.
  Proverbs 10:2 — related moral qualification of wealth's benefit: Sirach calls wealth good when free of sin; Proverbs likewise denies that treasures benefit the lawless and contrasts them with rescuing righteousness. The statement about poverty in ungodly speech remains distinct.

Sirach 13:25
A person’s heart changes his face, whether for good or for evil.
  Proverbs 15:13 — close heart reflected in the face: Sirach says the heart changes one's face for good or evil; Proverbs likewise contrasts a flourishing face from a glad heart with a gloomy face from sorrow.

Sirach 13:26
A cheerful face is the trace of a heart in good circumstances, and finding proverbs requires laborious deliberations.
  Ecclesiastes 12:9-10 — related painstaking search for wise sayings: Sirach says finding proverbs requires laborious deliberation; Ecclesiastes likewise describes an ear tracing an orderly arrangement of proverbs and the search for delightful, upright words. Sirach's cheerful-face clause is separate.

Sirach 14:1
Happy is the man who has not slipped with his mouth and has not been pierced by grief over sins.
  James 3:2 — close commendation of avoiding a slip in speech: Sirach calls the person who has not slipped with his mouth happy; James likewise treats not stumbling in speech as a mark of a complete person, without repeating the grief-over-sins clause.

Sirach 14:2
Happy is the one whose soul has not condemned him and who has not fallen away from his hope.
  1 John 3:21 — close confidence when the inner person does not condemn: Sirach blesses the one whose soul has not condemned him; John likewise connects a heart that does not condemn with confidence before God.

Sirach 14:3
Wealth is not good for a miserly man; what are possessions for a grudging person?
  Ecclesiastes 6:2 — related futility of possessions not enjoyed by their owner: Sirach questions wealth's value to a miserly, grudging owner; Ecclesiastes describes possessions the owner cannot enjoy while a stranger consumes them, assigning the inability to a different cause.

Sirach 14:4
The one who gathers by depriving his own soul gathers for others, and strangers will enjoy his good things.
  Ecclesiastes 6:2 — close wealth accumulated for another's enjoyment: Sirach says self-depriving accumulation lets strangers enjoy one's goods; Ecclesiastes likewise depicts a stranger enjoying the wealthy owner's possessions, without attributing that outcome specifically to self-denial.

Sirach 14:5
To whom will the one who is bad to himself be good? He will certainly not rejoice in his possessions.
  Proverbs 11:17 — related connection between mercy and treatment of one's own life: Sirach questions how one who is bad to himself can be good to others; Proverbs likewise links mercy with doing good to one's own life and mercilessness with bodily destruction.

Sirach 14:8
Evil is the one who begrudges with his eye, turning his face away and disregarding souls.
  Deuteronomy 15:9 — close evil-eye image for withholding generosity: Sirach describes the grudging eye turning away and disregarding souls; Deuteronomy likewise warns against an evil eye toward a needy brother that gives him nothing.

Sirach 14:9
A greedy person’s eye is not satisfied with its portion, and evil injustice dries up the soul.
  Ecclesiastes 5:9-10 — parallel insatiable desire for possessions: Sirach says the greedy eye is never satisfied with its share; Ecclesiastes likewise says love of silver is not filled by silver and questions wealth's benefit beyond looking at it.

Sirach 14:10
An evil eye begrudges bread, and his own table is lacking.
  Proverbs 23:6-8 — close begrudging bread at an inhospitable table: Sirach describes an evil eye begrudging bread and an impoverished table; Proverbs likewise warns against the begrudging man's food and the harm of sharing a meal with him.

Sirach 14:11
Child, do yourself good according to what you have, and bring offerings worthily to the Lord.
  Deuteronomy 16:16-17 — parallel worthy offering according to one's means: Sirach joins doing good from what one has with bringing worthy offerings; Deuteronomy likewise forbids appearing empty before the Lord and measures each gift by the giver's ability and blessing.

Sirach 14:12
Remember that death will not delay, and the covenant of Hades has not been shown to you.
  Ecclesiastes 9:12 — parallel uncertainty about the time of death: Sirach reminds the reader that death will not delay and Hades's covenant has not been disclosed; Ecclesiastes likewise says a person does not know his time and can be caught suddenly, without using the covenant expression.

Sirach 14:13
Before you die, do good to a friend; according to your strength, reach out and give to him.
  Proverbs 3:27-28 — close timely generosity while the hand can help: Sirach urges giving to a friend before death and according to one's strength; Proverbs likewise commands present help when the hand has means and warns against postponing it because the next day is unknown.

Sirach 14:14
Do not miss a good day, and do not let your share of a good desire pass you by.
  Ecclesiastes 9:7-9 — parallel enjoyment of one's available share in life: Sirach warns against missing a good day and one's share of good desire; Ecclesiastes likewise urges glad food, drink and shared life as one's portion beneath the sun.

Sirach 14:15
Will you not leave your labors to another, and your toils to the division of an inheritance?
  Ecclesiastes 2:18-21 — close toil passing to a successor: Sirach asks whether one's labor will be left for another's inheritance; Ecclesiastes likewise laments leaving the fruits of wise toil to a successor who did not perform it.

Sirach 14:16
Give and receive, and beguile your soul, for in Hades there is no seeking luxury.
  Ecclesiastes 9:7-10 — parallel present enjoyment before Hades ends earthly activity: Sirach urges giving, receiving and enjoyment because luxury is not sought in Hades; Ecclesiastes likewise urges glad living and vigorous action before Hades, where work and knowledge are absent.

Sirach 14:17
All flesh grows old like a garment, for the covenant from eternity is, “You will surely die.”
  Isaiah 51:6 — close garment-like aging and mortality image: Sirach says flesh grows old like a garment under death's decree; Isaiah similarly says earth grows old like a garment and its inhabitants die, contrasting this with enduring divine salvation.

Sirach 14:18
Like flourishing foliage on a leafy tree—some it casts down, while other leaves it grows—so is a generation of flesh and blood: one dies, and another is born.
  Ecclesiastes 1:4 — close succession of mortal generations: Sirach's falling and growing leaves illustrate one generation dying as another is born; Ecclesiastes likewise says a generation goes and a generation comes, without the leaf image.

Sirach 14:19
Every decaying work comes to an end, and its maker will depart with it.
  Psalms 101:26-28 — contrast between mortal makers and the enduring Creator: Sirach says a decaying work and its maker both depart; the psalm contrasts perishable, garment-like created works with the Lord who remains unchanged. The difference between human makers and God is explicit.

Sirach 14:20
Happy is the man who will meditate on wisdom and speak with his understanding,
  Psalms 1:1-2 — parallel blessedness in sustained meditation: Sirach blesses meditation on wisdom; the psalm likewise blesses the person whose desire and day-and-night meditation are in the Lord's law.

Sirach 14:21
who considers her ways in his heart and will reflect upon her hidden things.
  Proverbs 2:2-5 — parallel inward and searching attention to wisdom: Sirach directs the heart toward wisdom's ways and hidden things; Proverbs likewise directs heart and ear toward understanding and urges searching for it as for treasure.

Sirach 14:22
Go out after her like a tracker, and lie in wait on her paths.
  Proverbs 2:3-5 — parallel active pursuit of wisdom: Sirach imagines tracking wisdom along her paths; Proverbs similarly calls for urgent seeking and treasure-like searching, using a different pursuit image.

Sirach 14:23
The one who peers through her windows will also listen at her doorways;
  Proverbs 8:34 — close listening at wisdom's entrance: Sirach depicts peering through wisdom's windows and listening at her doorways; personified wisdom in Proverbs likewise blesses the listener watching daily at her doors and entrances.

Sirach 14:24
the one who lodges near her house will fix a tent peg in her walls.
  Proverbs 8:34 — close abiding beside wisdom's dwelling: Sirach pitches lodging and a tent peg near wisdom's house; Proverbs likewise pictures persistent attendance at her doors and entrance-posts, without the same tent details.

Sirach 14:25
He will pitch his tent beside her and lodge in a lodging of good things.
  Proverbs 9:1-6 — related welcome into wisdom's beneficial house: Sirach's tent beside wisdom offers lodging in good things; Proverbs likewise presents wisdom's built house and invitation to life-giving food and understanding, without describing the guest's tent.

Sirach 14:26
He will place his children in her shelter and spend the night beneath her branches.
  Proverbs 3:18 — close wisdom as a protective tree: Sirach places children in wisdom's shelter and the learner beneath her branches; Proverbs likewise calls wisdom a tree of life and a secure support for those holding and leaning upon her, without specifying children.

Sirach 14:27
He will be sheltered by her from the heat and lodge in her glory.
  Isaiah 4:5-6 — related glorious shelter from heat: Sirach makes wisdom a shelter from heat and dwelling in glory; Isaiah similarly depicts glory-covered Zion as shade from heat and shelter, in a different setting.

Sirach 15:1
The one who fears the Lord will do this, and the one who holds fast to the law will gain her.
  Deuteronomy 4:5-6 — close wisdom gained by keeping divine instruction: Sirach joins gaining wisdom with holding fast to the law; Deuteronomy likewise calls keeping and doing God's ordinances Israel's wisdom and understanding.

Sirach 15:2
She will meet him like a mother and welcome him like a wife of his youth.
  Wisdom of Solomon 8:2 — close wisdom portrayed as a desired wife: Sirach says wisdom welcomes the learner like a wife of his youth; Wisdom's speaker likewise seeks her as a bride from youth. Sirach also adds the distinct mother image.

Sirach 15:3
She will feed him bread of understanding and give him water of wisdom to drink.
  Proverbs 9:5-6 — close wisdom's nourishing invitation: Sirach has wisdom feed the learner bread of understanding and water of wisdom; personified wisdom in Proverbs likewise offers bread and drink leading away from foolishness toward understanding and life, using wine rather than water.

Sirach 15:4
He will lean upon her and certainly not be shaken; he will hold to her and certainly not be put to shame.
  Proverbs 3:18 — close security through holding and leaning upon wisdom: Sirach says the person leaning on wisdom will not be shaken; Proverbs similarly calls her secure for those who hold and lean upon her.

Sirach 15:5
She will raise him above his neighbors and open his mouth in the midst of the assembly.
  Wisdom of Solomon 8:10-12 — close public honor and authoritative speech through wisdom: Sirach describes wisdom raising a person above neighbors and opening his mouth in the assembly; Wisdom's speaker likewise expects public honor and an attentive audience when he speaks through her.

Sirach 15:6
He will find gladness and a crown of rejoicing, and inherit an everlasting name.
  Proverbs 4:8-9 — close wisdom's crown of rejoicing: Sirach promises a crown of rejoicing through wisdom; Proverbs likewise says honoring her brings a crown of graces and delight. The everlasting name is an additional Sirach promise.

Sirach 15:7
People without understanding will certainly not gain her, and sinful men will certainly not see her.
  Wisdom of Solomon 1:4 — close exclusion of sinful people from wisdom: Sirach says sinful people will not see wisdom; Wisdom likewise says she will not enter an evil-devising soul or dwell in a body indebted to sin.

Sirach 15:8
She is far from pride, and lying men will certainly not remember her.
  Proverbs 11:2 — parallel opposition between pride and wisdom: Sirach says wisdom is far from pride; Proverbs likewise contrasts insolence and dishonor with the humble mouth meditating on wisdom. Sirach additionally excludes lying people.

Sirach 15:9
Praise is not fitting in a sinner’s mouth, because it was not sent from the Lord.
  Psalms 49:16-17 — related rejection of religious speech detached from obedience: Sirach finds praise unfitting in a sinner's mouth; God in the psalm likewise questions a sinner reciting His decrees and covenant while hating instruction.

Sirach 15:10
For praise will be spoken in wisdom, and the Lord will cause it to prosper.
  Psalms 46:7-8 — close praise offered with understanding: Sirach says praise will be spoken in wisdom; the psalm likewise commands music to God with understanding, without repeating Sirach's promise that He will prosper it.

Sirach 15:11
Do not say, “Because of the Lord I fell away,” for He will not do the things He hates.
  James 1:13 — close refusal to blame God for moral failure: Sirach forbids attributing falling away to the Lord, who does not do what He hates; James likewise forbids saying God tempts a person with evil.

Sirach 15:12
Do not say, “He Himself led me astray,” for He has no need of a sinful man.
  James 1:13-15 — close rejection of God as the source of sinful enticement: Sirach rejects the claim that God led a person astray; James likewise locates sinful temptation in one's own desire rather than God's action.

Sirach 15:13
The Lord has hated every abomination, and it is not loved by those who fear Him.
  Proverbs 8:13 — close fear of the Lord expressed in hatred of evil: Sirach joins the Lord's hatred of abominations with the attitude of those fearing Him; Proverbs likewise says fear of the Lord hates injustice, arrogance and wicked ways.

Sirach 15:14
He Himself made the human being from the beginning and left him in the hand of his own deliberation.
  Deuteronomy 30:19-20 — related human responsibility to choose a course before God: Sirach says the created human was left to his own deliberation; Deuteronomy likewise calls for choosing life through love and obedience. The link concerns moral choice, not an added comprehensive theory of freedom.

Sirach 15:15
If you are willing, you will keep the commandments; to act faithfully is a matter of good will.
  Deuteronomy 30:14-16 — close willingness and practicable obedience: Sirach connects keeping commandments with willingness and faithful intent; Deuteronomy likewise says the command is near enough to do and sets out life through obedience.

Sirach 15:16
He has placed fire and water before you; toward whichever you wish, you will stretch out your hand.
  Deuteronomy 30:15-19 — parallel alternatives placed before human choice: Sirach pictures fire and water set before the chooser; Deuteronomy similarly sets life and death before Israel and commands choosing life. The pairs are compared rather than declared identical images.

Sirach 15:17
Before human beings are life and death, and whichever a person chooses will be given to him.
  Deuteronomy 30:19 — close life-and-death choice formula: Both set life and death before the human recipient and connect choice with its outcome; Deuteronomy expressly commands choosing life.

Sirach 15:18
For the Lord’s wisdom is great; He is strong in dominion and sees all things.
  Psalms 146:5 — close greatness of divine strength and understanding: Sirach celebrates the Lord's great wisdom and strong dominion; the psalm likewise joins His greatness and strength with understanding beyond number. Sirach adds explicit universal sight.

Sirach 15:19
His eyes are upon those who fear Him, and He Himself will know every human deed.
  Psalms 32:13-18 — close divine sight of deeds and regard for those who fear Him: Sirach joins God's eyes upon those fearing Him with knowledge of every deed; the psalm likewise joins seeing humanity, understanding all deeds and eyes upon those who fear Him.

Sirach 15:20
He commanded no one to act ungodly, and He gave no one license to sin.
  Psalms 5:5 — parallel divine opposition to lawlessness: Sirach denies that God commands ungodliness or licenses sin; the psalm likewise says God does not desire lawlessness and will not receive an evildoer, without using the same command-and-license terms.

Sirach 16:1
Do not desire a multitude of useless children, or rejoice in ungodly sons.
  Wisdom of Solomon 4:3 — close useless multitude of ungodly offspring: Sirach warns against desiring many useless, ungodly children; Wisdom likewise calls the prolific multitude of the ungodly useless and without a secure root.

Sirach 16:2
Even if they multiply, do not rejoice in them unless the fear of the Lord is with them.
  Wisdom of Solomon 4:1-3 — parallel virtue valued above mere fertility: Sirach makes fear of the Lord the condition for rejoicing over many children; Wisdom similarly prefers virtuous childlessness to the ungodly's prolific but useless multitude.

Sirach 16:3
Do not trust in their life or rely on their multitude. For one is better than a thousand, and it is better to die childless than to have ungodly children.
  Wisdom of Solomon 4:1-3 — close preference for virtuous childlessness over ungodly descendants: Sirach explicitly prefers dying childless to having ungodly children; Wisdom likewise calls childlessness with virtue better and denies the value of ungodly proliferation.

Sirach 16:4
For through one understanding person a city will be settled, but a tribe of lawless people will become desolate.
  Ecclesiastes 9:15-18 — close one wise person's benefit to a city: Sirach says one understanding person can settle a city while lawless people bring desolation; Ecclesiastes likewise pictures one poor wise man delivering a city and warns that one sinner destroys much good.

Sirach 16:6
In an assembly of sinners fire will be kindled, and in a disobedient nation wrath was kindled.
  Numbers 16:35 — narrative example of fire in a rebellious assembly: Sirach recalls fire among sinners and wrath in a disobedient nation; Numbers supplies the Lord's fire consuming the assembled incense-offerers within the rebellion narrative.

Sirach 16:7
He did not make atonement for the ancient giants, who rebelled in their strength.
  Wisdom of Solomon 14:6 — close judgment of the ancient arrogant giants: Sirach recalls ancient giants rebelling in strength without receiving atonement; Wisdom likewise recalls arrogant giants perishing at the beginning while the world's hope survives on a raft.

Sirach 16:8
He did not spare Lot’s neighbors, whom He loathed because of their pride.
  Ezekiel 16:49-50 — close Sodom judged for arrogance: Sirach identifies Lot's neighbors as judged for pride; Ezekiel likewise names Sodom's arrogance, boasting and lawlessness before reporting her removal. The separately read Genesis 19 establishes the Lot setting.

Sirach 16:10
and likewise on the six hundred thousand foot soldiers gathered together in their hardness of heart.
  Numbers 14:27-30 — wilderness-generation judgment allusion: Sirach recalls the hardened six hundred thousand foot soldiers; Numbers announces death in the wilderness for the counted generation that grumbled. Exodus 12:37 supplies the separately read six-hundred-thousand departure count.

Sirach 16:11
Even if there is one stiff-necked person, it is a wonder if he will be acquitted. For mercy and wrath are with Him; He is mighty in atonements and pours out wrath.
  Exodus 34:6-7 — close mercy joined with refusal to acquit wrongdoing: Sirach pairs great atoning mercy with poured-out wrath and doubt that the stiff-necked will be acquitted; Exodus likewise joins abundant mercy and forgiveness with refusal to cleanse the guilty.

Sirach 16:12
As His mercy is great, so also His rebuke is great; He will judge a man according to his deeds.
  Romans 2:6-8 — close divine judgment according to deeds: Sirach says God will judge a person according to deeds amid mercy and rebuke; Romans likewise describes repayment according to works with differing outcomes for perseverance and unrighteousness.

Sirach 16:13
A sinner will not escape with his plunder, and a godly person’s endurance will certainly not be left behind.
  Habakkuk 2:6-8 — parallel judgment preventing a plunderer's escape: Sirach says a sinner will not escape with his plunder; Habakkuk likewise announces that one who plundered nations will become their plunder. Sirach's promise about godly endurance is additional.

Sirach 16:14
He will make room for every act of almsgiving; each person will receive according to his deeds.
  Matthew 6:3-4 — close divine recognition and repayment of almsgiving: Sirach says every almsgiving receives room and each person receives according to deeds; Jesus likewise promises that the Father sees secret almsgiving and repays it.

Sirach 16:17
Do not say, “I will be hidden from the Lord; who will remember me from on high? Among a numerous people I will certainly not be known, for what is my soul in the immeasurable creation?”
  Jeremiah 23:23-24 — close denial of hiding from the God who fills creation: Sirach rejects the excuse that one small life is hidden within immeasurable creation; Jeremiah likewise asks whether anyone can hide from the Lord who fills heaven and earth.

Sirach 16:18
Behold, heaven and the heaven of heaven, the abyss and the earth will be shaken at His visitation.
  Psalms 17:8-10 — parallel cosmic shaking at divine visitation: Sirach describes heaven, abyss and earth shaken at God's visitation; the psalm likewise describes earth and mountain foundations shaking as God descends in anger.

Sirach 16:19
Together the mountains and the foundations of the earth are shaken with trembling when He looks upon them.
  Psalms 17:8 — close trembling of foundations and mountains: Sirach makes mountains and earth's foundations tremble at God's look; the psalm likewise joins trembling earth with shaken mountain foundations under divine anger.

Sirach 16:20
Yet no heart will consider these things, and who will ponder His ways?
  Psalms 91:6-7 — related failure to understand divine works: Sirach's questions about hearts considering God's ways culminate in the verdict of folly at 16:23; the psalm likewise contrasts God's deep works and reasonings with the senseless person's failure to understand.

Sirach 16:22
Who will announce works of righteousness? Who will wait? For the covenant is far away.
  2 Peter 3:3-4 — related skeptical questioning of a delayed divine promise: Sirach's questions about waiting and the distant covenant are followed by a judgment of foolish reasoning; Peter likewise reports scoffers questioning the promise of the Lord's coming. The specific promises and settings differ.

Sirach 16:23
Someone deficient in heart thinks these things; a foolish and wandering man thinks foolish things.
  Psalms 91:6-7 — close foolish failure to understand God's works: Sirach identifies the preceding reasoning as that of someone deficient in heart; the psalm likewise calls the person unable to understand God's deep works senseless and without understanding.

Sirach 16:24
Listen to me, child, and learn knowledge; attend to my words with your heart.
  Proverbs 4:20-22 — close teacher's appeal to a child's attentive heart: Sirach asks the child to listen, learn and attend with the heart; Proverbs similarly asks the son to attend to words, apply the ear and keep them in the heart.

Sirach 16:26
By the Lord’s judgment His works have existed from the beginning; from their making He distinguished their portions.
  Genesis 1:14-18 — creation example of assigned functions from the beginning: Sirach says God distinguished His works' portions from their making; Genesis illustrates that ordering by assigning the lights their functions in day, night and seasons.

Sirach 16:27
He ordered His works for ever and their dominions through their generations. They neither hungered nor grew weary, and they did not cease from their tasks.
  Psalms 148:5-6 — close enduring establishment of created works: Sirach says God ordered His works and their dominions forever; the psalm likewise joins creation by command with their establishment for the age and a command that does not pass away. The absence of hunger and weariness is Sirach's additional description.

Sirach 16:28
None crowded out its neighbor, and for ever they will not disobey His word.
  Psalms 148:7-8 — parallel creation obeying divine command: Sirach says the works will not disobey God's word; the psalm likewise depicts earthly elements and storm wind fulfilling His word, without describing their relation to neighboring works.

Sirach 16:29
After these things the Lord looked upon the earth and filled it with His good things.
  Psalms 103:24-28 — close earth filled with God's provision: Sirach says the Lord filled earth with good things; the psalm likewise joins earth filled with His possession and living creatures filled with goodness when His hand opens.

Sirach 16:30
He covered its face with the life of every living creature, and to it they return.
  Psalms 103:29-30 — close creaturely life and return to dust: Sirach joins earth's living creatures with their return to it; the psalm likewise describes creatures returning to dust when breath is taken and renewed creation through God's Spirit.

Sirach 17:1
The Lord created a human being from earth and returned him to it again.
  Genesis 3:19 — close earth-origin and earth-return formula: Sirach says the human was created from earth and returned to it; Genesis likewise declares the human earth, taken from earth and returning to earth.

Sirach 17:2
He gave them numbered days and an appointed time, and He gave them authority over the things upon it.
  Job 14:5 — close numbered lifespan and appointed limit: Sirach gives humanity numbered days and an appointed time; Job likewise speaks of months numbered with God and a boundary that cannot be exceeded. The authority over earthly things is not repeated there.

Sirach 17:3
He clothed them with strength like His own and made them according to His image.
  Genesis 1:26-27 — close creation in God's image: Sirach says God made humans according to His image; Genesis likewise explicitly narrates humanity's making in God's image and likeness.

Sirach 17:4
He placed the fear of the human being upon all flesh and granted dominion over beasts and winged creatures.
  Genesis 9:2 — close animals' fear and humanity's dominion: Sirach says fear of human beings was placed upon flesh with dominion over beasts and birds; Genesis likewise places human dread upon beasts, birds and other creatures given into human hands.

Sirach 17:6
He gave them deliberation, a tongue and eyes, ears and a heart for understanding.
  Proverbs 20:12 — close divine gift of hearing and sight: Sirach attributes eyes and ears, among other capacities, to God; Proverbs likewise calls the hearing ear and seeing eye the Lord's works, without listing all Sirach's other faculties.

Sirach 17:7
He filled them with discerning knowledge and showed them good things and evil things.
  Deuteronomy 30:15-16 — related divine presentation of good and evil: Sirach says God supplied discerning knowledge and showed good and evil; Deuteronomy likewise places life, death, good and evil before Israel with instruction for obedience, within a particular covenant address.

Sirach 17:10
and they will praise the holy name, so that they may recount the greatness of His works.
  Psalms 104:1-3 — close holy-name praise and recounting divine works: Sirach joins praising the holy name with recounting God's great works; the psalm likewise joins calling on His name, announcing His works and recounting His wonders.

Sirach 17:11
He added knowledge to them and gave them the law of life as an inheritance.
  Deuteronomy 33:4 — close law given as an inheritance: Sirach calls the law of life an inheritance; Deuteronomy likewise calls Moses's commanded law an inheritance for Jacob's assemblies.

Sirach 17:12
He established an everlasting covenant with them and showed them His judgments.
  Psalms 104:8-10 — close everlasting covenant established for Israel: Sirach recalls an everlasting covenant and revealed judgments; the psalm likewise says God remembered His covenant forever and established it for Israel as an everlasting covenant.

Sirach 17:13
Their eyes saw the greatness of glory, and their ear heard the glory of His voice.
  Deuteronomy 5:22-24 — close glory seen and divine voice heard: Sirach describes the people's eyes seeing great glory and their ears hearing God's voice; Deuteronomy recounts the assembly hearing the voice from fire and affirming that God showed His glory.

Sirach 17:14
He said to them, “Beware of every injustice,” and commanded each of them concerning the neighbor.
  Leviticus 19:13-18 — close prohibition of injustice toward one's neighbor: Sirach says God warned against injustice and commanded concerning neighbors; Leviticus provides explicit commands against wronging, robbing, unjustly judging, hating and taking vengeance against a neighbor.

Sirach 17:15
Their ways are continually before Him; they will not be hidden from His eyes.
  Proverbs 5:21 — close human ways continually before God's eyes: Both say human ways lie before God's eyes; Proverbs adds that He watches every track, corresponding to Sirach's denial that those ways are hidden.

Sirach 17:17
He appointed a ruler for each nation, but Israel is the Lord’s portion.
  Deuteronomy 32:8-9 — close division of nations and Israel as the Lord's portion: Sirach contrasts appointed national rulers with Israel as God's own portion; Deuteronomy likewise describes national bounds according to God's angels and Jacob as the Lord's portion.

Sirach 17:19
All their works are like the sun before Him, and His eyes are continually upon their ways.
  Psalms 32:13-15 — parallel complete divine observation of human deeds: Sirach makes human works visible before God and His eyes constant upon their ways; the psalm likewise says He sees earth's inhabitants and understands all their deeds, without the sun comparison.

Sirach 17:20
Their injustices were not hidden from Him, and all their sins are before the Lord.
  Jeremiah 16:17 — close sins not hidden from God's eyes: Sirach says human injustices and sins are unhidden before the Lord; Jeremiah likewise explicitly denies that their ways or injustices are concealed from His eyes.

Sirach 17:22
A man’s almsgiving is like a seal with Him, and He will guard human kindness like the pupil of an eye.
  Acts 10:4 — related divine remembrance of almsgiving: Sirach compares almsgiving with a seal preserved before God; the angel likewise says Cornelius's alms have ascended as a memorial before Him, without repeating the pupil-of-the-eye comparison.

Sirach 17:23
After these things He will rise and repay them; He will return their recompense upon their head.
  Obadiah 1:15 — close recompense returning upon the doer's head: Sirach says God will return recompense upon people's heads; Obadiah likewise announces action repaid upon the doer's own head in the day of the Lord.

Sirach 17:24
Yet He gave a way back to those who repent and encouraged those whose endurance was failing.
  Wisdom of Solomon 12:19-20 — parallel divine opportunity for repentance: Sirach says God gives repentant people a way back and renewed courage; Wisdom likewise finds hope in God's gift of repentance and His provision of time and room to leave evil.

Sirach 17:25
Return to the Lord and leave sins behind; petition Him face to face and lessen your stumbling.
  Isaiah 55:6-7 — close return to God with abandoned sins and petition: Sirach commands return to the Lord, leaving sins and petitioning Him; Isaiah likewise joins seeking and calling upon God with abandoning wicked ways and returning for mercy.

Sirach 17:26
Come back to the Most High, turn away from injustice, and hate abomination intensely.
  Ezekiel 18:30-31 — close repentance as turning away from injustice: Sirach commands return to the Most High and rejection of injustice and abomination; Ezekiel likewise commands turning from impieties and casting them away in a renewed heart and spirit.

Sirach 17:27
Who in Hades will praise the Most High in place of the living who give acknowledgment?
  Psalms 6:6 — close question about thanksgiving in Hades: Both ask who in Hades will give God thanks or praise, contrasting death's silence with the living person's appeal to God.

Sirach 17:28
From the dead, as from one who does not exist, thanksgiving perishes; the living and healthy will praise the Lord.
  Isaiah 38:18-19 — close living praise contrasted with the dead: Sirach says thanksgiving perishes from the dead while the living praise; Isaiah likewise denies praise in Hades and assigns blessing to the living.

Sirach 17:29
How great is the Lord’s mercy and His atonement for those who return to Him!
  Isaiah 55:7 — close abundant mercy for those returning to God: Sirach celebrates mercy and atonement for those who return; Isaiah likewise promises mercy and abundant forgiveness to the ungodly who abandon their ways and return to the Lord.

Sirach 17:30
For human beings cannot have everything, because a son of man is not immortal.
  Genesis 3:22-24 — related limitation of mortal access to unending life: Sirach says humanity cannot possess everything because it is not immortal; Genesis narrates exclusion from the tree that would enable living forever, without restating Sirach's whole claim about having everything.

Sirach 17:31
What is brighter than the sun? Yet even it fails; and flesh and blood will conceive evil.
  Job 25:4-6 — related celestial comparison exposing human frailty: Sirach contrasts even the bright sun's failure with flesh and blood conceiving evil; Bildad similarly contrasts the moon and stars' limitations with mortal impurity. The speakers and celestial objects are distinguished rather than equated.

Sirach 17:32
He Himself surveys the power of the height of heaven, but all human beings are earth and ashes.
  Genesis 18:27 — close earth-and-ashes description of humanity before God: Sirach contrasts God's heavenly survey with humans as earth and ashes; Abraham uses the same earth-and-ashes description while addressing the Lord, without the heavenly-height clause.

Sirach 18:1
He who lives for ever created all things together.
  Wisdom of Solomon 9:1 — Divine creation of all things: Sirach attributes the creation of all things to the one who lives for ever; Wisdom's prayer likewise addresses God as the maker of all things, expressly adding creation by His word.

Sirach 18:2
The Lord alone will be justified.
  Romans 3:4 — Divine vindication: Sirach declares that the Lord alone will be justified; Romans contrasts God's truth with human falsehood and quotes the declaration that God is justified in His words.

Sirach 18:4
He has empowered no one to declare His works fully; who will trace out His mighty deeds?
  Job 9:10 — Unsearchable divine works: Sirach denies human ability to declare or trace God's mighty works fully; Job calls His great works unsearchable and beyond numbering.

Sirach 18:5
Who will count the might of His greatness? Who will go on to recount His mercies?
  Psalms 144:3-8 — Divine greatness and mercy beyond measure: Sirach asks who can count God's greatness and recount His mercies; the psalm describes His greatness as without limit and His mercy as abundant while recounting His mighty acts.

Sirach 18:6
It is impossible to diminish or to add, and impossible to trace out the Lord’s wonders.
  Ecclesiastes 3:14 — Nothing added to or removed from God's work: Both passages deny the possibility of adding to or diminishing what God has done. Ecclesiastes additionally states that His work endures for ever.

Sirach 18:8
What is a human being, and what is his use? What is his good, and what is his evil?
  Psalms 8:5 — Question about the human being's significance: Sirach asks what a human being is and what use or good belongs to him; the psalm asks what a human being is that God remembers and visits him.

Sirach 18:9
The number of a person’s days, in many years, is a hundred.
  Psalms 89:10 — Contrasting estimates of a bounded human lifespan: Sirach describes a hundred years as many human years; the psalm speaks of seventy or, with strength, eighty. The numbers differ, but both reckon mortal life in a limited span of years.

Sirach 18:10
Like a drop of water from the sea or a grain of sand, so are a few years in a day of eternity.
  Psalms 89:4 — Human years set beside eternity: Sirach reduces human years to a drop or grain beside a day of eternity; the psalm compares a thousand years in God's eyes to a past day or night watch.

Sirach 18:11
Therefore the Lord was patient with them and poured His mercy upon them.
  Psalms 102:13-17 — Mercy in view of human frailty: Sirach's description of short human life leads to the Lord's patience and mercy; the psalm joins His compassion to knowledge that people are dust and pass like grass.

Sirach 18:12
He saw and knew that their end was grievous; therefore He multiplied His atonement.
  Psalms 77:38-39 — Atonement and remembrance of mortality: Sirach connects God's knowledge of the grievous human end with increased atonement; the psalm joins His compassionate atonement to remembrance that people are passing flesh.

Sirach 18:13
A person’s mercy is toward his neighbor, but the Lord’s mercy is upon all flesh: rebuking, disciplining, teaching, and bringing back like a shepherd his flock.
  Psalms 144:9 — Mercy extending beyond one's neighbor: Sirach contrasts limited human mercy with the Lord's mercy upon all flesh; the psalm likewise declares Him kind to all, with compassion over all His works. The target concerns this broad scope.

Sirach 18:14
He has mercy on those who receive discipline and those who hasten eagerly toward His judgments.
  Proverbs 3:11-12 — Divine favor and receptive discipline: Sirach associates mercy with receiving discipline; Proverbs urges the child not to reject the Lord's correction, because He disciplines the one He loves.

Sirach 18:15
Child, do not add reproach to good deeds, or the grief of words to any gift.
  James 1:5 — Giving without reproach: Sirach forbids adding reproach or hurtful words to a gift; James portrays God's generous giving as without reproach, supplying a positive example of that manner of giving.

Sirach 18:16
Will not dew bring relief from scorching heat? So a word is better than a gift.
  Proverbs 16:24 — Beneficial words pictured through bodily relief: Sirach compares a good word's benefit to dew relieving heat; Proverbs compares good words to honeycombs whose sweetness heals life. Both give speech a restorative sensory image.

Sirach 18:17
Behold, is not a word beyond a good gift? Yet both are found with a gracious man.
  Sirach 18:15-16 — Same-chapter union of gracious speech and giving: The earlier warning forbids hurtful words with gifts and praises a word's value; this verse draws the two together by locating both the good word and good gift in a gracious person.

Sirach 18:18
A fool will reproach gracelessly, and a grudging person’s gift makes eyes waste away.
  Proverbs 23:6-8 — The harm of begrudging generosity: Sirach depicts the distress caused by a grudging gift and graceless reproach; Proverbs warns against a begrudging host and describes the resulting ruin of good words.

Sirach 18:19
Learn before speaking, and attend to your health before illness.
  Proverbs 18:13 — Preparation before speaking: Sirach requires learning before speech; Proverbs condemns answering a matter before hearing it as foolishness and reproach. This supports the speech clause, not the separate medical advice.

Sirach 18:20
Examine yourself before judgment, and at the hour of visitation you will find atonement.
  1 Corinthians 11:28-32 — Self-examination before judgment: Sirach urges self-examination before visitation and judgment; Paul requires self-examination and says that discerning ourselves would avert judgment, within his specific teaching about the Lord's meal.

Sirach 18:21
Humble yourself before you fall ill, and show repentance at the time of sins.
  Joel 2:12-13 — Repentance and inward humbling: Sirach calls for humility and repentance while sin can be addressed; Joel calls for a whole-hearted return to God and the tearing of hearts. Neither link requires treating illness as a particular sin's result.

Sirach 18:22
Do not be held back from repaying a vow at the right time, and do not wait until death to be justified.
  Ecclesiastes 5:3-4 — Prompt fulfillment of a vow: Both warn against delaying payment of a vow. Ecclesiastes explicitly says it is better not to vow than to vow and fail to pay.

Sirach 18:23
Prepare yourself before making a vow, and do not become like a person testing the Lord.
  Proverbs 20:25 — Deliberation before a vow: Sirach requires preparation before making a vow; Proverbs calls quick consecration a trap when reconsideration comes only after the vow.

Sirach 18:24
Remember wrath in the days of death, and the time of retribution when the face is turned away.
  Romans 2:5-6 — Remembering wrath and retribution: Sirach directs attention to the coming time of wrath and retribution; Romans warns of stored-up wrath on the day of righteous judgment and repayment according to deeds.

Sirach 18:25
Remember a time of famine in a time of fullness, poverty and need in days of wealth.
  Genesis 41:34-36 — Preparing for famine during abundance: Sirach tells the prosperous to remember famine and need; Joseph's plan to reserve food during abundant years for coming famine gives a concrete example of such foresight.

Sirach 18:26
From morning until evening the time changes, and all things are swift before the Lord.
  Proverbs 27:1 — Uncertainty of changing days: Sirach emphasizes how quickly circumstances change between morning and evening; Proverbs forbids boasting about tomorrow because the coming day's outcome is unknown.

Sirach 18:27
A wise person will be cautious in everything, and in days of sins will guard against wrongdoing.
  Proverbs 14:16 — Wise caution turning from evil: Sirach's wise person guards against wrongdoing; Proverbs contrasts the wise person who fears and turns from evil with the self-confident fool who mixes with a lawless person.

Sirach 18:29
Those understanding in words have themselves become wise and rained down precise proverbs.
  Ecclesiastes 12:9-10 — Wisdom expressed in carefully formed sayings: Sirach associates understanding speech with producing precise proverbs; Ecclesiastes connects wisdom and teaching with an orderly arrangement of proverbs and the search for truthful, upright words.

Sirach 18:30
Do not walk after your desires, and restrain your appetites.
  Galatians 5:16-17 — Resisting the rule of desire: Sirach forbids following one's desires and commands restraint of appetites; Galatians similarly warns against fulfilling fleshly desire and contrasts it with walking by the Spirit.

Sirach 18:31
If you supply your soul with the satisfaction of desire, it will make you an object of your enemies’ gloating.
  Romans 6:12 — The danger of yielding to desire: Sirach warns of the humiliating outcome of satisfying desire; Romans forbids letting sin reign through obedience to bodily desires. The shared issue is surrender to desire, not an identical stated penalty.

Sirach 18:32
Do not rejoice in great luxury, lest you be bound to contribute to it.
  Proverbs 23:20 — Costly participation in feasting: Sirach warns that rejoicing in luxury can bind a person to contribute to it; Proverbs likewise warns against stretching oneself over contributions and purchases of meat.

Sirach 18:33
Do not become poor by feasting on borrowed money when you have nothing in your purse.
  Proverbs 23:20-21 — Feasting and impoverishment: Sirach warns against impoverishing oneself through borrowed feasts; Proverbs joins a warning about meal contributions to the poverty of the drunkard. Borrowing is Sirach's additional circumstance.

Sirach 19:1
A drunken worker will not become rich; the one who despises small things will fall little by little.
  Proverbs 23:21 — Drunkenness and poverty: Sirach says a drunken worker will not become rich; Proverbs likewise predicts poverty for the drunkard. The link concerns the first clause, not the separate warning about despising small things.

Sirach 19:2
Wine and women will lead understanding men astray, and the one who clings to prostitutes will become more reckless.
  Proverbs 31:3-5 — Women, wine, and impaired judgment: Sirach warns that wine and women lead understanding men astray; the royal instruction warns against giving wealth to women and drinking so as to forget wisdom and fail in judgment.

Sirach 19:3
Rot and worms will inherit him, and a reckless soul will be taken away.
  Sirach 10:11 — Worms as a mortal inheritance: Both use inheritance language for the association between death and worms. Chapter 10 applies it to human death generally, while this verse concludes a warning about reckless conduct.

Sirach 19:4
The one who trusts quickly is lighthearted, and the one who sins will wrong his own soul.
  Proverbs 14:15 — Caution about ready belief: Sirach criticizes quick trust; Proverbs contrasts the person who believes every word with the resourceful person who reconsiders. This supports the warning about credulity.

Sirach 19:6
but the one who hates chatter diminishes evil.
  Proverbs 10:19 — Restrained speech limiting evil: Sirach says hatred of chatter diminishes evil; Proverbs says much talking does not escape sin and praises sparing one's lips.

Sirach 19:7
Never repeat a word, and you will certainly lose nothing.
  Proverbs 11:13 — Keeping matters confidential: Sirach warns against repeating a word; Proverbs contrasts disclosure by a double-tongued person with a faithful person's concealment of matters.

Sirach 19:8
Do not tell it to friend or enemy, and unless silence is a sin for you, do not reveal it.
  Proverbs 11:13 — Discretion about disclosure: Sirach forbids revealing a matter to friend or enemy unless silence would be sinful; Proverbs praises faithful concealment. Sirach expressly states an exception that the proverb does not discuss.

Sirach 19:9
For someone has heard you and kept watch on you, and in time he will hate you.
  Sirach 19:7-8 — Same-chapter consequence of indiscreet disclosure: The preceding commands forbid repeating and disclosing a word; this verse explains the danger through a hearer who keeps watch and later hates the speaker.

Sirach 19:10
Have you heard a word? Let it die with you. Take courage; it will certainly not make you burst.
  Proverbs 11:13 — Allowing a heard matter to remain concealed: Sirach's instruction to let a heard word die with oneself corresponds to the faithful person's concealment of matters in Proverbs.

Sirach 19:11
A fool will suffer labor pains over a word, like a woman giving birth over her baby.
  Sirach 19:12 — Same-chapter painful images for an unrestrained word: The word troubling a fool is compared first to labor pains and then to an arrow fixed in flesh. Both images portray the fool's inability to bear a word quietly.

Sirach 19:12
An arrow fixed in the flesh of a thigh—so is a word in a fool’s belly.
  Sirach 19:11 — Same-chapter bodily pain as a speech image: The arrow and belly image continues the preceding comparison of a fool's distress over a word to childbirth pains; the particular connection is the painful pressure of an uncontained word.

Sirach 19:13
Question a friend; perhaps he did not do it, and if he did, question him so that he may not repeat it.
  Matthew 18:15 — Personal correction seeking a restored relationship: Sirach urges questioning a friend to establish what happened and prevent repetition; Matthew directs private reproof of a sinning brother with the aim of gaining him. Sirach additionally warns that the deed may not have occurred.

Sirach 19:14
Question your neighbor; perhaps he did not say it, and if he has said it, question him so that he may not say it again.
  Sirach 19:13 — Same-chapter questioning of alleged conduct and speech: The two instructions apply the same cautious questioning first to an alleged deed and then to alleged speech, allowing innocence and seeking to prevent repetition if the report is true.

Sirach 19:15
Question a friend, for slander often occurs; do not trust every report.
  Proverbs 14:15 — Testing reports instead of believing everything: Sirach warns that slander is frequent and forbids trusting every report; Proverbs contrasts believing every word with thoughtful reconsideration.

Sirach 19:16
There is someone who slips without intending it in his soul; who has not sinned with his tongue?
  James 3:2 — The common danger of stumbling in speech: Sirach asks who has not sinned with the tongue and allows unintended slips; James says all stumble in many things and makes freedom from stumbling in speech a mark of completeness.

Sirach 19:17
Question your neighbor before threatening him, and give place to the law of the Most High.
  Leviticus 19:17-18 — Lawful correction instead of hostility: Sirach calls for questioning before threatening and giving place to God's law; Leviticus commands reproof of one's neighbor while forbidding hatred, vengeance, and lasting anger.

Sirach 19:20
All wisdom is fear of the Lord, and in all wisdom there is doing the law.
  Job 28:28 — Wisdom as reverence and obedient conduct: Sirach identifies wisdom with fear of the Lord and doing the law; Job identifies reverence for God with wisdom and abstention from evil with knowledge.

Sirach 19:22
Knowledge of wickedness is not wisdom, and where sinners take counsel there is no prudence.
  James 3:14-17 — Distinguishing wisdom from wicked cleverness: Sirach denies that knowledge of wickedness or sinful counsel is wisdom; James rejects jealous, self-seeking wisdom as earthly and demonic and contrasts it with pure, peaceable wisdom.

Sirach 19:23
There is shrewdness that is an abomination, and there is a foolish person deficient in wisdom.
  James 3:15-17 — A kind of cleverness unworthy of wisdom's name: Sirach allows for abhorrent shrewdness; James similarly distinguishes purported wisdom that is earthly and demonic from the pure wisdom from above.

Sirach 19:24
Better someone lacking understanding but full of fear than someone abounding in prudence who transgresses the law.
  Sirach 19:20 — Same-chapter priority of fear and obedience over cleverness: The preference for a less knowledgeable but God-fearing person over a lawbreaking expert follows the chapter's definition of wisdom as fear of the Lord and doing the law.

Sirach 19:25
There is precise shrewdness that is unjust, and there is someone who twists matters in order to bring a judgment to light.
  Sirach 19:22 — Same-chapter rejection of unjust shrewdness: The first clause's precise but unjust shrewdness illustrates the earlier refusal to call knowledge of wickedness wisdom. The link does not resolve the separate clause about twisting matters to bring judgment to light.

Sirach 19:26
There is someone who acts wickedly while bowed down in gloom, with his inward parts full of deceit.
  Proverbs 26:24-26 — Humble-looking behavior concealing deceit: Sirach describes gloomy, bowed-down conduct with deceit within; Proverbs describes an enemy's tears and agreeable lips while his heart devises deceit.

Sirach 19:27
Hiding his face and pretending to be deaf, where he has not been recognized he will get ahead of you.
  Proverbs 26:24-26 — Deceptive outward conduct: The concealed face and pretended deafness belong to Sirach's deceptive character portrait; Proverbs similarly warns that an enemy's outward entreaties can conceal inward evil.

Sirach 19:28
If lack of strength prevents him from sinning, he will do evil when he finds an opportunity.
  Sirach 20:21 — Circumstances preventing a sinful act: Lack of strength prevents sin here, while need prevents it in chapter 20. Sirach 19 adds an evil intention for a future opportunity; that intention is not imputed to the other passage.

Sirach 19:29
A man will be recognized from his appearance, and an understanding person from the look of his face.
  Ecclesiastes 8:1 — Wisdom visible in the face: Sirach associates recognizing understanding with the look of a person's face; Ecclesiastes says wisdom lights up the face and contrasts a shameless face.

Sirach 19:30
A man’s clothing, the grin of his teeth, and a person’s steps will declare what he is like.
  Sirach 19:29 — Same-chapter expansion of visible character signs: After appearance and facial expression, the description adds clothing, the grin of the teeth, and one's steps as outward signs by which a person's character is recognized.

Sirach 20:1
There is a rebuke that is not fitting, and there is a silent person who is prudent.
  Proverbs 17:27-28 — Prudent restraint in speech: Sirach contrasts an unfitting rebuke with prudent silence; Proverbs praises restraint of a harsh word and says someone who makes himself mute will seem intelligent.

Sirach 20:2
How much better to rebuke than to be angry,
  Leviticus 19:17-18 — Reproof preferred to sustained anger: Sirach prefers rebuke to anger; Leviticus requires reproof of the neighbor while forbidding hatred and remaining angry with one's people.

Sirach 20:3
and the one who acknowledges his fault will be kept from loss.
  1 John 1:9 — Acknowledgment of wrongdoing: Sirach associates acknowledging fault with preservation from loss; 1 John calls for confession of sins and promises forgiveness and cleansing. The outcomes are stated differently.

Sirach 20:5
There is a silent person who is found wise, and there is someone hated for much chatter.
