  Proverbs 17:28 — Silence and the appearance of wisdom: Both sayings associate silence with being regarded as wise or intelligent. Sirach adds the contrast of hatred caused by much chatter.

Sirach 20:6
There is someone silent because he has no answer, and someone silent because he knows the right time.
  Ecclesiastes 3:7 — Recognizing a time for silence: Sirach distinguishes inability to answer from knowing the right time to remain silent; Ecclesiastes explicitly gives both silence and speech their proper time.

Sirach 20:7
A wise person will keep silent until the right time, but a babbler and a fool will miss the right time.
  Ecclesiastes 3:7 — Speaking and keeping silent at the proper time: The wise person's waiting to speak corresponds to Ecclesiastes' recognition of a time to be silent and a time to speak; Sirach contrasts the fool who misses that time.

Sirach 20:8
The one who multiplies words will be loathed, and the one who assumes authority will be hated.
  Ecclesiastes 10:12-14 — The harmful multiplication of words: Sirach warns that multiplying words brings loathing; Ecclesiastes portrays the fool multiplying words while his own lips drown him. The connection concerns excess speech, not Sirach's separate authority clause.

Sirach 20:9
There is success for a man amid adversity, and there is a gain that brings loss.
  Ecclesiastes 5:12-13 — Gain that turns into harm: Sirach allows for a gain that brings loss; Ecclesiastes describes wealth kept to its owner's harm and then lost through a grievous distraction.

Sirach 20:11
There is loss for the sake of glory, and there is someone who has lifted his head from lowliness.
  James 4:10 — Elevation from lowliness: Sirach's second clause describes someone lifting his head from lowliness; James promises exaltation to those who humble themselves before the Lord. James supplies the explicitly religious setting.

Sirach 20:13
A wise person will make himself welcome by his words, but fools’ favors will be wasted.
  Ecclesiastes 10:12 — Wise words winning favor: Sirach says the wise person makes himself welcome through words; Ecclesiastes describes a wise person's words as gracious and contrasts the self-destructive lips of a fool.

Sirach 20:14
An unthinking person’s gift will not benefit you, for his eyes seek many things in exchange for one.
  Proverbs 23:6-8 — A gift or meal spoiled by the giver's disposition: Sirach rejects a gift whose giver seeks excessive return; Proverbs warns against dining with a begrudging person. Both expose apparent generosity that does not benefit the recipient.

Sirach 20:15
He will give little and reproach much, and open his mouth like a herald. Today he will lend, and tomorrow he will demand repayment; such a person is hateful.
  Sirach 20:14 — Same-chapter portrait of an ungenerous giver: The giver seeking many things for one gift is further portrayed as giving little, reproaching loudly, and demanding repayment almost immediately.

Sirach 20:16
A fool will say, “I have no friend, and there is no gratitude for my good deeds.”
  Sirach 20:14-15 — Same-chapter contrast between complaint and conduct: The fool complains of having no friend or gratitude after the passage has described gifts demanding excessive return and reproach. The connection places his complaint beside the specific conduct that precedes it.

Sirach 20:18
Better a slip on the ground than a slip of the tongue; so the fall of the wicked will come swiftly.
  James 3:2 — Stumbling with the tongue: Sirach considers a slip of the tongue worse than a slip on the ground; James similarly uses stumbling in speech as an image of human moral failure.

Sirach 20:19
A graceless person is an untimely tale; it will be continually in the mouth of the uninstructed.
  Sirach 20:20 — Same-chapter speech spoiled by poor timing: The untimely tale associated with a graceless person is followed by the rejected proverb a fool does not speak at its proper time. Both concern inappropriate verbal performance.

Sirach 20:20
A proverb will be rejected from a fool’s mouth, for he will certainly not say it at its proper time.
  Proverbs 26:7 — A proverb unfitted to a fool's mouth: Sirach explains rejection of a fool's proverb through his failure to speak at the proper time; Proverbs also separates a proverb from the mouth of fools without supplying that particular explanation.

Sirach 20:21
There is someone prevented from sinning by need; in his rest he will not be pierced with remorse.
  Sirach 19:28 — External circumstances restraining sin: Need prevents sin in this verse, as lack of strength does in chapter 19. Only the earlier passage explicitly adds an intention to do evil when opportunity comes.

Sirach 20:22
There is someone who destroys his soul through shame, and through regard for a foolish face he will lose it.
  Proverbs 29:24-25 — Fearful regard for people causing a fall: Sirach warns that shame and regard for a foolish person can destroy one's soul; Proverbs describes people tripped up by fear and shame before others, in the setting of failing to report wrongdoing.

Sirach 20:24
A lie is an evil blemish in a person; it will be continually in the mouth of the uninstructed.
  Proverbs 12:22 — Condemnation of lying speech: Sirach calls a lie an evil blemish that repeatedly appears in uninstructed speech; Proverbs calls lying lips an abomination to the Lord.

Sirach 20:25
A thief is preferable to someone who persists in lying, but both will inherit destruction.
  Zechariah 5:3-4 — Destruction overtaking theft and false speech: Sirach says both theft and persistent lying end in destruction; Zechariah's curse brings death and destruction upon thieves and false swearers. False swearing is the target's particular form of falsehood.

Sirach 20:26
A lying person’s character is dishonor, and his shame is continually with him.
  Proverbs 13:5 — Falsehood and shame: Sirach associates a liar's character with dishonor and continuing shame; Proverbs contrasts the righteous person's hatred of an unjust word with the impious person's shame and loss of frankness.

Sirach 20:27
A wise person will advance himself through his words, and a prudent man will please dignitaries.
  Proverbs 16:13 — Speech finding favor with rulers: Sirach connects wise speech with advancement and pleasing dignitaries; Proverbs says righteous lips are acceptable to a king and that he loves upright words.

Sirach 20:28
The one who works the land will raise his heap of grain, and the one who pleases dignitaries will atone for injustice.
  Proverbs 12:11 — Working the land and receiving food: Sirach's cultivator raises a heap of grain; Proverbs says the worker of his own land will be filled with loaves. The link concerns cultivation, not the separate clause about pleasing dignitaries.

Sirach 20:29
Presents and gifts blind the eyes of the wise and turn aside rebukes like a muzzle in the mouth.
  Exodus 23:8 — Gifts blinding judgment: Both explicitly say gifts blind the eyes and interfere with right speech or correction. Exodus prohibits receiving such gifts; Sirach illustrates their silencing effect with a muzzle.

Sirach 20:30
Hidden wisdom and an unseen treasure—what benefit is there in either?
  Sirach 41:14 — Repeated hidden-wisdom and unseen-treasure saying: The later passage repeats the question about the benefit of hidden wisdom and unseen treasure, preserving the paired images and the challenge about their usefulness.

Sirach 20:31
Better a person who hides his foolishness than a person who hides his wisdom.
  Sirach 41:15 — Repeated preference for hiding folly rather than wisdom: Both passages state the same comparison: the person concealing foolishness is better than the person concealing wisdom.

Sirach 21:1
Child, have you sinned? Do not add to it any more, and pray concerning your earlier sins.
  Acts 8:22 — Repentance and prayer over committed sin: Sirach calls for ceasing to add sins and praying concerning earlier ones; Peter likewise tells Simon to repent of his wickedness and plead with the Lord for forgiveness.

Sirach 21:2
Flee from sin as from the presence of a snake, for if you approach it, it will bite you. Its teeth are lions’ teeth, destroying human lives.
  1 Corinthians 6:18 — Fleeing sin rather than approaching it: Sirach commands flight from sin because it attacks and destroys; Paul commands flight from sexual immorality and identifies its harm to one's own body. Sexual immorality is the target's specific case.

Sirach 21:3
All lawlessness is like a two-edged sword; there is no healing for its wound.
  Proverbs 5:4-5 — Sin's deadly two-edged blade image: Sirach compares lawlessness to an incurably wounding two-edged sword; Proverbs uses a two-edged blade for the bitter outcome of involvement with a prostitute and describes the path to death and Hades.

Sirach 21:4
Intimidation and insolence will lay wealth waste; so the house of a proud person will be laid waste.
  Proverbs 15:25 — The destruction of the proud person's house: Sirach predicts the wasting of the proud person's house; Proverbs explicitly says the Lord pulls down the houses of the insolent.

Sirach 21:5
A poor person’s petition goes from his mouth to His ears, and His judgment comes swiftly.
  Psalms 33:7 — The poor person's cry heard by the Lord: Sirach describes the poor petitioner's words reaching God's ears; the psalm recounts a poor man's cry being heard and his rescue from afflictions.

Sirach 21:6
The one who hates rebuke is in a sinner’s tracks, but the one who fears the Lord will turn back in heart.
  Proverbs 12:1 — Hatred of correction: Sirach puts hatred of rebuke on the sinner's path and contrasts a receptive fear of God; Proverbs contrasts love of discipline with the foolishness of hating rebukes.

Sirach 21:8
The one who builds his house with others’ money is like someone gathering its stones for a storm.
  Habakkuk 2:6-11 — A house endangered by another's resources: Sirach likens building with others' money to collecting stones for a storm; Habakkuk condemns accumulating what is not one's own and acquiring evil gain for a house that will suffer shame.

Sirach 21:9
An assembly of the lawless is gathered tow, and their end is a flame of fire.
  Isaiah 1:31 — The lawless as tow consumed by fire: Both describe lawless people through the image of tow ending in fire. Isaiah adds sparks from their works and the absence of anyone to quench the burning.

Sirach 21:10
The sinners’ road is smoothed with stones, but at its end is the pit of Hades.
  Proverbs 14:12 — An attractive road ending in Hades: Sirach's smoothly paved sinners' road ends in the pit of Hades; Proverbs warns of a way that seems upright but ends at the bottom of Hades.

Sirach 21:11
The one who keeps the law masters his thoughts, and wisdom is the fulfillment of the fear of the Lord.
  Sirach 19:20 — Wisdom, fear of God, and the law: Sirach 19 identifies wisdom with fear of the Lord and doing the law; this verse again joins keeping the law with wisdom as the fulfillment of that fear.

Sirach 21:12
The one who is not shrewd will not be instructed, yet there is a shrewdness that multiplies bitterness.
  Sirach 19:23 — Shrewdness with a harmful character: The warning that some shrewdness multiplies bitterness corresponds to the earlier statement that there is shrewdness which is an abomination. Both refuse to treat cleverness alone as sufficient wisdom.

Sirach 21:13
A wise person’s knowledge will increase like a flood, and his counsel is like a spring of life.
  Proverbs 13:14 — Wise instruction as a spring of life: Sirach compares the wise person's counsel to a spring of life; Proverbs calls the wise person's law a fountain of life. The life-giving water image is shared.

Sirach 21:14
A fool’s inward parts are like a broken vessel; he will retain no knowledge.
  Sirach 22:9 — Broken pottery and the failure to instruct a fool: Sirach depicts the fool's inability to retain knowledge as a broken vessel; the next chapter compares teaching a fool to gluing a potsherd together.

Sirach 21:15
If a knowledgeable person hears a wise word, he will praise it and add to it. A self-indulgent person heard it, and it displeased him; he cast it behind his back.
  Proverbs 9:8-9 — The wise learner receives and increases instruction: Sirach's knowledgeable hearer praises and adds to a wise word; Proverbs says the wise person responds to reproof with love and becomes wiser when given a starting point. Both contrast receptive wisdom with resistance.

Sirach 21:16
A fool’s explanation is like a burden on a journey, but grace will be found on an understanding person’s lip.
  Ecclesiastes 10:12 — Grace in an understanding person's speech: Sirach contrasts the burden of a fool's explanation with grace on an understanding lip; Ecclesiastes likewise calls wise words gracious and contrasts the fool's harmful lips.

Sirach 21:17
The mouth of a prudent person will be sought in the assembly, and they will consider his words in their heart.
  Job 29:21-23 — Attentive reception of wise counsel: Sirach describes an assembly seeking and considering the prudent person's words; Job remembers hearers attending silently to his counsel and awaiting his speech like thirsty earth awaiting rain.

Sirach 21:19
Instruction is fetters on the feet of the senseless, and like handcuffs on his right hand.
  Sirach 6:24-30 — Contrasting responses to instruction's bonds: The senseless experience instruction as fetters and handcuffs; chapter 6 tells the learner to accept wisdom's fetters and promises that her bonds will become shelter, glory, and gladness.

Sirach 21:20
A fool raises his voice in laughter, but a shrewd man will barely smile quietly.
  Ecclesiastes 7:6 — The sound of foolish laughter: Sirach contrasts a fool's loud laughter with a prudent person's quiet smile; Ecclesiastes likens foolish laughter to crackling thorns beneath a cauldron and calls it futility.

Sirach 21:21
Instruction is like a golden ornament to the prudent, and like a bracelet on his right arm.
  Sirach 6:29-31 — Instruction worn as a precious ornament: Instruction is a golden ornament and bracelet for the prudent here; chapter 6 likewise describes wisdom with a golden ornament, glorious clothing, and a crown of rejoicing.

Sirach 21:22
A fool’s foot is quick to enter a house, but an experienced person will feel shame before it.
  Proverbs 25:17 — Restraint when entering another's space: Sirach contrasts the fool's hurried entrance into a house with an experienced person's reserve; Proverbs advises bringing one's foot rarely to a friend lest the friend become filled with one's presence and respond with hatred.

Sirach 21:23
A foolish person peers into a house from its door, but an instructed man will stand outside.
  Sirach 21:22 — Same-chapter restraint at another person's house: The contrast between hasty entry and experienced reserve is sharpened into the contrast between peering through the door and respectfully standing outside.

Sirach 21:24
It is a person’s lack of instruction to listen at a door, but the prudent will feel weighed down by the dishonor.
  Sirach 21:23 — Same-chapter respect for a household's boundary: Peering into a house and listening at its door are successive examples of uninstructed intrusion; the instructed person stands outside and recognizes the dishonor of such conduct.

Sirach 21:25
Strangers’ lips will talk about these things, but the words of the prudent will be weighed in a balance.
  Proverbs 16:23 — Deliberate assessment of one's words: Sirach depicts prudent words as weighed in a balance; Proverbs describes the wise heart understanding what comes from its own mouth and carrying discernment on its lips.

Sirach 21:28
The whisperer stains his own soul and will be hated where he dwells.
  Sirach 28:13-16 — The whisperer's destructive speech: Sirach condemns the whisperer's self-staining conduct and local hatred; chapter 28 names whispering and double speech as destroyers of peaceful people and causes of unrest.

Sirach 22:1
A lazy person has been compared to a filthy stone, and everyone will hiss at his dishonor.
  Sirach 22:2 — Same-chapter repulsive images of laziness: The comparison of the lazy person to a filthy stone is followed by a comparison to dung. The two images portray others' disgust without requiring a claim that the objects are identical.

Sirach 22:2
A lazy person has been compared to a lump of dung; everyone who picks it up will shake his hand.
  Sirach 22:1 — Same-chapter disgust at laziness: The dung image and the hand shaken after contact continue the preceding filthy-stone image and the public dishonor of the lazy person.

Sirach 22:3
A father’s shame is in begetting an uninstructed child, and a daughter brings about loss.
  Proverbs 17:21 — Parental sorrow over an uninstructed child: Sirach calls begetting an uninstructed child a father's shame; Proverbs says a father does not rejoice over an uneducated son. The connection concerns that clause, not the separate statement about a daughter.

Sirach 22:4
A prudent daughter will inherit her husband, but one who brings shame brings grief to her parent.
  Proverbs 10:1 — A child's conduct affecting the parent: Sirach contrasts the prudent daughter with one who brings parental grief; Proverbs similarly contrasts a wise son who gladdens his father with a foolish son who grieves his mother. The target does not explain inheriting a husband.

Sirach 22:5
A shameless woman shames both father and husband and will be dishonored by both.
  Proverbs 12:4 — A wife's harmful conduct within marriage: Sirach describes a shameless woman's dishonor to father and husband; Proverbs contrasts a courageous wife with an evil-doing woman who destroys a man like a worm in wood.

Sirach 22:6
An untimely discourse is music in mourning, but lashes and discipline are wisdom at every time.
  Ecclesiastes 3:4 — Recognizing the proper occasion for mourning and joy: Sirach compares untimely discourse to music during mourning; Ecclesiastes distinguishes times for weeping and laughing, mourning and dancing. This concerns appropriate occasions, not Sirach's separate commendation of discipline.

Sirach 22:9
The one teaching a fool is gluing a potsherd together, waking a sleeper from deep sleep.
  Sirach 21:14 — The fool and broken pottery as a learning image: Teaching the fool is compared to repairing a potsherd, corresponding to the earlier description of the fool's inward parts as a broken vessel that cannot retain knowledge.

Sirach 22:10
The one speaking to a fool is speaking to someone drowsy; at the end he will say, “What is it?”
  Proverbs 23:9 — Speech wasted on a foolish hearer: Sirach's drowsy fool fails to take in what has been said; Proverbs forbids speaking into a fool's ears because he will sneer at intelligent words. The failures differ, but both concern an unreceptive hearer.

Sirach 22:11
Weep over a dead person, for he has lost the light; weep also over a fool, for he has lost understanding. Weep more gently over the dead, because he has found rest, but the fool’s life is worse than death.
  Sirach 38:23 — The dead person's rest tempering grief: Sirach gives the dead person's rest as a reason for gentler weeping; chapter 38 similarly calls for comfort and the resting of remembrance when the dead person rests. This addresses the consolation clause, not the whole comparison with a fool.

Sirach 22:12
Mourning for the dead lasts seven days; for a fool and an ungodly person, all the days of his life.
  Genesis 50:10 — Seven days of mourning: Sirach names seven days as the period of mourning for the dead; Genesis records Joseph mourning his father for seven days. The narrative illustrates that duration, not the lifelong grief over a fool.

Sirach 22:13
Do not multiply words with a fool or go to someone without understanding. Guard against him so that you do not have trouble, and you will certainly not be stained by what he shakes off. Turn away from him, and you will find rest and will certainly not grow weary of his madness.
  Proverbs 23:9 — Avoiding fruitless speech with a fool: Sirach warns against multiplying words with someone without understanding; Proverbs likewise forbids speaking into a fool's ears because intelligent words will meet contempt.

Sirach 22:14
What is heavier than lead? What is its name except a fool?
  Proverbs 27:3 — The fool as an exceptionally heavy burden: Sirach calls the fool heavier than lead; Proverbs calls a fool's anger heavier than stone and sand. The materials and scope differ, but the disproportionate weight image is shared.

Sirach 22:15
Sand, salt, and a lump of iron are easier to bear than a person without understanding.
  Proverbs 27:3 — The fool outweighing heavy materials: Sirach finds sand, salt, and iron easier to bear than an uncomprehending person; Proverbs similarly compares stone and sand with the still heavier anger of a fool.

Sirach 22:16
A wooden bond fitted into a building will not be loosened in a shaking; so a heart established on thoughtful counsel will not be cowardly when the time comes.
  Matthew 7:24-25 — Wise understanding pictured as a stable building: Sirach compares a heart established on counsel to a fitted wooden bond that withstands shaking; Jesus compares hearing and doing His words to a prudent builder whose house withstands wind and water.

Sirach 22:17
A heart made firm upon an understanding thought is like a sanded ornament on a polished wall.
  Sirach 22:16 — Same-chapter architectural images of a firm heart: The heart established through understanding is depicted first through a fitted structural bond and then through an ornament on a polished wall. Both images concern a heart made firm by thought or counsel.

Sirach 22:18
Stakes set on a height will certainly not endure before the wind; so a cowardly heart resting on a foolish thought will certainly not endure before any fear.
  Matthew 7:26-27 — Folly exposed by a destructive storm: Sirach likens a fearful heart resting on foolish thought to stakes unable to withstand wind; Jesus compares failure to act on His words to a foolish builder whose house falls when the storm strikes.

Sirach 22:20
Throwing a stone at birds drives them away, and the one who reproaches a friend will break up friendship.
  Proverbs 17:9 — Reproach and the breaking of friendship: Sirach says reproaching a friend drives friendship away like stones scattering birds; Proverbs contrasts concealing wrongdoings to seek friendship with exposing them in a way that separates friends.

Sirach 22:21
If you draw a sword against a friend, do not despair, for there is a way back.
  Matthew 5:23-24 — The possibility of reconciliation after hostility: Sirach tells someone who has drawn a sword against a friend not to despair of a way back; Jesus directs a person whose brother has a grievance to seek reconciliation before presenting a gift.

Sirach 22:22
If you open your mouth against a friend, do not be afraid, for there is reconciliation—except for reproach, pride, disclosure of a secret, and a treacherous blow; at these things every friend will flee.
  Sirach 27:16-21 — Disclosure of secrets as a grave breach of friendship: Sirach distinguishes reparable hostile speech from breaches that drive a friend away, including disclosed secrets; chapter 27 likewise allows reconciliation after abuse but treats betrayed secrets as destroying trust and hope.

Sirach 22:23
Establish trust with your neighbor in poverty, so that you may be filled together with him in his good circumstances. Stay with him in a time of affliction, so that you may share his inheritance.
  Proverbs 17:17 — Friendship maintained in adversity: Sirach urges faithfulness to a neighbor in poverty and affliction; Proverbs calls for a friend at all times and for brothers to be useful in need.

Sirach 22:24
Before fire come a furnace’s vapor and smoke; so before bloodshed come insults.
  Proverbs 15:1 — Inflammatory words preceding conflict: Sirach says insults precede bloodshed as smoke precedes fire; Proverbs says a painful word stirs up anger and contrasts a yielding answer that turns wrath away.

Sirach 22:25
I will not be ashamed to shelter a friend, and I will certainly not hide from his face.
  Proverbs 27:10 — Refusing to abandon a friend: Sirach declares willingness to shelter a friend without hiding from him; Proverbs directly forbids abandoning one's friend or an ancestral friend.

Sirach 22:27
Who will place a guard upon my mouth and a skillful seal upon my lips, so that I do not fall because of it and my tongue destroy me?
  Psalms 140:3 — Prayer for a guard over the mouth: Both ask for a guard upon the mouth and protection around the lips. Sirach describes a skillful seal, while the psalm describes a restraining door.

Sirach 23:1
Lord, Father and Master of my life, do not abandon me to their counsel; do not let me fall through them.
  Psalms 140:3-4 — Prayer against being led into sinful speech: Following the request to guard the mouth, Sirach asks God not to abandon him to their counsel or let him fall through them; the psalm also joins a guarded mouth to prayer against a heart turned toward wicked words.

Sirach 23:2
Who will set lashes over my thought and wisdom’s discipline over my heart, so that they may not spare my errors of ignorance, and He may certainly not pass over their sins,
  Psalms 140:5 — Welcoming corrective discipline: Sirach seeks discipline over thought and heart so that errors are not spared; the psalm welcomes a righteous person's merciful discipline and correction.

Sirach 23:3
so that my errors of ignorance do not multiply and my sins increase, and I fall before my adversaries and my enemy gloat over me?
  Psalms 37:17-19 — Sin, stumbling, and enemies' gloating: Sirach prays that multiplying sins not cause a fall before gloating enemies; the psalm joins concern over lawlessness to the plea that enemies not rejoice when the speaker's feet are shaken.

Sirach 23:4
Lord, Father and God of my life, do not give me haughty eyes,
  Psalms 130:1 — Rejecting haughty eyes: Sirach asks God not to give him haughty eyes; the psalmist declares that his eyes were not raised high and his heart was not exalted.

Sirach 23:5
and turn desire away from me.
  Psalms 118:36-37 — Prayer for desire to be redirected: Sirach asks that desire be turned away; the psalm asks God to bend the heart away from greed and turn the eyes from futility toward His way.

Sirach 23:6
Do not let the appetite of the belly and sexual intercourse overtake me, and do not hand me over to a shameless soul.
  Romans 13:13-14 — Resisting bodily and sexual appetites: Sirach asks not to be overcome by belly and sexual appetite; Romans forbids revelry, drunkenness, and sexual promiscuity and rejects provision for fleshly desires.

Sirach 23:7
Listen, children, to discipline of the mouth; the one who keeps it will certainly not be caught.
  Proverbs 13:3 — Guarded speech preserving life: Sirach promises that the keeper of mouth-discipline will not be caught; Proverbs says guarding one's mouth preserves life and contrasts rash lips.

Sirach 23:8
A sinner will be caught by his lips, and an abusive person and a proud person will stumble through them.
  Proverbs 12:13 — A sinner trapped through speech: Both explicitly describe a sinner falling into a trap through the lips. Sirach additionally names abusive and proud speakers as stumbling through them.

Sirach 23:9
Do not accustom your mouth to an oath, and do not make a habit of naming the Holy One.
  James 5:12 — Restraint from habitual oaths: Sirach warns against accustoming the mouth to oaths; James commands plain yes and no instead of swearing and connects the warning with judgment.

Sirach 23:10
For just as a household slave under continual examination will not be free of bruises, so the one who continually swears and invokes the name will certainly not be cleansed from sin.
  Exodus 20:7 — Invoking God's name and accountability for sin: Sirach warns that continual swearing and invoking the name will not leave a person cleansed from sin; Exodus refuses acquittal to someone who takes the Lord's name in vain. The target specifically addresses vain invocation.

Sirach 23:11
A man of many oaths will be filled with lawlessness, and a scourge will not depart from his house. If he offends, his sin is upon him; if he disregards it, he has sinned twice. If he swore in vain, he will not be justified, for his house will be filled with calamities.
  Zechariah 5:3-4 — False swearing bringing calamity upon a house: Sirach warns that vain or offending oaths fill a house with calamities; Zechariah describes the curse entering and destroying the false swearer's house.

Sirach 23:13
Do not accustom your mouth to crude ignorance, for sinful speech is in it.
  Ephesians 5:4 — Rejecting crude and foolish speech: Sirach forbids making crude ignorance a habit of the mouth because sinful speech lies in it; Ephesians rejects shamefulness, foolish talk, and coarse joking as unfitting.

Sirach 23:14
Remember your father and mother, for you sit among dignitaries, lest you forget yourself before them and become foolish through your habit; then you will wish you had not been born and curse the day of your birth.
  Job 3:1-3 — Cursing the day of one's birth: Sirach describes birth-regret and a curse on one's birth day as a possible result of public foolishness; Job actually curses his birth day in suffering. The particular expression is shared, while its circumstances differ.

Sirach 23:15
A person accustomed to words of reproach will certainly not be instructed throughout all his days.
  Sirach 23:13 — Same-chapter harmful habits of speech: The warning against habitual crude speech is extended to a person habituated to reproach who remains uninstructed. Both focus on speech becoming an entrenched habit.

Sirach 23:17
a heated soul, like a burning fire, will certainly not be quenched until it is consumed; a sexually immoral man in his fleshly body will certainly not stop until he kindles a fire. Every loaf is sweet to a sexually immoral man; he will certainly not cease until he dies.
  Proverbs 6:27-29 — Sexual misconduct pictured as consuming fire: Sirach places the heated soul's consuming fire beside the sexually immoral man's kindled fire; Proverbs compares involvement with a married woman to carrying fire or walking on burning coals without escaping injury.

Sirach 23:18
A man who transgresses from his marriage bed says in his soul, “Who sees me? Darkness is around me, and the walls cover me; no one sees me. What should I fear? The Most High will certainly not remember my sins.”
  Job 24:15 — The adulterer relying on darkness for concealment: Both give the adulterer's claim that no one will see him in darkness. Job also describes concealing the face; Sirach exposes the further mistaken assumption that God will not remember.

Sirach 23:19
Human eyes are what he fears; he does not know that the Lord’s eyes are ten thousand times brighter than the sun, surveying every human way and observing hidden places.
  Proverbs 5:21 — God's eyes observing every human way: Sirach denies that hidden places conceal misconduct from the Lord's eyes; Proverbs says a man's ways are before God's eyes and that He watches all his tracks, in a warning about another woman.

Sirach 23:20
Before all things were created, they were known to Him, and likewise after they were completed.
  Jeremiah 1:5 — Divine knowledge before formation: Sirach states God's knowledge of all things before their creation; God's declaration that He knows Jeremiah before forming him supplies a particular instance of prior knowledge.

Sirach 23:21
This man will receive retribution in the city’s squares and be seized where he did not suspect it.
  Proverbs 5:22-23 — Sexual wrongdoing overtaken by its consequences: Sirach's adulterer is seized where he did not expect it; Proverbs describes lawless acts hunting a person and the cords of sins binding him, within its warning about another woman.

Sirach 23:22
So also a woman who leaves her husband and presents an heir from another man.
  Romans 7:2-3 — A wife joining another man during marriage: Sirach condemns the wife leaving her husband and presenting another man's heir; Romans describes a woman belonging to another man while her husband lives as adulterous. The heir is Sirach's additional detail.

Sirach 23:23
For first she disobeyed the law of the Most High; second she wronged her husband; and third she committed adultery in sexual immorality and presented children from another man.
  Exodus 20:13 — Adultery as disobedience to God's law: Sirach explicitly names the woman's adultery as disobedience to the Most High's law; the native Exodus 20:13 gives the command against adultery.

Sirach 23:24
She will be brought before the assembly, and there will be a visitation upon her children.
  Sirach 23:25 — Same-chapter elaboration of visitation upon offspring: The announcement of visitation upon the woman's children is followed by the image of children without a spreading root and branches without fruit. The later verse develops this stated consequence.

Sirach 23:25
Her children will not spread out into a root, and her branches will not bear fruit.
  Wisdom of Solomon 4:3-6 — Unlawful offspring described by unstable roots and fruit: Sirach describes the children through roots that do not spread and fruitless branches; Wisdom likewise uses insecure roots and useless fruit for offspring from unlawful unions.

Sirach 23:26
She will leave her memory as a curse, and her reproach will not be erased.
  Proverbs 6:32-33 — Adultery's lasting reproach: Sirach predicts an unerased reproach and cursed memory for the adulterous woman; Proverbs describes the adulterer's dishonor and says his reproach will never be wiped away.

Sirach 23:27
Those left behind will know that nothing is better than the fear of the Lord, and nothing sweeter than attending to the Lord’s commandments.
  Psalms 18:10-12 — The excellence of fearing God and the sweetness of His judgments: Sirach praises fear of the Lord and the sweetness of attending to His commandments; the psalm places enduring fear of the Lord beside judgments sweeter than honey and the reward of keeping them.

Sirach 24:1
Wisdom will praise her own soul and boast in the midst of her people.
  Proverbs 8:2-4 — Personified wisdom speaking publicly: Sirach introduces Wisdom praising herself among her people; Proverbs likewise presents Wisdom at public ways and gates, sending her voice to human beings.

Sirach 24:2
She will open her mouth in the assembly of the Most High and boast before His host:
  Proverbs 8:2-4 — Wisdom's public address: Wisdom opens her mouth before the Most High's assembly in Sirach; Proverbs presents her speaking at public gathering places to human beings. The audiences differ, but the speaking personification is shared.

Sirach 24:3
“I came forth from the mouth of the Most High and covered the earth like mist.
  Wisdom of Solomon 7:25 — Wisdom coming forth from God: Sirach has Wisdom come from the Most High's mouth; Wisdom of Solomon calls her a breath of God's power and a pure outflow of His glory. Both portray her origin through an image of proceeding from God.

Sirach 24:4
I dwelt in the heights, and my throne was in a pillar of cloud.
  Exodus 13:21-22 — The pillar of cloud: Wisdom locates her throne in a pillar of cloud; Exodus describes God's guidance of Israel in a pillar of cloud. The connection is the distinctive image of divine presence, without claiming that Exodus explicitly names Wisdom.

Sirach 24:5
Alone I encircled heaven’s circuit and walked in the depth of the abysses.
  Proverbs 8:24-30 — Wisdom associated with the cosmic heights and depths: Sirach's Wisdom traverses heaven and the abyss; Proverbs describes her before the deeps and present with God as heaven and the world's springs are established.

Sirach 24:6
In the waves of the sea, in all the earth, and in every people and nation I gained possession.
  Wisdom of Solomon 8:1 — Wisdom's universal reach and rule: Sirach describes Wisdom's possession extending through sea, earth, and every people; Wisdom of Solomon says she reaches from end to end and governs all things well.

Sirach 24:7
Among all these I sought rest, and in whose inheritance I might lodge.
  Sirach 24:8 — Same-chapter search for a dwelling answered: Wisdom's search for rest and an inheritance in which to lodge receives the particular answer of the Creator settling her tent in Jacob and assigning her Israel as an inheritance.

Sirach 24:8
Then the Creator of all commanded me; my Creator settled my tent and said, ‘Dwell in Jacob, and receive your inheritance in Israel.’
  Psalms 147:8-9 — Divine instruction entrusted particularly to Israel: Wisdom is commanded to dwell in Jacob and receive Israel as her inheritance; the psalm describes God's word and judgments being given to Jacob and Israel in a way not granted to every nation. Sirach 24:23 identifies the discourse with the law.

Sirach 24:9
Before the age, from the beginning, He created me, and for ever I will certainly not cease.
  Proverbs 8:22-25 — Wisdom created before the age: Both personifications speak of the Lord creating or founding Wisdom before the age. Proverbs expands this into a description of Wisdom before the earth, deeps, mountains, and hills.

Sirach 24:10
I ministered before Him in the holy tent, and thus I was established in Zion.
  Psalms 131:13-16 — Zion as the chosen sacred dwelling: Wisdom's ministry in the holy tent and establishment in Zion correspond to the psalm's selection of Zion as the Lord's dwelling and its mention of His priests. The psalm speaks of the Lord's choice, not a separate statement by Wisdom.

Sirach 24:11
Likewise He settled me in the beloved city, and my authority was in Jerusalem.
  Psalms 131:13-14 — The chosen place of dwelling in Zion: Wisdom describes being settled in the beloved city, Jerusalem; the psalm names Zion as the place the Lord selected for His dwelling and rest.

Sirach 24:12
I took root in a glorified people, in the Lord’s portion, His inheritance.
  Deuteronomy 32:9 — Jacob as the Lord's portion and inheritance: Sirach's description of the people as the Lord's portion and inheritance directly corresponds to Deuteronomy's naming of Jacob and Israel in those terms.

Sirach 24:13
I rose high like a cedar in Lebanon, like a cypress in the mountains of Hermon.
  Psalms 91:13 — Flourishing like a cedar of Lebanon: Wisdom's tall cedar of Lebanon image corresponds to the psalm's comparison of the righteous person's flourishing with a cedar in Lebanon.

Sirach 24:14
I rose high like a palm in En-gedi, like rose plants in Jericho, like a stately olive in the plain; I rose high like a plane tree.
  Psalms 51:10 — The flourishing olive-tree image: Sirach compares Wisdom to a stately olive among other trees; the psalmist compares himself to a fruitful olive in God's house. The shared image concerns flourishing, while the subjects and settings differ.

Sirach 24:15
Like cinnamon and aromatic aspalathus I gave a scent; like choice myrrh I spread fragrance, like galbanum, onycha, and stacte, and like a cloud of frankincense in a tent.
  Exodus 30:34-36 — Fragrance using the sanctuary's incense ingredients: Sirach's description of Wisdom's scent names galbanum, onycha, stacte, and frankincense; Exodus lists these ingredients for holy incense placed before the testimonies in the tent.

Sirach 24:16
Like a terebinth I spread out my branches, and my branches were branches of glory and grace.
  Proverbs 3:18 — Wisdom pictured as a tree: Wisdom's spreading terebinth branches form a tree image for her glory and grace; Proverbs likewise calls Wisdom a tree of life for those who hold her fast.

Sirach 24:17
Like a vine I sprouted grace, and my blossoms were fruit of glory and wealth.
  Hosea 14:7-8 — Vine blossom as an image of flourishing: Wisdom describes her gracious growth and blossoms through a vine; Hosea's restoration imagery similarly culminates in blossoming like a vine. Hosea applies the image to restored Israel rather than to personified Wisdom.

Sirach 24:19
Come to me, you who desire me, and be filled from my produce.
  Proverbs 9:4-6 — Wisdom inviting people to eat: Both personified Wisdom discourses invite hearers to come and receive nourishment: Sirach offers her produce, while Proverbs offers her bread and mixed wine and calls for leaving foolishness.

Sirach 24:20
For my memory is sweeter than honey, and my inheritance than a honeycomb.
  Psalms 118:103 — Instruction sweeter than honey: Wisdom calls her memory and inheritance sweeter than honey and honeycomb; the psalmist calls God's sayings sweeter than honey and honeycomb. Sirach's identification with the law in 24:23 makes the instructional connection explicit.

Sirach 24:21
Those who eat me will still hunger, and those who drink me will still thirst.
  John 6:35 — Contrasting hunger and thirst language: Sirach's Wisdom says those eating and drinking her will continue to hunger and thirst; Jesus, the bread of life, promises no hunger or thirst to those who come and believe. The paired images are close, while the stated outcomes contrast.

Sirach 24:22
The one who obeys me will not be ashamed, and those who work in me will not sin.”
  Psalms 118:6-11 — Obedience, freedom from shame, and avoidance of sin: Wisdom promises that obedience avoids shame and working in her avoids sin; the psalm joins attention to God's commandments with freedom from shame and hiding His sayings in the heart to avoid sinning.

Sirach 24:23
All these things are the book of the covenant of God Most High, the law which Moses commanded us as an inheritance for the assemblies of Jacob,
  Deuteronomy 33:4 — Direct correspondence in the law-as-inheritance wording: Sirach identifies the discourse with the law Moses commanded as an inheritance for Jacob's assemblies, closely reproducing the wording of Deuteronomy 33:4.

Sirach 24:25
which fills with wisdom like the Pishon, like the Tigris in the days of new crops,
  Genesis 2:10-14 — Eden's rivers in the picture of abundant wisdom: Sirach names the Pishon and Tigris in its flood image for wisdom; Genesis names both among the rivers flowing from Eden. The connection identifies the particular rivers, without making Genesis itself an allegory of wisdom.

Sirach 24:26
which fills with understanding like the Euphrates, like the Jordan in the days of harvest,
  Joshua 3:15 — The Jordan at harvest as an abundance image: Sirach compares overflowing understanding to the Jordan in harvest days; Joshua explicitly describes the Jordan overflowing its bank during wheat harvest.

Sirach 24:27
which makes instruction appear like light, like the Gihon in the days of vintage.
  Genesis 2:13 — The named river Gihon: Sirach's comparison of instruction to the Gihon participates in the surrounding series of named rivers; Genesis identifies Gihon as one of Eden's rivers. The light and vintage comparisons remain Sirach's own imagery.

Sirach 24:28
The first did not come to know her fully, and likewise the last did not trace her out.
  Job 28:20-23 — Wisdom beyond complete human discovery: Sirach says neither the first nor the last traced Wisdom fully; Job describes wisdom as hidden from human beings while God knows its place.

Sirach 24:29
For her thought grew abundant from the sea, and her counsel from a great abyss.
  Sirach 24:28 — Same-chapter depths explaining Wisdom's untraceability: Wisdom's thought growing abundant from the sea and her counsel from a great abyss develop the preceding statement that neither first nor last has traced her fully. The connection concerns the explicitly stated limits of discovery.

Sirach 24:30
I too came out like a channel from a river, like a watercourse into a garden.
  Proverbs 18:4 — Speech or teaching pictured as flowing water: Sirach's speaker compares himself to a river channel watering a garden; Proverbs compares a word in the heart to deep water and a springing river, a fountain of life.

Sirach 24:31
I said, “I will water my garden and drench my garden bed.” Then behold, my channel became a river, and my river became a sea.
  Sirach 24:30 — Same-chapter expansion of the teaching-water image: The channel and garden watercourse of the preceding verse grow into a river and sea as the speaker waters his garden. This is the deliberate expansion of the same water image.

Sirach 24:32
I will again make instruction shine like the dawn and reveal these things far away.
  Proverbs 6:23 — Instruction as illuminating light: Sirach promises to make instruction shine like dawn; Proverbs calls the law's commandment a lamp and light and joins it with correction and discipline.

Sirach 24:33
I will again pour out teaching like prophecy and leave it for generations of ages.
  Psalms 77:4-6 — Teaching preserved for future generations: Sirach's speaker promises to leave teaching for generations of ages; the psalm describes transmitting God's testimony and law so that children yet to be born will teach their own children.

Sirach 24:34
See that I have labored not for myself alone, but for all who seek her.
  Sirach 33:18 — Repeated declaration of labor for others: Both statements expressly say the speaker has labored not for himself alone but for others seeking wisdom or instruction.

Sirach 25:1
In three things I was adorned and stood beautiful before the Lord and human beings: harmony among brothers, friendship among neighbors, and a wife and husband living agreeably with one another.
  Psalms 132:1 — The beauty of brothers dwelling in harmony: Sirach includes harmony among brothers among the relationships beautiful before God and people; the psalm praises brothers dwelling together as good and delightful.

Sirach 25:3
You have not gathered in youth; how could you find anything in your old age?
  Sirach 6:18 — Learning in youth for wisdom in old age: The question about finding in old age what was not gathered in youth corresponds to the earlier instruction to choose discipline in youth and find wisdom until the hair is gray.

Sirach 25:4
How fitting is judgment for gray hair, and knowledge of counsel for elders!
  Job 12:12 — Wisdom and knowledge associated with long life: Sirach calls judgment and counsel fitting for gray-haired elders; Job associates wisdom with much time and knowledge with a long life.

Sirach 25:5
How fitting is wisdom for the old, and thought and counsel for the honored!
  Job 32:7-9 — Expectation of wisdom in age, with a qualification: Sirach calls wisdom fitting for the old and thought and counsel fitting for the honored; Elihu recounts expecting many years to bring wisdom, then qualifies this by denying that elders necessarily know judgment.

Sirach 25:6
Long experience is the crown of the old, and their boast is the fear of the Lord.
  Proverbs 16:31 — Old age as a crown associated with reverence or righteousness: Sirach calls long experience the old person's crown and fear of the Lord his boast; Proverbs calls old age a crown of boasting found in righteous ways.

Sirach 25:7
Nine things I considered happy in my heart, and the tenth I will speak with my tongue: a person rejoicing in children, one who lives to see his enemies’ fall;
  Proverbs 23:24-25 — Rejoicing in one's children: Sirach includes a person rejoicing in children among the happy; Proverbs describes parental rejoicing over a wise son and urges the child to gladden father and mother. The enemy's fall is a separate clause.

Sirach 25:8
happy is the one who lives with an understanding wife, who has not slipped with his tongue, and who has not served someone unworthy of him;
  Sirach 26:1-3 — The good wife and her husband's happiness: Sirach calls living with an understanding wife happy; chapter 26 similarly calls the good wife's husband happy and describes the gladness she brings. The target supports the marital clause.

Sirach 25:9
happy is the one who has found prudence and the one who speaks into listening ears.
  Proverbs 3:13 — Happiness in finding understanding: Sirach calls the finder of prudence happy; Proverbs blesses the person who finds wisdom and sees good sense. This supports the first of the verse's two commendations.

Sirach 25:10
How great is the one who has found wisdom! Yet he is not above the one who fears the Lord.
  Proverbs 9:10 — Fear of the Lord as wisdom's foundation: Sirach says that even a finder of wisdom is not above the person who fears the Lord; Proverbs makes fear of the Lord wisdom's beginning, connecting the two while giving fear a foundational role.

Sirach 25:11
The fear of the Lord has surpassed everything; to whom will the one who holds it fast be compared?
  Sirach 1:18 — The preeminence of fearing the Lord: The statement that fear of the Lord surpasses everything corresponds to its earlier description as the crown of wisdom, making peace and healing flourish.

Sirach 25:16
I would choose to live with a lion and a dragon rather than live with an evil woman.
  Proverbs 21:19 — Preference for harsh surroundings over a harmful marriage: Sirach prefers dwelling with a lion and dragon to an evil woman; Proverbs prefers desert dwelling to a battling, talkative, quick-tempered woman. Both express the comparison through an undesirable alternative dwelling.

Sirach 25:18
Her husband will recline among his neighbors and involuntarily groan bitterly.
  Sirach 25:23 — Same-chapter description of a husband's distress: The husband's involuntary bitter groaning among neighbors is further characterized in the chapter's description of an evil wife as a humbled heart, gloomy face, and wounded heart.

Sirach 25:20
A sandy ascent under an old person’s feet—so is a talkative wife to a quiet husband.
  Proverbs 27:15 — A difficult marriage compared to adverse physical conditions: Sirach compares a talkative wife with a sandy ascent beneath an old person's feet; Proverbs compares an abusive wife with wintry drops driving a man from his house. The comparisons and descriptions of the wife differ.

Sirach 25:21
Do not fall for a woman’s beauty, and do not long for a woman.
  Proverbs 6:25 — Resisting desire aroused by beauty: Both warn against being overcome by desire for a woman's beauty. Proverbs adds being hunted by one's eyes and seized by her eyelids.

Sirach 25:23
An evil wife is a humbled heart, a gloomy face, and a wound of the heart; one who will not make her husband happy is slack hands and paralyzed knees.
  Proverbs 12:4 — A harmful wife portrayed through bodily damage: Sirach describes an evil wife through a wounded heart, slack hands, and paralyzed knees; Proverbs likens a woman doing evil to a worm in wood destroying a man.

Sirach 25:24
From a woman came the beginning of sin, and because of her we all die.
  Genesis 3:17-19 — An interpretation of sin and mortality in the Eden narrative: Sirach traces the beginning of sin and human death to the woman; God's sentence on Adam in Genesis connects his listening to his wife and eating with his eventual return to earth. Genesis also names Adam's own act of eating.

Sirach 25:26
If she does not walk according to your direction, cut her off from your flesh.
  Genesis 2:23-24 — Contrast with the marital bond expressed as shared flesh: Sirach's instruction to cut the wife off from one's flesh reverses the unity expressed when Genesis calls the woman flesh of the man's flesh and the married pair one flesh. The target explains the marital image, not a rule endorsing Sirach's stated condition.

Sirach 26:1
Happy is the husband of a good wife, and the number of his days is doubled.
  Proverbs 18:22 — wisdom parallel: Both call finding a good wife a fortunate gift: Sirach pronounces her husband happy, while Proverbs says he has found favors and gladness from God.

Sirach 26:2
A courageous wife gladdens her husband, and he will complete his years in peace.
  Proverbs 31:10-12 — wisdom parallel: Sirach's courageous wife gives her husband a peaceful life; Proverbs describes the courageous woman as trustworthy to her husband and doing him good throughout life.

Sirach 26:3
A good wife is a good portion; she will be given among the portion of those who fear the Lord.
  Proverbs 19:14 — wisdom parallel: Sirach places a good wife among the Lord's gifts to those who fear Him; Proverbs distinguishes inherited property from the wife whom God joins to a husband.

Sirach 26:4
Whether rich or poor, his heart is good, and at every time his face is cheerful.
  Proverbs 15:13 — wisdom parallel: The good heart and cheerful face in Sirach correspond to Proverbs' rejoicing heart and flourishing face; the outward expression follows the inward condition in both.

Sirach 26:5
My heart feared three things, and before a fourth presence I made supplication: a city’s slander, an assembled mob, and a false accusation—all more grievous than death.
  Matthew 26:59-60 — narrative illustration: Sirach fears collective hostility and false accusation; the council's search for false testimony against Jesus supplies a particular instance of assembled accusers pursuing a deadly charge.

Sirach 26:6
A wife jealous of another wife is heartache and mourning, and a scourge of the tongue that reaches everyone.
  Genesis 30:1 — narrative illustration: Sirach connects jealousy between wives with household anguish. Rachel's envy of her sister and distressed demand for children illustrate that rivalry, without identifying her with every detail of Sirach's warning.

Sirach 26:7
An evil wife is a shifting ox-yoke; the one who takes hold of her is like someone grasping a scorpion.
  Sirach 25:16 — literary parallel: Both sayings portray a harmful spouse through dangerous animals: grasping a scorpion in chapter 26 corresponds to chapter 25's preference for living with a lion and dragon over an evil woman.

Sirach 26:8
A drunken wife is great wrath, and she will not conceal her shame.
  Proverbs 23:29-35 — ethical parallel: Sirach warns that drunkenness exposes a wife's shame; Proverbs describes drinkers' bruises, crooked speech, exposure, and loss of awareness. The warning about intoxication is shared, though Proverbs addresses drinkers more broadly.

Sirach 26:9
A woman’s sexual immorality will be known in the lifting of her eyes and in her eyelids.
  Proverbs 6:25 — shared imagery: Sirach associates the woman's eyelids with sexual misconduct; Proverbs warns against being seized by a woman's eyelids in its surrounding warning about sexual temptation. Both make the gaze part of that warning.

Sirach 26:10
Keep a firm watch over a headstrong daughter, so that she does not find freedom and use it for herself.
  Sirach 42:11 — literary parallel: Both passages expressly require a firm watch over a headstrong daughter. Chapter 42 expands the warning by describing the household's public shame, while chapter 26 emphasizes her use of freedom.

Sirach 26:11
Be on guard after a shameless eye, and do not marvel if she offends against you.
  Proverbs 6:25 — ethical parallel: Sirach warns the hearer to be on guard in connection with a shameless eye and possible offense. Proverbs warns against desire and being captured by a woman’s eyelids; in their surrounding sexual warnings, both make the gaze a matter for vigilance.

Sirach 26:12
Like a thirsty traveler she will open her mouth and drink from every nearby water; she will sit before every tent peg and open her quiver before an arrow.
  Proverbs 5:15-17 — imagery contrast: Sirach depicts sexual indiscrimination as drinking from every nearby water; Proverbs commands drinking from one's own vessels and keeping the waters exclusive. The water imagery serves opposite patterns of sexual conduct.

Sirach 26:13
A wife’s grace will delight her husband, and her knowledge will fatten his bones.
  Proverbs 12:4 — wisdom comparison: Sirach describes a wife's grace and knowledge strengthening her husband. Proverbs likewise honors a courageous wife as his crown, then contrasts the destructive effect of a woman who does evil.

Sirach 26:14
A silent wife is a gift of the Lord, and there is no exchange for a disciplined soul.
  1 Peter 3:4 — ethical parallel: Sirach values a silent, disciplined wife as the Lord's gift; Peter values the wife's gentle and quiet inner spirit as precious before God. Both place worth on inward restraint.

Sirach 26:15
A modest wife is grace upon grace, and no weight is worth a self-controlled soul.
  1 Peter 3:3-4 — ethical parallel: Sirach says no weight can match a modest, self-controlled wife; Peter contrasts external gold and clothing with the precious, imperishable beauty of a gentle and quiet heart.

Sirach 26:16
The sun rising in the Lord’s heights—so is a good wife’s beauty in the order of her house.
  Song of Songs 6:10 — shared imagery: Sirach compares a good wife's beauty to the rising sun; the Song compares the beloved to dawn, moon, and sun. Both praise feminine beauty through heavenly light.

Sirach 26:17
A lamp shining upon a holy lampstand—so is a beautiful face upon a firmly established stature.
  Exodus 25:31-37 — shared imagery: The shining holy lampstand supplies Sirach's comparison for a beautiful face. Exodus describes the sanctuary lampstand and its lamps shining; the connection is the sacred object used as an image, not an identification of the woman with it.

Sirach 26:18
Golden pillars upon a silver base—so are beautiful feet upon the breast of one who stands firm.
  Song of Songs 5:15 — shared imagery: Both describe bodily beauty through pillars resting on precious bases. Sirach uses gold pillars and a silver base for beautiful feet; the Song uses marble pillars and golden bases for the beloved's legs.

Sirach 26:28
At two things my heart has grieved, and at the third anger came upon me: a warrior in want through need, understanding men when they are treated as refuse, and someone turning back from righteousness to sin. The Lord will prepare him for the sword.
  Ezekiel 18:24 — ethical parallel: One of Sirach's grievous sights is a righteous person turning to sin and facing judgment. Ezekiel likewise says that a righteous person's later injustice brings death despite earlier righteous acts.

Sirach 26:29
A merchant will hardly be delivered from wrongdoing, and a retailer will not be acquitted of sin.
  Hosea 12:8-9 — ethical parallel: Sirach warns that trade readily becomes entangled with wrongdoing. Hosea joins an unjust balance, the claim to have become rich, and sinful labors, giving a particular example of corrupt gain.

Sirach 27:1
Many have sinned for profit, and the one who seeks to increase his gains will turn his eye away.
  1 Timothy 6:9-10 — ethical parallel: Sirach says the pursuit of profit leads many into sin; Paul describes the desire for riches as a snare and the love of money as leading people away from faith into many pains.

Sirach 27:2
A peg will be fixed between the joints of stones, and sin will be squeezed between selling and buying.
  Hosea 12:8-9 — ethical parallel: Sirach locates sin between buying and selling; Hosea's unjust balance and sinful accumulation of wealth give concrete commercial form to that warning.

Sirach 27:3
Unless a person holds fast diligently in the fear of the Lord, his house will quickly be overthrown.
  Proverbs 14:11 — wisdom parallel: Sirach warns that a house falls when its owner fails to hold fast to the Lord's fear; Proverbs contrasts disappearing impious houses with the standing tents of those who act rightly.

Sirach 27:4
When a sieve is shaken, refuse remains; so a person’s rubbish remains in his reasoning.
  Matthew 12:34-37 — ethical parallel: Sirach's sieve image exposes a person's refuse through reasoning; Jesus says speech comes from the heart's abundance and makes the speaker accountable. Both treat expressed words as evidence of inner character.

Sirach 27:5
A furnace tests a potter’s vessels, and a person’s testing is in his deliberation.
  Proverbs 17:3 — shared imagery: Sirach compares testing a person's deliberation with firing pottery; Proverbs compares the Lord's examination of hearts with testing precious metal in a furnace. Both use heat-testing to illuminate personal assessment.

Sirach 27:6
A tree’s fruit reveals its cultivation; so a word reveals the thought of a person’s heart.
  Luke 6:43-45 — literary parallel: Both join a tree known by its fruit with speech revealing the heart. Sirach's cultivation and spoken thought correspond to Jesus' fruit, inner treasure, and mouth speaking from the heart's abundance.

Sirach 27:7
Do not praise a man before he reasons, for this is the testing of human beings.
  Proverbs 18:13 — wisdom comparison: Sirach postpones praise until a person has spoken and been tested; Proverbs condemns answering before hearing. Both require listening before forming an outward response rather than making a premature assessment.

Sirach 27:8
If you pursue what is right, you will attain it and put it on like a glorious robe reaching to the feet.
  Job 29:14 — shared imagery: Sirach pictures attained righteousness as a glorious robe; Job says he clothed himself in righteousness and wrapped himself in justice like a double cloak. Moral conduct is worn as clothing in both.

Sirach 27:9
Birds will lodge with their like, and truth will return to those who practice it.
  Sirach 13:15 — literary parallel: Sirach 27's birds lodging with their own kind repeat the association principle stated in chapter 13: every living creature loves its like. The zoological comparison supports the surrounding discussion of human company.

Sirach 27:10
A lion lies in wait for prey; so sin lies in wait for those who do unjust things.
  1 Peter 5:8 — shared imagery: Sirach depicts sin waiting like a lion for unjust people; Peter depicts the devil as a lion seeking someone to devour. The shared image is predatory danger requiring vigilance, with different agents named.

Sirach 27:11
A godly person’s discourse is always wisdom, but a fool changes like the moon.
  Proverbs 10:31 — wisdom parallel: Sirach characterizes godly discourse as wisdom; Proverbs says the righteous person's mouth drips wisdom. Both link the moral character of the speaker with wise speech.

Sirach 27:12
Among people without understanding, watch the time; among thoughtful people, linger.
  Proverbs 13:20 — ethical parallel: Sirach recommends limiting time among the uncomprehending and lingering with thoughtful people; Proverbs contrasts walking with the wise and walking with fools. Both make chosen company consequential for wisdom.

Sirach 27:13
Fools’ discourse is offensive, and their laughter is in sinful indulgence.
  Ecclesiastes 7:6 — wisdom parallel: Sirach rejects fools' offensive discourse and indulgent laughter; Ecclesiastes likens fools' laughter to noisy thorns beneath a pot and calls it futile. Both strip foolish laughter of its apparent value.

Sirach 27:14
The talk of someone who swears many oaths will make hair stand up, and their quarrel makes one stop the ears.
  Matthew 5:34-37 — ethical comparison: Sirach depicts frequent oath-taking as disturbing speech associated with quarrels. Jesus directs hearers away from oaths toward plain yes and no, supplying a stronger restraint on the same speech practice.

Sirach 27:15
The quarrel of the proud is bloodshed, and their mutual abuse is grievous to hear.
  Proverbs 29:22 — ethical parallel: Sirach connects proud quarrels with bloodshed and mutual abuse; Proverbs says a wrathful man digs up strife and an angry man digs out sins. Both connect quarrelsome behavior with further wrongdoing or harm.

Sirach 27:16
The one who reveals secrets has destroyed trust and will certainly not find a friend close to his soul.
  Proverbs 11:13 — wisdom parallel: Sirach says exposing secrets destroys trust and close friendship; Proverbs contrasts a double-tongued revealer of plans with the faithful person who conceals matters. Confidentiality marks trustworthiness in both.

Sirach 27:17
Love a friend and keep faith with him; but if you reveal his secrets, do not pursue him afterward.
  Proverbs 11:13 — ethical parallel: Sirach's command to keep faith with a friend is immediately threatened by revealing secrets; Proverbs identifies faithful conduct with concealing matters rather than disclosing them.

Sirach 27:18
For as a person has lost his dead, so you have lost your neighbor’s friendship.
  2 Samuel 1:26 — narrative illustration: Sirach compares lost friendship with bereavement. David's grief for Jonathan gives a concrete expression of mourning a beloved friend; David's loss is through death, while Sirach uses that grief as an analogy for broken trust.

Sirach 27:19
As you have released a bird from your hand, so you have let your neighbor go, and you will not catch him.
  Proverbs 6:5 — shared imagery: Sirach pictures the lost friend as a bird released beyond recovery; Proverbs pictures escape from a guarantor's entanglement as a bird escaping a trap. Both use the freed bird, with different human situations in view.

Sirach 27:20
Do not pursue him, because he has gone far away and escaped like a gazelle from a trap.
  Proverbs 6:5 — shared imagery: The friend who has escaped like a gazelle from a trap repeats the escape image Proverbs uses for someone freeing himself from surety. Sirach stresses the pursuer's inability to recover the friend.

Sirach 27:21
For a wound can be bound up, and there is reconciliation after abuse, but the one who revealed secrets has lost hope.
  Sirach 22:21-22 — literary parallel: Both distinguish repairable injury to friendship from disclosure of secrets. Chapter 22 allows return after a drawn sword but names secret-revealing among causes of flight; chapter 27 contrasts healing wounds with lost hope after betrayal.

Sirach 27:22
The one who winks with his eye devises evil things, and no one will remove them from him.
  Proverbs 6:12-14 — literary parallel: Both connect winking or signaling with the eye to a crooked person's schemes. Proverbs expands the gestures into a portrait of a lawless man continually devising evil.

Sirach 27:23
Before your eyes he will make his mouth sweet and marvel at your words, but afterward he will twist his mouth and put a stumbling block in your words.
  Proverbs 26:24-26 — wisdom parallel: Sirach's flatterer speaks sweetly in person but twists words afterward; Proverbs' enemy agrees with his lips while concealing deceit and hostility in his heart. Both expose agreeable speech masking harm.

Sirach 27:24
I have hated many things, but compared none to him; the Lord will hate him too.
  Proverbs 6:16-19 — ethical parallel: In context Sirach names the deceptive flatterer as hated by the Lord. Proverbs includes the unjust tongue, evil schemes, false witness, and manufactured disputes among what the Lord hates.

Sirach 27:25
The one who throws a stone upward throws it upon his own head, and a treacherous blow will open wounds.
  Proverbs 26:27 — shared imagery: Sirach's stone thrown upward returns on the thrower's head; Proverbs' rolled stone returns on the roller. Both portray intended harm recoiling upon its initiator through a returning stone.

Sirach 27:26
The one who digs a pit will fall into it, and the one who sets a trap will be caught in it.
  Ecclesiastes 10:8 — literary parallel: Both expressly say that the person who digs a pit will fall into it. Sirach adds a setter caught in his own trap, extending the pattern of the action's adverse result returning on the actor. Ecclesiastes' surrounding examples also concern work hazards, so malicious intention is not assigned to its pit digger.
  Psalms 7:16 — literary parallel: Both say that the person who digs a pit falls into the very hole he made. The psalm places this reversal within its account of injustice returning upon its maker.

Sirach 27:27
The one who does evil things will have them roll back upon him, and he will certainly not know where they have come to him from.
  Proverbs 26:27 — ethical parallel: Sirach says evil rolls back on its maker unexpectedly; Proverbs depicts the digger falling into his own pit and the rolled stone returning on its roller. The concrete images exemplify returning harm.
  Psalms 7:15-17 — ethical parallel: Sirach says evil deeds roll back upon their maker; the psalm describes conceived injustice and trouble returning upon the offender’s own head. Both portray wrongdoing becoming the wrongdoer’s harm.

Sirach 27:28
Mockery and reproach await the proud, and retribution will lie in wait for him like a lion.
  Proverbs 16:18 — wisdom parallel: Sirach places mockery, reproach, and pursuing retribution before the proud; Proverbs says insolence leads toward ruin. Both connect pride with the downfall that follows it.

Sirach 27:29
Those who rejoice at the fall of the godly will be caught in a trap, and pain will consume them before their death.
  Obadiah 1:12-15 — ethical parallel: Sirach warns that rejoicing at the godly person's fall ends in punishment. Obadiah forbids rejoicing over Judah's destruction and announces recompense returning on the offender's own head.

Sirach 27:30
Resentment and wrath—these too are abominations, and a sinful man will hold them fast.
  Ephesians 4:31 — ethical parallel: Sirach calls resentment and wrath abominations held by a sinner; Ephesians commands bitterness, rage, anger, and malice to be removed. Both reject the deliberate retention of these dispositions.

Sirach 28:1
The one who takes revenge will find revenge from the Lord, and He will certainly keep his sins in view.
  Romans 12:19 — ethical parallel: Sirach warns the avenger that the Lord will avenge and retain his sins; Romans prohibits self-avenging because repayment belongs to the Lord. Both place vengeance under divine judgment.

Sirach 28:2
Forgive your neighbor’s wrong, and then when you pray, your sins will be released.
  Matthew 6:14-15 — ethical parallel: Both explicitly connect forgiving others' wrongs with receiving divine forgiveness. Sirach places the connection in prayer, while Matthew states both the forgiving and unforgiving outcomes.

Sirach 28:3
Does a person keep wrath against another person and seek healing from the Lord?
  Matthew 18:32-35 — ethical comparison: Sirach challenges asking the Lord for healing while keeping anger against another. The parable challenges a servant who receives mercy but refuses mercy to his fellow servant; both expose the inconsistency, though the parable speaks of debt rather than healing.

Sirach 28:4
Does he have no mercy on a person like himself and pray concerning his own sins?
  Matthew 18:32-35 — ethical parallel: Sirach asks how someone without mercy for a fellow human can seek forgiveness for himself. The master in Matthew condemns precisely the servant who received mercy yet withheld it from his fellow servant.

Sirach 28:5
If he himself, being flesh, keeps resentment, who will atone for his sins?
  Matthew 6:15 — ethical parallel: Sirach questions atonement for a mortal who retains resentment; Matthew says the Father will not forgive the person who refuses to forgive others. Both confront persistent unforgiveness as an obstacle to receiving forgiveness.

Sirach 28:6
Remember the last things and stop being hostile; remember decay and death, and remain in the commandments.
  Ecclesiastes 12:7-14 — wisdom parallel: Sirach joins remembrance of decay and death with keeping commandments. Ecclesiastes describes dust returning to earth and concludes with fearing God, keeping commandments, and awaiting judgment.

Sirach 28:7
Remember the commandments and do not be resentful toward your neighbor; remember the covenant of the Most High and overlook ignorance.
  Leviticus 19:18 — ethical parallel: Sirach's command not to resent a neighbor recalls the law's prohibition of vengeance and lasting anger against one's people, joined to loving the neighbor as oneself.

Sirach 28:8
Keep away from quarrelling, and you will lessen sins, for a hot-tempered person will kindle a quarrel.
  Proverbs 29:22 — wisdom parallel: Sirach says avoiding quarrels lessens sins because temper kindles conflict; Proverbs links a wrathful man with strife and an angry man with sins. The same temper-to-conflict-to-sin sequence is present.

Sirach 28:9
A sinful man will disturb friends and cast slander among those at peace.
  Proverbs 16:28 — ethical parallel: Sirach's sinner disrupts friends by slander; Proverbs' crooked man spreads evil and deceit and separates friends. Both identify malicious communication as a destroyer of relationships.

Sirach 28:10
As the fuel for a fire is, so it will burn; as a quarrel grows firm, so it will burn. A person’s anger will match his strength, and he will raise his wrath according to his wealth.
  Proverbs 26:20-21 — shared imagery: Both compare the supply of fuel to the growth of a quarrel. Proverbs makes wood and coals correspond to the abusive person who sustains conflict; Sirach similarly connects increasing fuel and increasing strife.

Sirach 28:11
A hurried dispute kindles fire, and a rushing quarrel pours out blood.
  Proverbs 26:21 — shared imagery: Sirach describes a hurried dispute as kindling fire and spilling blood; Proverbs makes an abusive person fuel for the turmoil of battle. Both give conflict the destructive action of a fed fire.

Sirach 28:12
If you blow on a spark, it will burn; if you spit on it, it will be quenched. Both come out of your mouth.
  Proverbs 15:1 — wisdom parallel: Sirach contrasts blowing a spark into fire with quenching it, both from the mouth. Proverbs likewise contrasts a yielding answer that turns wrath away with a painful word that stirs it up.

Sirach 28:13
Curse a whisperer and someone double-tongued, for he has destroyed many who were at peace.
  Proverbs 16:28 — ethical parallel: Sirach condemns whispering and double speech for destroying peace; Proverbs describes a crooked speaker separating friends. Both trace communal damage to deceptive speech.

Sirach 28:14
A third tongue has shaken many and scattered them from nation to nation; it has pulled down fortified cities and overthrown dignitaries’ houses.
  James 3:5-6 — ethical comparison: Sirach describes a destructive tongue scattering peoples and overturning houses; James describes the small tongue setting a great forest and the course of life ablaze. Both emphasize damage far larger than the speaking organ.

Sirach 28:16
The one who attends to it will certainly not find rest or dwell in quiet.
  Proverbs 26:20 — ethical comparison: Sirach denies rest and quiet to someone who attends to destructive speech. Proverbs says conflict becomes quiet when the double-minded person is absent, showing the peace gained by removing its verbal fuel.

Sirach 28:17
A lash’s blow makes a bruise, but a tongue’s blow will shatter bones.
  Proverbs 12:18 — shared imagery: Sirach compares the tongue's injury with a lash and shattered bones; Proverbs says some people wound with a sword as they speak. Both describe verbal harm as bodily injury.

Sirach 28:18
Many have fallen by the edge of a sword, yet not as many as those who have fallen through a tongue.
  Proverbs 18:21 — ethical parallel: Sirach attributes more deaths to the tongue than to the sword; Proverbs places death and life in the tongue's hand. Both assign speech power over life and death without making identical numerical claims.

Sirach 28:19
Happy is the one sheltered from it, who has not passed through its anger, who has not pulled its yoke or been bound in its bonds.
  Psalms 30:21 — literary parallel: Sirach blesses the person sheltered from the destructive tongue; the psalm says God shelters His people in a tent from the contradiction of tongues. Both make safety from hostile speech a form of shelter.

Sirach 28:20
For its yoke is an iron yoke, and its bonds are bronze bonds.
  Sirach 6:24-29 — imagery contrast: The harmful tongue imposes iron and bronze bondage, whereas wisdom's fetters and collar become rest, shelter, and glory in chapter 6. The repeated restraint imagery distinguishes oppressive speech from beneficial instruction.

Sirach 28:22
It will certainly not master the godly, and they will not be burned in its flame.
  Psalms 30:21 — ethical parallel: Sirach says the destructive tongue will not master the godly; the psalm describes divine shelter from human turmoil and hostile tongues. Both place the protected person beyond the tongue's destructive rule.

Sirach 28:23
Those who abandon the Lord will fall into it; among them it will burn and certainly not be quenched. It will be sent against them like a lion and ravage them like a leopard.
  Psalms 139:12 — shared imagery: Sirach portrays those abandoning the Lord as prey of destructive speech, likened to a ravaging animal. The psalm joins a warning about a talkative man with evils hunting the unjust person to destruction.

Sirach 28:24
Behold, fence your property with thorns; bind up your silver and gold.
  Sirach 42:6 — literary parallel: Sirach 28's instruction to secure property and precious metal corresponds to chapter 42's instruction to lock things where many hands are present. In chapter 28 this practical guarding prepares the following comparison with guarded speech.

Sirach 28:25
Make a balance and a weight for your words, and make a door and a bar for your mouth.
  Psalms 140:3 — literary parallel: Both expressly picture controlling speech as placing a door around the mouth: Sirach commands a door and bar, while the psalm asks the Lord for a guard and restraining door around the lips.

Sirach 28:26
Take care lest you slip through it, lest you fall before someone lying in ambush.
  Proverbs 21:23 — ethical parallel: Sirach warns that an unguarded mouth can cause a fall before an ambusher; Proverbs says guarding mouth and tongue keeps one's life from affliction. Both connect speech restraint with personal safety.

Sirach 29:1
The one who shows mercy will lend to his neighbor, and the one who strengthens his hand keeps commandments.
  Psalms 111:5 — ethical parallel: Sirach identifies lending to a neighbor with mercy and commandment-keeping; the psalm likewise calls compassionate lending the conduct of a kind man.

Sirach 29:2
Lend to your neighbor at his time of need, and in turn repay your neighbor at the appointed time.
  Psalms 36:21 — ethical parallel: Sirach joins timely lending with timely repayment; the psalm contrasts the sinner who borrows without repaying with the righteous person's compassionate giving. Both distinguish honest help from exploitative borrowing.

Sirach 29:3
Confirm your word and keep faith with him, and at every time you will find what you need.
  Psalms 14:4-5 — ethical parallel: Sirach tells the borrower to confirm his word and keep faith so help remains available. The psalm honors the person who does not set aside an oath to his neighbor, alongside integrity in financial dealings.

Sirach 29:4
Many regarded a loan as a windfall and brought trouble upon those who helped them.
  Psalms 36:21 — ethical parallel: Sirach describes borrowers who treat loans as windfalls and harm their helpers; the psalm identifies borrowing without repayment as sinful. Both address taking the benefit of a loan while refusing its obligation.

Sirach 29:5
Until he receives it, he will kiss the lender’s hands and lower his voice over his neighbor’s money; when repayment is due, he will drag out the time, return words of indifference, and blame the season.
  Psalms 36:21 — ethical parallel: Sirach details a borrower's change from pleading to delaying payment and making excuses. The psalm's sinner who borrows but does not repay states the ethical failure embodied in that conduct.

Sirach 29:6
