If the lender succeeds, he will scarcely recover half and regard it as a windfall. If not, the borrower has deprived him of his money and made him an enemy for nothing. He will repay him with curses and insults, and repay dishonor in place of honor.
  Psalms 108:4-5 — ethical parallel: The borrower in Sirach repays assistance with insults and dishonor; the psalmist receives slander, evil, and hatred in exchange for love and good. Both expose hostility returned for a benefactor's help.

Sirach 29:7
Many have turned away, not from wickedness, but because they feared being deprived for nothing.
  Luke 6:34-35 — ethical comparison: Sirach observes that fear of losing money deters lending, before urging continued help in the following verses. Jesus similarly challenges lending governed only by expected repayment and commands lending without expecting anything back.

Sirach 29:8
Nevertheless, be patient with the lowly person and do not keep him waiting for alms.
  Proverbs 3:27-28 — ethical parallel: Sirach says not to leave the lowly waiting for alms; Proverbs says not to send the needy away until tomorrow when one already has the means to help. Both reject avoidable delay in assistance.

Sirach 29:9
For the commandment’s sake, support the poor person; in his need do not turn him away empty.
  Deuteronomy 15:7-10 — legal parallel: Sirach grounds support for the poor in the commandment and forbids sending them away empty. Deuteronomy commands openhanded lending to the needy brother and forbids withholding help out of self-interest.

Sirach 29:10
Lose silver for a brother and a friend, and do not let it corrode under a stone to destruction.
  Luke 12:33 — ethical parallel: Sirach prefers spending silver for a brother or friend to letting it corrode unused. Jesus links giving alms with a lasting treasure beyond the reach of moth and theft, redirecting possessions toward help rather than hoarding.

Sirach 29:11
Place your treasure according to the Most High’s commandments, and it will benefit you more than gold.
  Matthew 6:19-21 — ethical parallel: Sirach locates worthwhile treasure in obedience to the Most High rather than gold's mere possession; Jesus contrasts vulnerable earthly treasures with treasure in heaven and links treasure to the heart's allegiance.

Sirach 29:12
Store up almsgiving in your chambers, and it will rescue you from every affliction.
  Psalms 40:2-3 — ethical parallel: Sirach describes stored almsgiving as rescue in affliction. The psalm blesses care for the poor and needy and promises the Lord's rescue in an evil day, making generosity and protection correspond.

Sirach 29:13
More than a strong shield and more than a weighty spear, it will fight for you against an enemy.
  Psalms 40:2-3 — ethical parallel: In Sirach, almsgiving provides protection stronger than weapons against an enemy. The psalm joins care for the poor with the Lord's guarding and a plea not to be handed into the enemy's hands.

Sirach 29:14
A good man will stand surety for his neighbor, but someone who has lost shame will abandon him.
  Genesis 44:32-34 — narrative illustration: Sirach commends the good man who stands surety for his neighbor. Judah's surety for Benjamin becomes an offer to remain enslaved in his place, a concrete instance of accepting another's liability.
  Philemon 1:18-19 — narrative illustration: Sirach commends standing surety for another; Paul offers to have any debt Onesimus may owe charged to himself and personally promises repayment. The letter supplies an explicit offer of financial guarantee.

Sirach 29:15
Do not forget a guarantor’s favors, for he has given his life on your behalf.
  Genesis 44:32-34 — narrative illustration: Sirach stresses the guarantor's personal exposure on another's behalf. Judah, having guaranteed Benjamin to his father, offers himself as a household slave in Benjamin's place, showing what that responsibility can cost.

Sirach 29:16
A sinner will overthrow a guarantor’s good circumstances, and someone ungrateful in thought will abandon his rescuer.
  Proverbs 17:13 — ethical parallel: Sirach condemns the ungrateful person who ruins and abandons his guarantor; Proverbs condemns returning evil for good and attaches continuing harm to that reversal of kindness.

Sirach 29:17
Surety has destroyed many who were prospering and shaken them like a wave of the sea.
  Proverbs 22:26-27 — wisdom parallel: Sirach warns that surety destroys formerly prosperous people. Proverbs warns against becoming a pledge without means to pay because even one's bedding may be taken, giving concrete form to financial collapse.

Sirach 29:18
It has driven powerful men from home, and they wandered among foreign nations.
  Proverbs 22:26-27 — wisdom comparison: Sirach describes surety driving powerful people from home; Proverbs pictures a pledge costing the debtor his bedding. Both make the danger reach the guarantor's domestic security, though only Sirach describes exile.

Sirach 29:19
A sinner who falls into surety and pursues profitable undertakings will fall into lawsuits.
  Proverbs 6:1-3 — wisdom parallel: Sirach warns of a sinner entering surety and ending in lawsuits. Proverbs describes the guarantor as trapped by his own words and delivered through his friend into hostile hands, requiring urgent release.

Sirach 29:20
Support your neighbor according to your ability, and take care of yourself so that you do not fall.
  2 Corinthians 8:12-13 — ethical parallel: Sirach limits support to one's ability and warns against falling oneself. Paul accepts generosity according to what one has and rejects relief for others purchased by affliction for the giver.

Sirach 29:21
The first necessities of life are water, bread, clothing, and a house that covers nakedness.
  1 Timothy 6:8 — wisdom parallel: Sirach lists bread, water, clothing, and shelter as life's first necessities. Paul likewise treats food and coverings as sufficient grounds for contentment, concentrating on basic provision rather than wealth.

Sirach 29:22
Better a poor person’s life beneath a shelter of beams than splendid dishes in other people’s homes.
  Proverbs 17:1 — wisdom comparison: Sirach prefers a poor person's own shelter to splendid meals in others' homes; Proverbs prefers a peaceful morsel to a well-provisioned house with conflict. Both place a modest, secure condition above troubled abundance.

Sirach 29:23
Be content with little or much, and you will certainly not hear the reproach of living as a guest.
  Philippians 4:11-12 — ethical parallel: Sirach commands contentment with little or much; Paul describes learning contentment both in abundance and need, whether filled or hungry. The contrasting material conditions are explicit in both.

Sirach 29:27
Go out, guest, before a more honored person; my brother has come as my guest, and I need the house.”
  Luke 14:8-10 — social parallel: Sirach's dependent guest is displaced when a more honored guest arrives. Jesus describes the shame of surrendering one's place to a more honored invitee; the situations differ between lodging and seating but share humiliating displacement.

Sirach 30:1
The one who loves his son will continually give him lashes, so that he may rejoice at his last days.
  Proverbs 13:24 — wisdom parallel: Both ancient sayings connect love for a son with parental discipline. Proverbs contrasts this with sparing the staff; Sirach adds the father's hoped-for joy in later days.

Sirach 30:2
The one who disciplines his son will benefit from him and boast about him among acquaintances.
  Proverbs 29:17 — wisdom parallel: Sirach says a disciplined son benefits his father and gives him grounds for public pride; Proverbs promises the disciplining parent rest and adornment to his life. Both portray a favorable parental result.

Sirach 30:3
The one who teaches his son will make his enemy envious and rejoice over him before friends.
  Proverbs 23:24-25 — wisdom parallel: Sirach's father rejoices before friends over his taught son; Proverbs calls father and mother to rejoice over a wise son. The shared connection is parental joy arising from the child's formation.

Sirach 30:4
His father died, yet it is as though he did not die, for he left someone like himself after him.
  Sirach 44:11-14 — literary parallel: Sirach 30 pictures the dead father continuing through the son who resembles him. Chapter 44 describes descendants preserving the ancestors' inheritance and covenants while the buried ancestors' name lives through generations.

Sirach 30:5
During his life he saw him and rejoiced, and at his death he was not grieved.
  Proverbs 23:24-25 — wisdom parallel: Sirach emphasizes a father's joy in seeing his son during life; Proverbs describes the righteous father's rejoicing over a wise son. Sirach extends that consolation to the father's death, which Proverbs does not separately address.

Sirach 30:6
He left an avenger against enemies and one who would repay kindness to friends.
  1 Kings 2:5-9 — narrative illustration: Sirach's son inherits responsibility to answer enemies and repay friends' kindness. David's instructions to Solomon combine judgment on Joab and Shimei with mercy toward Barzillai's sons, illustrating both sides of that succession.

Sirach 30:7
The one who pampers a son will bind up his wounds, and at every cry his inward parts will be troubled.
  Proverbs 29:15 — wisdom parallel: Sirach warns that indulgence in raising a son brings distress to the parent's inward life. Proverbs contrasts corrective formation with a wandering child who shames his parents; both describe parental suffering from an unrestrained child.

Sirach 30:8
An untamed horse turns out stubborn, and an unrestrained son turns out headstrong.
  Proverbs 29:15 — wisdom parallel: Sirach compares the unrestrained son's stubbornness with an untamed horse. Proverbs contrasts blows and reproof that teach wisdom with the wandering child who brings shame, likewise opposing formation to unchecked behavior.

Sirach 30:9
Pamper a child, and he will terrify you; play with him, and he will grieve you.
  Proverbs 29:15 — wisdom parallel: Sirach says pampering a child brings the parent fear and grief; Proverbs says the wandering child shames his parents. Both warnings focus on the household consequences of failing to restrain the child.

Sirach 30:10
Do not laugh together with him, so that you do not suffer together and in the end gnash your teeth.
  Proverbs 29:17 — wisdom contrast: Sirach warns that indulgent familiarity can end in shared suffering and grief. Proverbs offers the opposite outcome through discipline: rest and adornment for the parent. The connection concerns the contrasting results of upbringing.

Sirach 30:11
Do not give him authority in youth.
  Galatians 4:1-2 — social comparison: Sirach withholds authority from a son in youth. Paul's illustration likewise places the child heir under guardians and stewards until the father's appointed time, despite the child's status as master of everything.

Sirach 30:12
Bruise his sides while he is little, lest he become hardened and disobey you.
  Proverbs 23:13-14 — wisdom parallel: Both texts present corporal discipline of a young child as preventative correction. Sirach names hardening and disobedience as the danger; Proverbs presents the rod as rescuing the child's life from death.

Sirach 30:13
Discipline your son and work upon him, so that you do not stumble through his shamelessness.
  Proverbs 29:17 — wisdom parallel: Sirach directs the parent to discipline the son to avoid suffering through his shamelessness. Proverbs commands discipline and describes the resulting rest and adornment of the parent's life.

Sirach 30:16
There is no wealth better than bodily health, and no gladness beyond joy of heart.
  Proverbs 17:22 — wisdom parallel: Sirach links bodily well-being with joy of heart as surpassing goods. Proverbs says a rejoicing heart makes a person flourish while sorrow dries the bones, directly connecting inward joy and bodily condition.

Sirach 30:17
Death is better than a bitter life, and eternal rest than persistent illness.
  Job 3:20-22 — lament parallel: Sirach presents death as preferable to bitter life and enduring illness. Job asks why suffering people receive life when they long for death and rejoice to obtain it, expressing the same comparison from within anguish.

Sirach 30:18
Good things poured out before a closed mouth are like offerings of food set beside a tomb.
  Ecclesiastes 6:2 — wisdom parallel: Sirach's good food before a closed mouth depicts possessions that cannot be enjoyed. Ecclesiastes describes a rich owner whom God does not empower to eat from his own goods, exposing the same gap between having and enjoying.

Sirach 30:19
What benefit is an offering to an idol? For it neither eats nor smells; so is the one pursued by the Lord.
  Psalms 113:12-14 — literary parallel: Sirach's comparison depends on an idol's inability to eat or smell. The psalm describes manufactured idols whose mouths do not speak and whose noses do not smell, supplying the same image of lifeless sensory incapacity.

Sirach 30:21
Do not give your soul over to grief, and do not afflict yourself with your deliberation.
  Philippians 4:6-7 — ethical comparison: Sirach warns against handing the soul over to grief and tormenting oneself in thought. Paul directs anxiety into prayer and describes God's peace guarding hearts and thoughts, addressing the same vulnerable inner life with a distinct response.

Sirach 30:22
Gladness of heart is a person’s life, and a man’s rejoicing is length of days.
  Proverbs 17:22 — wisdom parallel: Sirach says gladness of heart supports life and length of days; Proverbs says a rejoicing heart makes one flourish and contrasts sorrow's effect upon the bones. Both connect joy with vitality.

Sirach 30:23
Beguile your soul, comfort your heart, and put grief far from you; for grief has destroyed many, and there is no benefit in it.
  2 Corinthians 7:10 — ethical comparison: Sirach warns that grief destroys many and offers no benefit in the grief it describes. Paul also names sorrow that produces death, while expressly distinguishing beneficial godly sorrow leading to repentance; that distinction prevents equating all sorrow with harm.

Sirach 30:24
Jealousy and anger shorten days, and anxiety brings old age before its time.
  Sirach 38:18 — literary parallel: Sirach 30 says anger, jealousy, and anxiety shorten life and age a person. Chapter 38 likewise says grief brings death and bends strength, linking destructive inward distress with bodily decline.

Sirach 30:25
A bright and good heart will take care over the dishes it eats.
  Ecclesiastes 9:7 — wisdom parallel: Sirach connects a bright, good heart with taking care over food; Ecclesiastes directs eating bread with gladness and drinking wine with a good heart. Both connect a good heart with the manner of eating.

Sirach 31:1
Wakefulness over wealth wastes the flesh, and anxiety about it drives away sleep.
  Ecclesiastes 5:11 — wisdom parallel: Both directly connect wealth with lost sleep: Sirach describes anxious wakefulness wasting the flesh, while Ecclesiastes says being filled with riches does not let the owner sleep.

Sirach 31:2
The anxiety of wakefulness will drive away drowsiness, and a severe illness will rouse a person from sleep.
  Ecclesiastes 5:11 — wisdom parallel: Sirach continues the description of anxiety driving away sleep after its warning about wealth. Ecclesiastes likewise opposes restful sleep to the sleeplessness of being filled with riches; Sirach additionally mentions illness.

Sirach 31:3
A rich person toiled in gathering money, and at rest he is filled with his luxuries.
  Luke 12:18-19 — narrative illustration: Sirach describes the rich person's labor accumulating money followed by luxurious rest. The rich man in Jesus' parable plans larger storehouses and then tells his soul to rest, eat, drink, and rejoice; the surrounding parable challenges that security.

Sirach 31:5
The one who loves gold will not be justified, and the one who pursues profits will go astray in them.
  1 Timothy 6:9-10 — ethical parallel: Sirach says love of gold and pursuit of profits lead a person astray; Paul says desire for riches entraps people and love of money leads some away from faith into many pains.

Sirach 31:6
Many were given over to ruin for gold, and their destruction happened before their face.
  1 Timothy 6:9-10 — ethical parallel: Sirach describes people ruined through gold; Paul says the desire for wealth plunges people into ruin and destruction. Both identify the pursuit of riches as a cause of personal collapse.

Sirach 31:7
It is a stumbling block for those infatuated with it, and every fool will be caught by it.
  1 Timothy 6:9-10 — shared imagery: Sirach calls gold a stumbling block that catches the infatuated fool; Paul calls the desire for riches a snare accompanied by foolish and harmful desires. Both portray acquisitiveness as entrapment.

Sirach 31:8
Happy is the rich person who was found blameless and did not go after gold.
  Job 31:24-28 — ethical parallel: Sirach blesses the rich person who remains blameless without pursuing gold. Job rejects appointing gold as his strength or trusting riches, treating that misplaced confidence as grave lawlessness before God.

Sirach 31:9
Who is he? We will call him happy, for he has done wonderful things among his people.
  Job 29:11-12 — social parallel: Sirach calls the community to bless the wealthy person for wonderful deeds among them. Job recalls being called blessed because he rescued the poor and helped the orphan, illustrating public praise grounded in practical help.

Sirach 31:10
Who was tested by it and brought to completion? It will be a boast for him. Who could have transgressed and did not transgress, could have done evil things and did not do them?
  Job 31:24-28 — ethical parallel: Sirach praises integrity when wealth offers an opportunity for wrongdoing. Job's examination of whether he trusted gold or rejoiced in amassed riches supplies a particular statement of accountability under that temptation.

Sirach 31:11
His good circumstances will be established, and the assembly will recount his almsgiving.
  Psalms 111:9 — ethical parallel: Sirach links established well-being and public remembrance with almsgiving. The psalm links gifts scattered to the poor with enduring righteousness and a horn raised in glory, likewise associating generosity and lasting honor.

Sirach 31:12
Have you sat at a great table? Do not open your throat over it or say, “How much there is upon it!”
  Proverbs 23:1-3 — wisdom parallel: Sirach warns the guest at a great table against greedy exclamation and appetite; Proverbs tells the diner at rulers' tables to consider the food and reject insatiable desire.

Sirach 31:13
Remember that an evil eye is bad. What has been created worse than an eye? Therefore it weeps for every reason.
  Sirach 14:8-10 — literary parallel: Sirach 31 warns against the evil eye within its meal instruction. Chapter 14 explicitly connects the evil or greedy eye with dissatisfaction over portions and begrudging bread, clarifying the concern about acquisitive looking.

Sirach 31:14
Do not stretch your hand toward whatever it looks at, and do not crowd together with it in the dish.
  Proverbs 23:1-3 — wisdom parallel: Sirach restrains the hand that reaches greedily for a shared dish; Proverbs tells the diner to consider what is offered and not desire the food insatiably. Both require restraint in approaching a host's table.

Sirach 31:15
Understand your neighbor’s concerns from yourself, and consider every matter.
  Matthew 7:12 — ethical parallel: Sirach tells the diner to understand the neighbor's concerns through his own. Jesus likewise directs treating others according to the treatment one would wish for oneself, making self-understanding a guide to consideration.

Sirach 31:16
Eat what is set before you like a human being, and do not chew greedily, lest you be hated.
  Proverbs 23:1-3 — wisdom parallel: Sirach forbids greedy eating that makes a guest hated; Proverbs instructs the diner at a great table to curb insatiable desire. Both address appetite under the discipline of shared-table conduct.

Sirach 31:17
Be the first to stop for the sake of good discipline; do not be insatiable, lest you stumble.
  Proverbs 25:16 — wisdom parallel: Sirach says to stop eating before insatiability causes a stumble; Proverbs says to eat only sufficient honey lest fullness end in vomiting. Both counsel stopping at enough to avoid the consequence of excess.

Sirach 31:18
If you have sat among many, do not stretch out your hand before them.
  1 Corinthians 11:33 — ethical comparison: Sirach tells a diner among many not to reach out before the others. Paul tells those gathering to eat to wait for one another; his setting is the communal meal discussed with the Lord's supper, not an identification with Sirach's banquet.

Sirach 31:19
How sufficient a little is for an instructed person! He does not pant upon his bed.
  Proverbs 25:16 — wisdom parallel: Sirach says little suffices the instructed person and avoids discomfort in bed. Proverbs likewise prescribes only sufficient honey rather than a fullness that causes vomiting, making moderation protective of the body.

Sirach 31:20
Healthy sleep comes with a moderate stomach. He rose early, and his soul was with him. The pain of sleeplessness, bilious vomiting, and cramp accompany an insatiable man.
  Proverbs 25:16 — wisdom parallel: Sirach contrasts moderate eating and healthy sleep with excess, vomiting, and cramp. Proverbs also links excessive consumption with vomiting and counsels sufficient rather than unlimited food.

Sirach 31:21
If you have been forced to eat too much, rise, vomit at a distance, and you will find rest.
  Proverbs 25:16 — wisdom comparison: Both ancient dietary sayings connect overeating and vomiting. Proverbs counsels moderation to prevent that result; Sirach describes a response after someone has been compelled to eat too much. The preventive and subsequent instructions remain distinct.

Sirach 31:22
Listen to me, child, and do not despise me; in the end you will find my words true. In all your works be skillful, and no illness at all will meet you.
  Proverbs 4:20-22 — wisdom parallel: Sirach asks the child to heed instruction and connects careful conduct with freedom from illness. Proverbs likewise asks the son to attend to its words and calls them life and healing for the flesh.

Sirach 31:23
Lips will bless the generous provider of bread, and testimony to his excellence is trustworthy.
  Proverbs 11:26 — ethical parallel: Sirach promises spoken blessing and a good reputation to the generous provider of bread; Proverbs places blessing upon the head of the person who shares grain. Both praise distribution of staple food.

Sirach 31:24
A city will grumble against someone stingy with bread, and testimony to his meanness is precise.
  Proverbs 11:26 — ethical comparison: Sirach describes the city's complaint against stinginess with bread. Proverbs opposes withholding grain to the blessing attached to sharing it, likewise evaluating a food-holder by the treatment of others.

Sirach 31:25
Do not show your manliness with wine, for wine has destroyed many.
  Isaiah 5:22 — ethical parallel: Both combine references to strength with a warning about drinking. Sirach explicitly rejects proving one's manliness through wine; Isaiah pronounces woe on strong and powerful people who drink wine and mix strong drink.

Sirach 31:26
A furnace tests hardened metal in quenching; so wine tests hearts in the quarrel of the proud.
  Proverbs 20:1 — ethical parallel: Sirach says wine tests hearts in the proud person's quarrel; Proverbs calls drunkenness insolent and incompatible with wisdom. Both make intoxication disclose undisciplined, arrogant conduct.

Sirach 31:27
Wine is like life for human beings if you drink it in its measure. What is life for someone lacking wine? It too was created for human gladness.
  Psalms 103:15 — creation parallel: Sirach says wine was created for human gladness, while insisting on measure. The psalm places wine that gladdens the human heart among God's provisions; it does not separately state Sirach's measure requirement.

Sirach 31:28
Wine drunk at the proper time in sufficient measure is rejoicing of heart and gladness of soul.
  Ecclesiastes 9:7 — wisdom parallel: Sirach describes appropriately timed, measured wine as joy of heart and soul. Ecclesiastes likewise commends drinking wine with a good heart alongside glad eating, sharing the positive valuation without specifying quantity.

Sirach 31:29
Wine drunk in quantity amid provocation and conflict is bitterness of soul.
  Proverbs 23:29-35 — ethical parallel: Sirach connects excessive wine with bitterness, provocation, and conflict. Proverbs describes lingering over wine as producing uproar, lawsuits, bruises, crooked speech, and disorientation, spelling out that destructive pattern.

Sirach 31:30
Drunkenness multiplies a fool’s anger to his stumbling, diminishing strength and adding wounds.
  Proverbs 23:29-35 — ethical parallel: Sirach's drunken fool gains anger, stumbling, weakness, and wounds. Proverbs' drinkers acquire bruises, unstable movement, and repeated blows without awareness, showing the bodily and behavioral damage of intoxication.

Sirach 32:1
Have they appointed you leader? Do not exalt yourself. Be among them as one of them; take care of them, and then sit down.
  Luke 22:26-27 — ethical parallel: Sirach tells the appointed leader to remain among the others and serve before sitting. Jesus likewise places the leader in the role of servant and describes Himself among those at table as the one serving.

Sirach 32:2
After attending to every duty, recline, so that you may rejoice because of them and receive a crown for good order.
  Luke 12:42-43 — ethical comparison: Sirach's banquet leader completes duties before enjoying the meal and receiving honor. Jesus' prudent steward distributes the household's grain and is blessed when found performing that duty; both connect faithful service and favorable recognition.

Sirach 32:3
Speak, elder, for it is fitting for you, with exact knowledge, but do not hinder the music.
  Job 32:6-7 — social parallel: Sirach grants the elder a fitting opportunity to speak with knowledge. Elihu explains his initial silence by his expectation that years should speak wisdom; his following words question whether age guarantees wisdom, so this parallel concerns the initial deference only.

Sirach 32:4
Where there is a performance, do not pour out chatter or display wisdom at an unsuitable time.
  Ecclesiastes 3:7 — wisdom parallel: Sirach identifies a musical performance as an unsuitable time for chatter or displaying wisdom. Ecclesiastes distinguishes a time for silence from a time for speech, supplying the general timing principle applied at the banquet.

Sirach 32:5
A carbuncle signet upon a golden ornament—so is an ensemble of musicians at a wine banquet.
  Proverbs 25:11-12 — shared imagery: Sirach compares well-placed music at a banquet to a gem in gold. Proverbs compares a spoken wise word to precious stones in golden adornment; both use the fitting jewelry setting to praise an apt auditory contribution.

Sirach 32:6
An emerald signet in a gold setting—so is musicians’ melody with pleasant wine.
  Proverbs 25:12 — shared imagery: Sirach's melody with pleasant wine resembles an emerald in gold; Proverbs' wise word to a receptive ear resembles a costly stone set in a golden earring. Both praise an appropriate pairing through a jewel and its setting.

Sirach 32:7
Speak, young man, if you are needed; scarcely twice, if you are asked.
  Job 32:6-7 — social parallel: Sirach restrains the younger person's contribution until it is called for. Elihu describes holding back because he is young and his hearers older, showing deferential restraint before presenting his knowledge.

Sirach 32:8
Sum up your speech, much in few words; be like someone who knows and at the same time keeps silent.
  Ecclesiastes 5:1-2 — wisdom parallel: Sirach recommends summing up much in few words and remaining capable of silence. Ecclesiastes likewise commands few words and opposes them to a fool's many words, specifically applying its restraint before God.

Sirach 32:9
Among dignitaries do not make yourself their equal, and while another speaks do not chatter much.
  Proverbs 25:6-7 — ethical parallel: Sirach forbids treating oneself as a dignitary's equal or chattering over another. Proverbs forbids self-promotion into rulers' places and prefers receiving an invitation to being publicly lowered.

Sirach 32:10
Lightning hastens before thunder, and grace will go ahead of a modest person.
  James 4:6 — ethical parallel: Sirach says grace goes before the modest person; James says God gives grace to the humble while opposing the proud. Both attach grace to humility rather than self-exaltation.

Sirach 32:11
Rise at the right hour and do not linger at the rear; hurry home and do not be slack.
  Proverbs 25:17 — social comparison: Sirach advises leaving the banquet at the proper hour and returning home; Proverbs advises limiting the frequency of visits before the friend grows weary. Both set bounds on a guest’s presence, though one concerns departure and the other frequency.

Sirach 32:12
There enjoy yourself and carry out your thoughts, but do not sin by a proud word.
  James 4:16 — ethical parallel: Sirach permits private enjoyment but prohibits sinning through a proud word. James expressly calls boastful pretension evil, matching the warning about arrogant speech rather than condemning enjoyment itself.

Sirach 32:13
For these things bless your Maker, who intoxicates you with His good things.
  Deuteronomy 8:10 — ethical parallel: Sirach tells the recipient of good things to bless the Maker. Deuteronomy commands blessing the Lord after eating and being filled from the good land He gave, joining enjoyment of provision to gratitude.

Sirach 32:14
The one who fears the Lord will receive instruction, and those who rise early will find favor.
  Proverbs 1:7 — wisdom parallel: Sirach links fear of the Lord with receiving instruction. Proverbs makes reverence the beginning of perception and contrasts it with the ungodly person's rejection of wisdom and discipline.

Sirach 32:15
The one who seeks the law will be filled with it, but the hypocrite will stumble in it.
  Psalms 1:1-3 — ethical parallel: Sirach commends seeking and being filled with the law rather than stumbling hypocritically in it. The psalm describes the person whose will and continual meditation are in the Lord's law as a fruitful, flourishing tree.

Sirach 32:16
Those who fear the Lord will find judgment and kindle ordinances like light.
  Psalms 118:105 — shared imagery: Sirach's God-fearing person kindles ordinances like light; the psalm calls God's word a lamp for the feet and a light for the path. Both portray divine instruction as illumination for conduct.

Sirach 32:17
A sinful person will turn aside a rebuke and find an interpretation according to his own will.
  Proverbs 9:7-9 — ethical parallel: Sirach's sinner turns rebuke aside and adapts interpretation to his own will. Proverbs contrasts the evil person's hostile response to correction with the wise person's loving and teachable response.

Sirach 32:18
A man of counsel will certainly not disregard a thought; an alien and proud person will not shrink from fear.
  Proverbs 12:15 — wisdom parallel: Sirach's person of counsel does not disregard a considered thought. Proverbs likewise marks the wise person by listening to counsel, in contrast with the fool who regards his own way as upright.

Sirach 32:19
Do nothing without counsel, and after doing it, do not regret it.
  Proverbs 15:22 — wisdom parallel: Sirach requires counsel before action to prevent later regret. Proverbs criticizes dishonoring councils and says counsel remains with deliberating people, valuing consultation in forming plans.

Sirach 32:20
Do not walk on a road of obstacles, and do not stumble among stones.
  Proverbs 4:26-27 — shared imagery: Sirach warns against an obstructed road and stumbling among stones. Proverbs instructs the learner to straighten tracks, direct ways rightly, and turn the foot from evil, using the traveled path to teach careful conduct.

Sirach 32:21
Do not trust a road free of stumbling blocks,
  1 Corinthians 10:12 — ethical comparison: Sirach cautions against trusting an apparently unobstructed path. Paul cautions the person who thinks he stands to take care against falling; both warn that apparent security does not remove the need for vigilance.

Sirach 32:22
and guard yourself even against your children.
  Micah 7:6 — ethical comparison: Sirach extends caution even to one's children. Micah describes children dishonoring parents and enemies arising within the household, giving a concrete reason why kinship alone does not guarantee safety.

Sirach 32:23
In every undertaking trust your soul, for this too is keeping commandments.
  Sirach 37:13-15 — literary parallel: Sirach 32 tells the learner to trust the soul in undertaking action. Chapter 37 similarly commends the heart's counsel and the soul's watchfulness, while adding petition to the Most High for a straight path in truth.

Sirach 32:24
The one who trusts the law attends to commandments, and the one who trusts the Lord will not be diminished.
  Psalms 118:165 — ethical parallel: Sirach joins trusting the law and attending to commandments with confidence in the Lord. The psalm describes lovers of God's law as having great peace and no stumbling block, likewise linking adherence to the law with stability.

Sirach 33:1
Evil will not meet the one who fears the Lord; even in testing he will be rescued again.
  Psalms 33:20 — ethical parallel: Sirach promises renewed rescue to the God-fearing person even during testing. The psalm acknowledges many afflictions of the righteous and promises the Lord's rescue from all, preserving the presence of trouble as well as deliverance.

Sirach 33:2
A wise man will not hate the law, but the one who acts hypocritically in it is like a ship in a storm.
  James 1:6-8 — shared imagery: Sirach compares hypocritical engagement with the law to a ship in a storm. James compares divided, doubtful faith to a wind-driven sea wave and calls it unstable; both use storm-tossed water to portray inner inconsistency.

Sirach 33:3
An understanding person will trust the law, and the law is trustworthy for him, like an answer from the sacred tokens.
  Exodus 28:30 — sacred judgment comparison: Sirach likens the law's trustworthiness to an answer from sacred tokens. Exodus places Manifestation and Truth on the oracle-piece of judgment carried by Aaron before the Lord, supplying the sacred judgment setting for that comparison.

Sirach 33:4
Prepare your speech, and so you will be heard; bind instruction together and answer.
  Proverbs 15:28 — wisdom parallel: Sirach requires preparing speech and gathering instruction before answering. Proverbs contrasts righteous hearts meditating on faithful things with impious mouths answering evil, similarly locating the quality of the answer in prior inward consideration.

Sirach 33:5
A fool’s inward parts are a cartwheel, and his deliberation is like a turning axle.
  Sirach 27:11 — shared imagery: Sirach 33 describes a fool's deliberation as a rotating axle and wheel; chapter 27 contrasts godly wisdom with a fool changing like the moon. Both recurring-motion images portray the fool's instability.

Sirach 33:7
Why does one day surpass another when all the light of the year’s days comes from the sun?
  Genesis 1:14 — creation comparison: Sirach's question about unequal days begins with their shared solar light. Genesis assigns the heavenly lights the marking of seasons, days, and years, providing the created calendar within which Sirach asks about sacred distinctions.

Sirach 33:8
By the Lord’s knowledge they were distinguished, and He differentiated seasons and festivals.
  Leviticus 23:2-4 — legal parallel: Sirach attributes differentiated seasons and festivals to the Lord's knowledge. Leviticus identifies the appointed feasts and holy convocations as the Lord's and commands their proclamation at proper times.

Sirach 33:9
Some He raised high and sanctified, and some He placed among the ordinary numbered days.
  Genesis 2:3 — creation parallel: Sirach says God sanctified certain days while leaving others ordinary. Genesis gives the particular example of God blessing and hallowing the seventh day after His creative work.

Sirach 33:10
All human beings are from the ground, and Adam was created from earth.
  Genesis 2:7 — creation parallel: Sirach expressly says Adam was created from earth and all humans come from the ground. Genesis narrates God forming the human from earthly dust and giving him the breath of life.

Sirach 33:11
In abundant knowledge the Lord distinguished them and differentiated their ways.
  Numbers 16:5 — ethical comparison: Sirach describes the Lord's knowledgeable distinction among human beings and their ways. Moses says God knows those who are His and holy and brings those He chooses near, a particular instance of divine differentiation.

Sirach 33:12
Some He blessed and raised high, some He sanctified and brought near Himself; some He cursed and brought low, and overturned them from their position.
  1 Samuel 2:7-8 — literary parallel: Sirach contrasts people God raises and brings near with people brought low. Hannah's song likewise says the Lord makes poor and rich, humbles and raises high, and lifts the needy to a throne of glory.

Sirach 33:13
Like a potter’s clay in his hand—all its ways are according to his pleasure—so are human beings in the hand of their Maker, for Him to repay them according to His judgment.
  Romans 9:20-21 — shared imagery: Both compare the Maker's authority over people with a potter's authority over clay. Sirach emphasizes repayment according to judgment; Romans illustrates differing vessels made from the same lump for honor or dishonor.

Sirach 33:14
Good stands opposite evil, and life opposite death; so a sinner stands opposite a godly person.
  Deuteronomy 30:15 — literary parallel: Sirach sets good against evil and life against death before contrasting sinner and godly person. Deuteronomy explicitly places the same two pairs, life and death and good and evil, before its hearers.

Sirach 33:15
Look in this way at all the Most High’s works: two by two, one opposite the other.
  Sirach 42:24-25 — literary parallel: Both passages describe the Most High's works as paired, one opposite another. Chapter 42 adds that nothing is deficient and that one confirms the other's good, elaborating the ordered pairing named in chapter 33.

Sirach 33:16
I too, the last, kept watch like someone gleaning behind grape-gatherers.
  Ecclesiastes 12:9-10 — authorial comparison: Within Sirach 33:16-18 the late gleaner image describes gathering instruction for others. Ecclesiastes likewise describes a wise teacher teaching the people knowledge and seeking truthful written words; the agricultural image belongs only to Sirach.

Sirach 33:17
By the Lord’s blessing I came in time, and like a grape-gatherer I filled a winepress.
  Ecclesiastes 12:9-11 — authorial comparison: Sirach's filled winepress continues its image of successfully gathered instruction, as clarified by 33:18. Ecclesiastes describes collected wise words, their ordered arrangement, and their gift from one shepherd; the comparison concerns the gathered teaching, not a literal harvest.

Sirach 33:18
Consider that I have labored not for myself alone, but for all who seek instruction.
  Ecclesiastes 12:9 — authorial parallel: Sirach says his labor serves everyone seeking instruction rather than himself alone. Ecclesiastes likewise describes the wise author teaching the people knowledge.

Sirach 33:19
Listen to me, dignitaries of the people; give ear, leaders of the assembly.
  Wisdom of Solomon 6:1-2 — literary parallel: Both summon public leaders to listen and learn. Sirach addresses dignitaries and assembly leaders; Wisdom addresses kings, judges, and rulers of multitudes with the same call to give ear.

Sirach 33:21
As long as you still live and breath is in you, do not exchange yourself for any flesh.
  1 Corinthians 7:23 — ethical comparison: Sirach warns the living person not to exchange himself for any human being. Paul tells those bought at a price not to become slaves of humans; the resistance to human subjection is shared, with Paul's distinct theological reason retained.

Sirach 33:22
For it is better that your children ask you than that you look toward your sons’ hands.
  2 Corinthians 12:14 — social parallel: Sirach prefers children depending on the parent to the parent depending on the children's hands. Paul likewise says parents should store up for children rather than children for parents, using that family expectation to explain his own conduct.

Sirach 33:24
On the day when your life’s days are completed, at the time of death, distribute your inheritance.
  Hebrews 9:16-17 — legal comparison: Sirach locates inheritance distribution at the completion of life. Hebrews explains that a testament takes effect at the maker's death and is not in force during his life, connecting inheritance arrangements to that same transition.

Sirach 33:25
Fodder, a rod, and burdens for a donkey; bread, discipline, and work for a household slave.
  Proverbs 26:3 — shared imagery: Sirach places the donkey's rod and burdens beside the household slave's discipline and work. Proverbs compares horse and donkey restraints with a rod for a lawless nation; both ancient disciplinary analogies transfer animal-control imagery to human settings without naming the same human group.

Sirach 33:27
A yoke and strap will bend a neck, and racks and tortures are for a wrongdoing household slave.
  Exodus 21:20-21 — legal comparison: Sirach prescribes coercive restraints and torture for a wrongdoing household slave. Exodus addresses a master's beating of an enslaved person with a rod and distinguishes liability by the outcome. The comparison records ancient treatment and legal limits rather than equating their prescriptions.

Sirach 33:28
Put him to work so that he is not idle, for idleness has taught much evil.
  1 Timothy 5:13 — ethical comparison: Sirach associates idleness with learning evil and therefore assigns work to the servant. Paul describes certain widows learning idleness and becoming gossips and busybodies; both link inactivity with learned misconduct, while addressing different groups.

Sirach 33:29
Assign him tasks as befits him, and if he does not obey, make his fetters heavy.
  Ephesians 6:9 — ethical contrast: Sirach tells the master to assign tasks and increase fetters for disobedience. Ephesians addresses masters with a contrasting restraint: give up threats, remembering the impartial heavenly Master. Their distinct approaches to coercion are preserved.

Sirach 33:30
Do not go to excess against any flesh, and do nothing without judgment.
  Colossians 4:1 — ethical parallel: Within household discipline Sirach prohibits excess against any person and requires judgment. Colossians likewise requires masters to provide what is just and equitable to enslaved people, grounding that duty in their own accountability to a heavenly Master.

Sirach 33:31
If you have a household slave, let him be as yourself, because you acquired him with blood.
  Philemon 1:16-17 — ethical comparison: Sirach tells the owner to regard the household slave as himself. Paul asks that Onesimus be received as a beloved brother and welcomed as Paul himself; both require personal or familial regard for the enslaved person, while giving distinct reasons.

Sirach 33:32
If you have a household slave, treat him as a brother, because you will need him as your own soul.
  Philemon 1:16 — ethical parallel: Sirach explicitly requires treating the household slave as a brother. Paul likewise asks that Onesimus be received as more than a slave, a beloved brother in the flesh and in the Lord, extending the familial description in a Christian setting.

Sirach 33:33
If you mistreat him and he leaves and runs away, on what road will you look for him?
  Deuteronomy 23:16-17 — legal comparison: Sirach warns that mistreatment can make an enslaved person flee beyond recovery. Deuteronomy addresses the person who escapes a master and forbids returning or oppressing him, viewing the same flight from the receiving community's protective obligation.

Sirach 34:1
Empty and false hopes belong to a man without understanding, and dreams give wings to fools.
  Ecclesiastes 5:2-6 — parallel dreams associated with folly and futility: Sirach joins foolish hopes with dreams; Ecclesiastes likewise connects dreams with distraction and futilities, concluding with fear of God.

Sirach 34:2
Like someone grasping a shadow and chasing wind is the one who relies on dreams.
  Zechariah 10:2 — parallel futility of relying on dreams: Sirach compares reliance on dreams with grasping a shadow; Zechariah calls false dreams vain comfort, without repeating the shadow image.

Sirach 34:4
What will be cleansed from something unclean? What will prove true from something false?
  Job 14:4 — close question about cleanness from uncleanness: Both ask how cleanness can arise from uncleanness; Sirach adds the analogous question about truth coming from falsehood.

Sirach 34:5
Divinations, omens, and dreams are vain, and the heart imagines like a woman in labor.
  Deuteronomy 18:10-14 — parallel rejection of divination and omens: Sirach calls divinations and omens vain; Deuteronomy forbids Israel to follow those practices. The laboring-woman image belongs to Sirach.

Sirach 34:6
Unless they are sent from the Most High in a visitation, do not give your heart to them.
  Numbers 12:6-8 — qualifying parallel of divinely given dreams: Sirach excepts dreams sent by the Most High from its warning; Numbers explicitly allows divine speech through prophetic sleep, distinguishing Moses's manner of revelation.

Sirach 34:7
For dreams have led many astray, and those who hoped in them fell away.
  Jeremiah 23:25-32 — close warning that false dreams lead people astray: Sirach says reliance on dreams has led many astray; Jeremiah describes unsent prophets leading God's people astray through false dreams.

Sirach 34:8
The law will be fulfilled without falsehood, and wisdom is fulfillment in a faithful mouth.
  Matthew 5:17-18 — parallel assurance of the law's fulfillment: Sirach says the law will be fulfilled without falsehood; Jesus likewise denies its abolition and affirms fulfillment, with His own role expressly stated.

Sirach 34:9
A man who has wandered has learned much, and one with much experience will recount understanding.
  Sirach 39:4 — related learning through travel and human experience: Sirach 34 associates wandering with acquired understanding; 39:4 likewise joins foreign travel with experience of good and evil among people.

Sirach 34:10
The one who has not been tested knows little, but the one who has wandered will increase in skill.
  Hebrews 5:8 — related learning through undergone experience: Sirach contrasts the untested person's limited knowledge with experience; Hebrews gives the distinct case of the Son learning obedience through suffering. Sirach concerns practical skill; Hebrews specifically names obedience.

Sirach 34:12
Often I was in danger to the point of death, and because of these things I was preserved.
  2 Corinthians 1:8-10 — parallel survival after danger approaching death: Both speakers recall danger approaching death and preservation. Paul expressly identifies God as deliverer; Sirach leaves the preserving agency unnamed within its account of experience.

Sirach 34:13
The spirit of those who fear the Lord will live, for their hope rests upon the One who saves them.
  Psalms 32:18-22 — close fear of God joined with saving hope: Sirach joins fear of the Lord, life and hope in the Savior; the psalm similarly joins fear, hope, rescue from death and divine help.

Sirach 34:14
The one who fears the Lord will fear nothing and certainly not be cowardly, because He Himself is his hope.
  Psalms 26:1-3 — close freedom from fear through divine protection: Both deny the need for fear because the Lord protects and saves; both explicitly place hope in Him amid danger.

Sirach 34:15
Happy is the soul of the one who fears the Lord! On whom does he rely? Who is his support?
  Psalms 111:1-8 — close blessedness of the God-fearing person: Sirach calls the God-fearing soul happy and asks about its support; the psalm calls that person blessed and describes a heart established in hope.

Sirach 34:16
The Lord’s eyes are upon those who love Him: a mighty shield and strong support, shelter from scorching heat and shelter from midday, protection from stumbling and help against falling,
  Psalms 32:18-20 — close divine eyes and protective support: Sirach places God's eyes upon those who love Him and calls Him shield and support; the psalm similarly joins His attentive eyes with help and defense.
  Psalms 120:3-6 — close protection from stumbling and scorching sun: Both combine protection of the person's footing with divine shelter against the sun's heat, beyond their general language of help.

Sirach 34:17
raising the soul and brightening the eyes, giving healing, life, and blessing.
  Psalms 102:1-5 — parallel divine healing and renewal of life: Sirach attributes healing, life and blessing to God; the psalm likewise blesses Him for healing, redeeming life and renewing youth. Brightened eyes are Sirach's additional image.

Sirach 34:18
One who sacrifices from unjust gain makes a blemished offering, and the gifts of the lawless bring no acceptance.
  Malachi 1:13-14 — close rejection of unjustly acquired and blemished offerings: Sirach calls sacrifice from unjust gain blemished and unacceptable; Malachi likewise questions acceptance of things seized by force and damaged sacrificial animals.

Sirach 34:19
The Most High takes no pleasure in the offerings of the ungodly, nor does He atone for sins through a multitude of sacrifices.
  Isaiah 1:11-16 — close rejection of multiplied sacrifices without repentance: Sirach denies that many sacrifices atone for the ungodly; Isaiah likewise rejects a multitude of offerings from bloodstained worshippers and commands cleansing from evil.

Sirach 34:20
The one who presents a sacrifice from the property of the poor is slaughtering a son before his father.
  Isaiah 1:11-15 — related sacrificial worship joined with bloodguilt: Sirach likens sacrifice from poor people's property to killing a son before his father; Isaiah also joins rejected sacrifice with blood-filled hands, without Sirach's particular comparison.

Sirach 34:21
The bread of the needy is the life of the poor; the one who deprives them of it is a man of blood.
  Job 24:9-10 — parallel taking life-sustaining food from the vulnerable: Sirach condemns depriving poor people of bread as bloodshed; Job likewise describes oppressors taking the hungry person's morsel, without that explicit homicide designation.

Sirach 34:22
The one who takes away a livelihood murders his neighbor, and the one who withholds a hired worker’s wage sheds blood.
  James 5:4-6 — close withheld wages linked with bloodguilt: Sirach calls withholding wages bloodshed; James likewise joins withheld harvesters' wages, their cries, and the charge of murdering the righteous.
  Leviticus 19:13 — legal counterpart prohibiting withheld wages: Sirach describes withholding wages as bloodshed; Leviticus supplies the concrete command against retaining a hired worker's wages overnight, alongside prohibitions of robbery and wronging a neighbor.

Sirach 34:23
One builds and another tears down; what have they gained beyond toil?
  Galatians 2:18 — related opposing acts of building and demolition: Sirach pictures one person's building undone by another; Paul pictures himself rebuilding what he demolished. Both use opposing construction acts negatively, for different stated reasons.

Sirach 34:24
One prays and another curses; whose voice will the Master hear?
  James 3:9-10 — related opposition between prayerful speech and cursing: Sirach questions the outcome when one prays and another curses; James rejects blessing God and cursing people from the same mouth. The number of speakers differs.

Sirach 34:25
Someone washes after contact with a corpse and then touches it again; what has he gained by his washing?
  Numbers 19:11-13 — legal background of corpse-contact impurity: Sirach's washing-and-retouching example depends on corpse contact causing impurity; Numbers explicitly prescribes purification after such contact, explaining the example's renewed defilement.

Sirach 34:26
So is a person who fasts over his sins and goes again to do the same things. Who will hear his prayer, and what has he gained by humbling himself?
  2 Peter 2:20-22 — close return to defilement after cleansing: Sirach compares repeated sin after fasting with renewed corpse defilement; Peter likewise condemns return after escape and uses the washed sow returning to mud.
  Isaiah 58:3-9 — close fasting contradicted by continuing injustice: Both question fasting's benefit when wrongful conduct continues; Isaiah specifies oppression and violence and connects corrected conduct with prayer being heard.

Sirach 35:1
The one who keeps the law multiplies offerings; the one who attends to commandments sacrifices a peace offering.
  1 Samuel 15:22 — related obedience valued in relation to sacrifice: Sirach describes keeping commandments as sacrificial offering; Samuel declares obedience and attentiveness better than sacrifice, expressing a different relationship between conduct and ritual.

Sirach 35:2
The one who repays kindness offers fine flour, and the one who gives alms sacrifices a praise offering.
  Hebrews 13:15-16 — close beneficence described as sacrifice: Sirach calls kindness and almsgiving offerings; Hebrews similarly calls doing good and sharing sacrifices pleasing to God, alongside sacrifice of praise.

Sirach 35:3
To withdraw from wickedness is the Lord’s good pleasure, and to withdraw from injustice is atonement.
  Ezekiel 18:21-23 — close repentance associated with forgiveness and divine pleasure: Sirach calls withdrawal from wickedness God's pleasure and withdrawal from injustice atonement; Ezekiel likewise joins turning from lawlessness with forgotten transgressions and God's desire for the person's life.

Sirach 35:4
Do not appear empty before the Lord, for all these things are for the commandment’s sake.
  Exodus 23:15 — close prohibition of appearing empty before God: Sirach repeats the requirement not to appear empty before the Lord; Exodus states that requirement within the prescribed feast observance.

Sirach 35:5
A righteous person’s offering fattens the altar, and its fragrance is before the Most High.
  Philippians 4:18 — close pleasing sacrifice and fragrant aroma: Sirach describes a righteous offering's fragrance before God; Paul likewise calls the Philippians' material gift a fragrant aroma and acceptable sacrifice.

Sirach 35:6
A righteous man’s sacrifice is acceptable, and its memorial will not be forgotten.
  Acts 10:2-4 — related righteous giving remembered before God: Sirach promises an acceptable offering an unforgettable memorial; the angel describes Cornelius's alms and prayers as a memorial ascending before God.

Sirach 35:7
Glorify the Lord with a generous eye, and do not diminish the firstfruits of your hands.
  Proverbs 3:9-10 — close honoring God with firstfruits: Both command honoring the Lord through firstfruits; Sirach emphasizes generous giving without diminishing them, while Proverbs specifies righteous labors and resulting abundance.

Sirach 35:8
With every gift make your face cheerful, and consecrate a tenth with gladness.
  2 Corinthians 9:7 — close cheerful disposition in giving: Sirach commands a cheerful face and gladness with gifts and tithes; Paul likewise commends cheerful giving without sorrow or compulsion, without specifying a tithe.
  Deuteronomy 14:22-26 — related tithe observance joined with rejoicing: Sirach specifically joins consecrating a tenth with gladness; Deuteronomy prescribes the tithe and its observance before God with the household's rejoicing.

Sirach 35:9
Give to the Most High according to His gift, with a generous eye according to what your hand has found.
  Deuteronomy 16:17 — close giving measured by God's gift and human ability: Both measure the worshipper's gift by what the hand can provide and the blessing already received from God.

Sirach 35:10
For the Lord repays, and He will repay you sevenfold.
  Proverbs 19:17 — parallel divine repayment of generosity: Both promise that God repays giving; Proverbs specifies mercy to the poor, while Sirach adds the sevenfold description.

Sirach 35:11
Do not bribe, for He will not accept it, and do not rely on an unjust sacrifice.
  Deuteronomy 10:17 — close divine refusal of bribes: Sirach forbids attempting a bribe because God will not accept it; Deuteronomy likewise explicitly denies that He takes bribes.

Sirach 35:12
For the Lord is judge, and with Him there is no prestige of persons.
  Deuteronomy 10:17-18 — close impartial divine judgment: Sirach calls the Lord a judge without regard to prestige; Deuteronomy likewise denies divine partiality and names His judgment for vulnerable people.

Sirach 35:13
He will not show partiality against the poor, and He will hear the petition of the wronged.
  Psalms 9:19 — parallel divine regard for the poor: Sirach promises an impartial hearing for the poor and wronged; the psalm likewise denies that the poor or the needy person's endurance will be forgotten.

Sirach 35:14
He will certainly not disregard an orphan’s supplication or a widow when she pours out her words.
  Exodus 22:21-23 — close divine hearing of widows and orphans: Both explicitly identify widows and orphans whose cries God hears, with Exodus warning those who afflict them.

Sirach 35:15
Do not a widow’s tears run down her cheek, and her cry rise against the one who brought them down?
  Exodus 22:21-23 — close widow's cry against her afflicter: Sirach makes the widow's cry rise against the cause of her tears; Exodus likewise joins afflicted widows' cries with God's response against the afflicters.

Sirach 35:16
The one who serves acceptably will be received, and his petition will reach as far as the clouds.
  Psalms 33:16-18 — parallel hearing of righteous supplication: Sirach says the acceptable servant's petition reaches the clouds; the psalm likewise places God's ears toward righteous supplication, without the cloud image.

Sirach 35:17
A humble person’s prayer has passed through the clouds, and until it draws near, he will certainly not be comforted.
  Luke 18:13-14 — related acceptance of humble prayer: Sirach describes the humble person's prayer drawing near to God; Jesus presents the humbled tax collector's plea as accepted, without using the cloud image.

Sirach 35:18
He will certainly not withdraw until the Most High visits, judges in favor of the righteous, and executes judgment.
  Luke 18:1-8 — close persistent petition until divine vindication: Sirach describes perseverance until God judges for the righteous; Jesus likewise urges persistent prayer and promises justice for those crying to God.

Sirach 35:19
The Lord will certainly not delay or be patient with them,
  Luke 18:7-8 — close assurance that divine justice will not delay: Both deny indefinite delay in God's response to the wronged petitioners; Luke explicitly promises speedy justice for His chosen ones.

Sirach 35:20
until He crushes the loins of the merciless and repays the nations with retribution,
  Deuteronomy 32:41-43 — parallel retribution against hostile powers: Sirach anticipates punishment of the merciless and repayment of nations; Deuteronomy likewise promises repayment against enemies and vengeance for God's people's blood.

Sirach 35:21
until He removes the multitude of the insolent and crushes the scepters of the unjust,
  Isaiah 14:4-6 — related breaking of oppressive rulers' power: Sirach describes crushed unjust scepters; Isaiah describes God shattering rulers' yoke in the lament over Babylon's king. The instruments of domination differ.

Sirach 35:22
until He repays a person according to his actions and judges human works according to their thoughts,
  Romans 2:5-8 — close repayment according to deeds: Both explicitly describe God repaying each person according to actions, within an account of divine judgment.
  Jeremiah 17:9-10 — close judgment of inward disposition and conduct: Sirach includes thoughts in the judgment of works; Jeremiah likewise joins examination of hearts with repayment according to ways and practices.

Sirach 35:23
until He judges His people’s case and gladdens them with His mercy.
  Deuteronomy 32:36 — close vindication and compassion for God's people: Sirach joins judgment of His people's case with mercy; Deuteronomy likewise says the Lord judges His people and is moved concerning His weakened servants.

Sirach 35:24
Mercy is timely in the season of His people’s affliction, like rain clouds in a season without rain.
  Hosea 6:1-3 — related rain image for restoring divine aid: Sirach compares timely mercy during affliction with rain in drought; Hosea describes the healing Lord's coming as seasonal rain, within an appeal to return to Him.

Sirach 36:1
Have mercy on us, Master, God of all; look upon us and cast Thy fear upon all the nations.
  Psalms 66:2-3 — parallel mercy leading to worldwide recognition: Sirach petitions for mercy and divine fear among nations; the psalm likewise petitions for compassion and blessing so God's way and salvation become known among nations.

Sirach 36:2
Raise Thy hand against foreign nations, and let them see Thy dominion.
  Psalms 78:10-11 — parallel visible divine power before nations: Sirach asks God to raise His hand so nations see His dominion; the psalm asks that vengeance be known among nations and invokes His great arm for rescue.

Sirach 36:3
Just as Thou wast sanctified in us before them, so mayest Thou be magnified in them before us.
  Ezekiel 36:23 — related divine sanctification before nations: Sirach links God's sanctification among His people with His magnification among nations; Ezekiel likewise connects sanctification in Israel before nations with recognition of the Lord.

Sirach 36:4
Let them know Thee, as we also have known that there is no God except Thee, Lord.
  Psalms 85:8-10 — close nations acknowledging the only God: Sirach asks nations to know that no God exists except the Lord; the psalm likewise joins all nations' worship with the affirmation that He alone is the great God.

Sirach 36:5
Renew signs and make wonders different; glorify Thy hand and right arm.
  Micah 7:15-17 — related divine wonders manifested before nations: Sirach asks for renewed signs and different wonders; Micah promises wonders like those of departure from Egypt, with nations seeing and fearing God.

Sirach 36:6
Rouse anger and pour out wrath; remove the adversary and destroy the enemy.
  Psalms 78:6-7 — close petition for wrath against hostile nations: Both petition God to pour out wrath upon hostile nations; the psalm specifies their devastation of Jacob as the cause.

Sirach 36:7
Hasten the time and remember the oath, and let them recount Thy mighty deeds.
  Luke 1:71-75 — related remembered oath and deliverance from enemies: Sirach asks God to remember the oath within a plea for deliverance; Zechariah likewise joins rescue from enemies with remembrance of the covenant and oath to Abraham.

Sirach 36:8
Let the survivor be consumed in fiery wrath, and let those who harm Thy people find destruction.
  Psalms 20:9-11 — close fiery wrath consuming enemies: Sirach asks for fiery destruction of those harming God's people; the psalm likewise describes enemies consumed by fire in the Lord's anger.

Sirach 36:9
Crush the heads of enemy rulers who say, “There is no one except us.”
  Psalms 67:22 — close crushing of enemies' heads: Both describe God crushing enemy heads; Sirach specifically identifies rulers asserting their own exclusiveness.

Sirach 36:10
Gather all the tribes of Jacob, and give them their inheritance as from the beginning.
  Deuteronomy 30:3-5 — close gathering Israel and restoring inherited land: Sirach asks for Jacob's tribes to be gathered into their inheritance; Deuteronomy promises gathering the dispersed and bringing them into their fathers' land.

Sirach 36:11
Have mercy, Lord, on the people called by Thy name, and on Israel, whom Thou didst liken to a firstborn.
  Exodus 4:22-23 — close Israel designated God's firstborn: Sirach appeals to God's designation of Israel as firstborn; Exodus records that exact designation in the demand for Israel's release.
  Daniel 9:18-19 — close appeal for the people bearing God's name: Both petitions appeal to God's mercy because His name has been called over His people; Daniel also explicitly names Israel and Zion.

Sirach 36:12
Have compassion on the city of Thy sanctuary, Jerusalem, the place of Thy rest.
  Psalms 131:13-14 — close Zion as God's chosen rest: Sirach calls Jerusalem God's sanctuary and resting place; the psalm likewise identifies chosen Zion as His enduring dwelling and rest.

Sirach 36:13
Fill Zion with the recounting of Thine excellence, and Thy people with Thy glory.
  Isaiah 60:1-3 — parallel divine glory upon Jerusalem: Sirach asks for Zion to be filled with recounting God's excellence and the people with His glory; Isaiah announces the Lord's glory appearing over Jerusalem and attracting nations.

Sirach 36:14
Give testimony to those Thou didst create in the beginning, and raise up the prophecies spoken in Thy name.
  Isaiah 44:24-26 — parallel establishment of prophetic words: Sirach asks God to uphold prophecies spoken in His name; Isaiah describes Him establishing His servant's words and making His messengers' counsel true.

Sirach 36:15
Give a reward to those who wait for Thee, and let Thy prophets be found trustworthy.
  Lamentations 3:25-26 — parallel divine goodness toward those who wait: Sirach petitions for a reward to those awaiting God; Lamentations likewise calls Him good to those waiting for Him and enduring for His salvation.
  Jeremiah 35:9 — related confirmation of a prophet's faithfulness: Sirach asks that God's prophets be found trustworthy; Jeremiah describes fulfillment of a peace prophecy as the means of recognizing a prophet sent in faithfulness by the Lord.

Sirach 36:16
Hear, Lord, the petition of Thy supplicants, according to Aaron’s blessing concerning Thy people,
  Numbers 6:22-26 — explicit reference to Aaron's blessing: Sirach appeals to Aaron's blessing concerning the people; Numbers records God's instruction to Aaron and his sons and the blessing they are to pronounce.

Sirach 36:17
and all those upon the earth will know that Thou art Lord, the God of the ages.
  Psalms 66:3-8 — parallel blessing resulting in worldwide divine recognition: Sirach expects all earth to know the Lord after the petition and blessing; the psalm similarly joins blessing with salvation known among nations and fear at earth's ends.

Sirach 36:18
The belly will eat every food, yet one food is better than another.
  1 Corinthians 6:13 — related food-and-stomach formulation with differing conclusions: Both join food and the stomach; Sirach distinguishes better foods, while Paul emphasizes their transience and the body's belonging to the Lord.

Sirach 36:19
The throat tastes food from the hunt; so an understanding heart tastes false words.
  Job 12:11 — close tasting food as an analogy for discerning words: Both compare bodily tasting with discernment of speech; Sirach names the understanding heart and false words, while Job names the discerning ear.

Sirach 36:21
A woman will accept any male, yet one daughter is better than another.
  Proverbs 31:29 — related comparison distinguishing one daughter from others: Sirach says one daughter is better than another; the husband's praise in Proverbs similarly distinguishes one woman above many accomplished daughters. Sirach's opening clause has no counterpart there.

Sirach 36:22
A woman’s beauty gladdens the face and surpasses every human desire.
  Proverbs 5:18-19 — related delight in one's wife: Sirach describes a woman's beauty gladdening the face; Proverbs likewise commends joy with one's wife and delight in her affection, without ranking beauty above every desire.
  Proverbs 31:30 — contrast in the assessment of beauty: Sirach celebrates beauty's power to gladden and attract; Proverbs calls beauty vain while praising understanding and fear of the Lord. Their emphases are distinct.

Sirach 36:23
If mercy and gentleness are upon her tongue, her husband is unlike ordinary human sons.
  Proverbs 31:28 — related merciful speech and a wife's benefit to her family: Sirach joins mercy and gentleness on a wife's tongue with her husband's distinction; Proverbs likewise joins wise speech and mercy with flourishing children and the husband's praise.

Sirach 36:24
The one who acquires a wife begins a possession: a helper corresponding to him and a pillar of rest.
  Genesis 2:18-24 — close wife as a corresponding helper: Sirach calls the acquired wife a helper corresponding to the husband; Genesis explicitly describes the making of that corresponding helper and the resulting marital union.

Sirach 36:27
So it is with a person who has no nest and lodges wherever evening overtakes him.
  Proverbs 27:8 — close nest imagery for the displaced person: Sirach describes the unsettled person as having no nest; Proverbs likewise compares estrangement from one's own place with a bird departing its nest, with a different stated consequence.

Sirach 37:1
Every friend will say, “I too have shown friendship,” but there is a friend who is a friend in name alone.
  Sirach 6:7-10 — parallel distinction between professed and dependable friendship: Sirach 37 contrasts friendship's name with its reality; the earlier passage likewise requires testing because some companions abandon their friend in affliction.

Sirach 37:2
Is there not grief to the point of death when a companion and friend turns to enmity?
  Psalms 54:13-15 — close pain caused by a companion's enmity: Sirach describes a friend's change to enmity as grievous; the psalm likewise distinguishes the painful betrayal of a close companion from an ordinary enemy's reproach.

Sirach 37:4
A companion enjoys his friend’s gladness but will stand against him in a time of affliction.
  Sirach 6:10-12 — close friend sharing prosperity but opposing the afflicted: Both describe a companion enjoying shared prosperity or meals who becomes hostile when the friend is brought low.

Sirach 37:6
Do not forget a friend in your soul or be unmindful of him in your wealth.
  Proverbs 27:10 — parallel refusal to abandon an established friend: Sirach warns against forgetting a friend in wealth; Proverbs likewise forbids abandoning one's friend, without limiting the warning to prosperity.

Sirach 37:7
Every counselor exalts counsel, but there is someone who counsels for himself.
  2 Samuel 15:2-6 — narrative example of self-interested counsel: Sirach warns that some counselors advise for themselves; Absalom's favorable assurances and proposal that he become judge win the people's hearts for himself.

Sirach 37:9
and say to you, “Your road is good,” then stand opposite to see what happens to you.
  Proverbs 26:24 — related favorable speech concealing hostile intent: Sirach's counselor praises a road but stands opposite to watch the outcome; Proverbs likewise describes an enemy agreeing with his lips while devising deceit within.

Sirach 37:10
Do not take counsel with someone who looks suspiciously at you, and hide your counsel from those who envy you.
  Proverbs 26:24-26 — related caution about an apparently friendly enemy: Sirach warns against confiding counsel to suspicious or envious people; Proverbs likewise warns against persuasion by an enemy's appealing speech and concealed hostility.

Sirach 37:12
Instead, remain with a godly man whom you know to keep commandments, whose soul is in accord with your soul, and who will share your pain if you stumble.
  Sirach 6:14-17 — close godliness and dependable friendship: Sirach 37 commends the godly companion who shares pain; Sirach 6 likewise links faithful, life-supporting friendship with fear of the Lord.

Sirach 37:13
Establish the counsel of your heart, for you have nothing more trustworthy than it.
  Proverbs 28:26 — contrast between inward counsel and rash confidence: Sirach commends one's heart's counsel amid advice about godly companionship and prayer; Proverbs specifically condemns trusting a rash heart and commends walking in wisdom. Rashness is not attributed to every inward judgment.

Sirach 37:15
Besides all these things, petition the Most High to make your road straight in truth.
  Psalms 142:8-10 — close prayer for a rightly directed path: Sirach commands petitioning God to straighten one's road in truth; the psalm similarly asks Him to show the way and lead the speaker on level ground.

Sirach 37:16
A word is the beginning of every work, and counsel precedes every action.
  Proverbs 31:4 — close counsel preceding action: Sirach says counsel precedes every action; Proverbs likewise directly instructs the hearer to do everything with counsel. Sirach also names the initiating word.

Sirach 37:17
The trace of a heart’s change brings forth four parts:
  Deuteronomy 30:15-19 — related fourfold moral alternatives explained by context: Sirach's four parts are named in 37:18 as good, evil, life and death; Deuteronomy presents those same four alternatives and connects a turning heart with their outcome.

Sirach 37:18
good and evil, life and death; the tongue continually rules them.
  Proverbs 18:21 — close life and death under the tongue's power: Sirach says the tongue rules good, evil, life and death; Proverbs likewise places life and death in the tongue's hand, using a closely related agency image.

Sirach 37:19
There is a skillful man who instructs many, yet is useless to his own soul.
  Romans 2:19-23 — close teacher failing to benefit himself: Sirach describes a capable instructor useless to his own soul; Paul likewise questions the teacher who instructs others while failing to teach himself and transgressing his instruction.

Sirach 37:20
There is someone who displays wisdom in words but is hated; this person will lack all food,
  Ecclesiastes 9:15-17 — contrast in socially rejected wisdom: Sirach describes a speaker displaying wisdom yet hated and lacking food; the next verse denies him wisdom. Ecclesiastes instead affirms the genuine wisdom of the poor man whose wisdom is despised and whose words are unheard.

Sirach 37:22
There is someone wise for his own soul, and the fruits of his understanding are trustworthy upon his mouth.
  Proverbs 12:14 — related understanding's fruit upon the mouth: Sirach links personal wisdom with trustworthy fruits of understanding upon the mouth; Proverbs likewise describes good filling a person's life from the mouth's fruits.

Sirach 37:23
A wise man will instruct his own people, and the fruits of his understanding are trustworthy.
  Ecclesiastes 12:9 — parallel wise teacher instructing the people: Sirach says the wise man instructs his people; Ecclesiastes is likewise described as wise and teaching the people knowledge. Sirach additionally calls the fruits trustworthy.

Sirach 37:24
A wise man will be filled with blessing, and all who see him will call him happy.
  Proverbs 3:13 — close blessedness associated with wisdom: Sirach calls the wise man blessed and recognized as happy; Proverbs likewise calls the person who finds wisdom blessed, without the observers' testimony.

Sirach 37:25
A man’s life is a number of days, but Israel’s days are beyond numbering.
  Jeremiah 38:36-37 — related contrast between mortal duration and enduring Israel: Sirach contrasts numbered human days with Israel's unnumbered days; Jeremiah grounds Israel's continuing nationhood in the enduring celestial order, rather than an individual's lifespan.

Sirach 37:26
The wise person will inherit trust among his people, and his name will live for ever.
  Psalms 111:6 — parallel enduring remembrance of the virtuous: Sirach promises the wise person's name enduring life among the people; the psalm similarly promises the righteous person an everlasting memorial.

Sirach 37:27
Child, in your life test your soul; see what is bad for it and do not give it that.
  Sirach 18:30-31 — parallel refusing desires harmful to the soul: Sirach 37 requires examining what harms one's soul and withholding it; Sirach 18 likewise commands restraint of appetites and warns against supplying every desire.

Sirach 37:28
For not everything benefits everyone, and not every soul takes pleasure in everything.
  1 Corinthians 10:23 — related limits of what is beneficial: Sirach denies that everything benefits everyone; Paul likewise distinguishes what is permitted from what benefits or builds up. Sirach also notes differing individual pleasures.

Sirach 37:29
Do not be insatiable in every luxury, and do not abandon yourself to dishes of food.
  Proverbs 23:1-3 — close warning against insatiable desire for food: Sirach forbids abandoning oneself to luxurious dishes; Proverbs likewise warns the insatiable diner not to desire the ruler's foods.

Sirach 37:30
For with much food there will be illness, and insatiability will approach the point of bilious vomiting.
  Proverbs 25:16 — close overeating followed by vomiting: Sirach links excessive food with illness and vomiting; Proverbs gives honey as the specific example, warning that fullness leads to vomiting.

Sirach 37:31
Many have died through insatiability, but the one who takes care will add life.
  Sirach 31:19-20 — parallel bodily benefit of restrained eating: Sirach 37 contrasts deadly insatiability with life-preserving care; Sirach 31 similarly contrasts a moderate stomach's healthy sleep with the insatiable person's physical distress.

Sirach 38:1
Honor a physician with his due honors, according to the need for him; for the Lord created him also.
  Colossians 4:14 — related honorable recognition of a physician: Sirach commands honoring the physician; Paul's greeting expressly calls Luke the beloved physician. The greeting positively identifies a physician without repeating the creation claim.

Sirach 38:2
For healing is from the Most High, and the physician will receive a gift from a king.
  Exodus 15:26 — close attribution of healing to God: Sirach says healing comes from the Most High; Exodus explicitly identifies the Lord as the people's healer. The physician's royal gift is additional in Sirach.

Sirach 38:3
A physician’s knowledge will raise his head, and he will be admired before dignitaries.
  Proverbs 22:29 — related professional skill recognized before rulers: Sirach says medical knowledge brings honor before dignitaries; Proverbs likewise places the perceptive worker beside kings, without specifying medicine.

Sirach 38:4
The Lord created medicines from the earth, and a prudent man will not reject them.
  2 Kings 20:5-7 — narrative example of a natural remedy within divine healing: Sirach commends medicines made from earth; Hezekiah's promised divine healing includes applying a cake of figs to his ulcer. The narrative gives one concrete remedy, not a general pharmacology.

Sirach 38:5
