Was not water sweetened by wood, so that its strength might be known?
  Exodus 15:23-25 — direct narrative counterpart of wood sweetening water: Sirach recalls water sweetened by wood; Exodus narrates Moses casting the divinely shown wood into Marah's bitter water and its becoming sweet.

Sirach 38:6
He Himself gave human beings knowledge, to be glorified in His wonderful works.
  Exodus 31:1-5 — parallel God-given knowledge for skilled works: Sirach attributes human knowledge to God in the context of useful works; Exodus expressly attributes Bezalel's knowledge and craftsmanship to divine empowerment, in sanctuary construction.

Sirach 38:7
By these He healed and removed the sufferer’s pain; with these a perfumer will make a mixture,
  Exodus 30:22-25 — close perfumer's preparation of a compound: Sirach mentions the perfumer making a mixture; Exodus explicitly describes a compound prepared by a perfumer's skill. Exodus's purpose is holy anointing oil, not the medicinal relief preceding Sirach's clause.

Sirach 38:9
Child, do not be negligent in your illness, but pray to the Lord, and He Himself will heal you.
  James 5:13-16 — close prayer for divine healing during illness: Sirach instructs the sick person to pray to the Lord for healing; James likewise prescribes prayer during sickness and attributes the person's restoration to the Lord.

Sirach 38:10
Put wrongdoing away, straighten your hands, and cleanse your heart from every sin.
  James 4:8 — close repentance involving hands and heart: Sirach joins putting away wrongdoing with straightened hands and a cleansed heart; James likewise calls sinners to cleanse hands and purify hearts.

Sirach 38:11
Give a fragrant offering and a memorial of fine flour, and make your offering rich, as one who is no more.
  Leviticus 2:1-3 — close fine-flour memorial and fragrant offering: Sirach commands a fragrant offering and memorial of fine flour; Leviticus prescribes fine flour, frankincense and a memorial portion offered as a sweet savor.

Sirach 38:12
Give the physician a place too, for the Lord created him; do not let him leave you, for you need him.
  Luke 5:31-32 — close recognition that sick people need a physician: Sirach insists that the physician remain because the patient needs him; Jesus likewise states sick people's need for a physician, using that fact to explain His call to sinners.

Sirach 38:14
for they themselves will petition the Lord to grant them success in relief and healing for the sake of preserving life.
  Acts 4:29-30 — related human petition for God to grant healing: Sirach describes physicians asking God for successful life-preserving healing; the believers in Acts likewise petition God to extend His hand for healing. Their roles and means differ.

Sirach 38:16
Child, shed tears over the dead and begin a lament as one suffering terribly. Lay out his body according to his due, and do not neglect his burial.
  Genesis 23:2-4 — narrative example of mourning joined with burial care: Sirach requires lament and due burial for the dead; Abraham likewise mourns Sarah and then seeks a burial possession for her.

Sirach 38:17
Make your weeping bitter and your lament fervent; mourn according to his worth for one day or two because of slander, and be comforted because of grief.
  Genesis 50:10 — related fervent mourning with a stated duration: Sirach prescribes fervent lament for one or two days; Joseph's mourning for his father is likewise intense and bounded, but lasts seven days and has a different stated setting.

Sirach 38:18
For death comes from grief, and grief of heart will bend strength.
  Proverbs 17:22 — close bodily weakening through grief: Sirach says grief bends strength and can bring death; Proverbs likewise says the sorrowful person's bones dry up, contrasting a rejoicing heart.

Sirach 38:20
Do not give your heart over to grief; put it away, remembering the last things.
  Sirach 30:21-23 — close refusal to surrender the heart to grief: Both command refusing grief and putting it away; Sirach 30 further commands comforting the heart and warns that grief destroys and brings no benefit.

Sirach 38:21
Do not forget: there is no return. You will not benefit this person, and you will harm yourself.
  2 Samuel 12:22-23 — close stopping futile mourning because the dead do not return: Sirach says continued grief cannot benefit the dead; David similarly asks why he should continue fasting when he cannot bring his dead child back.

Sirach 38:22
“Remember my judgment, for yours is likewise: yesterday for me, today for you.”
  Ecclesiastes 9:2-3 — parallel shared mortal destiny: The dead person's warning in Sirach says the hearer faces the same judgment; Ecclesiastes likewise says one event reaches everyone and that people go to the dead.

Sirach 38:23
When the dead person rests, bring his remembrance to rest, and be comforted concerning him when his spirit departs.
  2 Samuel 12:19-23 — narrative example of resuming life after bereavement: Sirach commands rest from mourning once the dead person rests; David rises, worships and eats after his child's death, explaining that the child cannot return.

Sirach 38:26
He will give his heart to making furrows, and his wakefulness to fodder for heifers.
  Isaiah 28:24-25 — related attention to the work of cultivation: Sirach describes the farmer's attention to furrows and fodder; Isaiah likewise attends to plowing and leveling land before sowing. Its sowing example does not repeat Sirach's fodder clause or judgment about scribal leisure.

Sirach 38:27
So also every craftsman and master-builder who spends the night like the day, those who engrave signet engravings: his persistence is in varying the design. He will give his heart to making the depiction lifelike, and his wakefulness to finishing the work.
  Exodus 28:9-12 — close skilled work of seal engraving: Sirach includes workers engraving signets among devoted craftsmen; Exodus specifies stoneworker's seal-engraving craftsmanship for the inscribed memorial stones, with a different design and sacred purpose.

Sirach 38:28
So also a smith sitting near an anvil and studying works of iron: the fire’s vapor will melt his flesh, and he will struggle in the furnace’s heat. He will incline his ear to the hammer’s sound, and his eyes are upon the pattern of the vessel. He will give his heart to completing works, and his wakefulness to finishing their adornment.
  Isaiah 54:16 — shared image of a smith working with fire: Sirach describes the smith laboring by furnace heat; Isaiah employs a smith blowing coals and producing an implement as a comparison in God's address. It does not identify Sirach's vessel with that implement.

Sirach 38:29
So also a potter sitting at his work and turning a wheel with his feet, who is continually anxious over his work, and whose whole labor is measured by number.
  Jeremiah 18:2-4 — related close observation of a working potter: Sirach describes a potter's anxious, continuous work at his wheel; Jeremiah observes a potter working upon stones and remaking a failed vessel. The details of the equipment remain distinct.

Sirach 38:30
With his arm he will shape clay, and before his feet he will bend his strength. He will give his heart to completing the coating, and his wakefulness to cleaning the kiln.
  Wisdom of Solomon 15:7 — close labor of shaping clay for service: Sirach depicts the potter exerting his arm and finishing clay work; Wisdom likewise describes laborious kneading and shaping of vessels, before its separate warning about making idols.

Sirach 38:31
All these trusted in their hands, and each shows skill in his own work.
  Exodus 35:30-35 — parallel recognition of skilled manual craftsmanship: Sirach recognizes each artisan's skill in his work; Exodus likewise enumerates skilled metal, stone, wood and textile work, explicitly attributing their abilities to God.

Sirach 38:33
Yet they will not be sought for the people’s council or rise high in the assembly; they will not sit on a judge’s chair or consider a covenant of judgment.
  Sirach 39:4 — literary contrast between artisans and the learned person before rulers: Sirach denies the described artisans high standing in the assembly and a judge's seat; the following portrait instead places the learned person among dignitaries and rulers. This is the author's contrast of social roles.

Sirach 38:34
They will certainly not make instruction and judgment known or be found among proverbs, but they will sustain the fabric of the world, and their petition is in the work of their craft.
  Sirach 39:1-3 — literary contrast between craft labor and study of instruction: Sirach distinguishes artisans sustaining the world from teachers of judgment and proverbs; the following portrait explicitly turns to study of law, ancient wisdom, prophecies and parables.

Sirach 39:1
Different is the one who gives his soul to considering the law of the Most High: he will seek out the wisdom of all the ancients and occupy himself with prophecies.
  Psalms 1:2 — close sustained meditation on the Lord's law: Sirach describes a person devoted to considering God's law; the psalm likewise describes continual meditation upon that law. Sirach further names ancient wisdom and prophecies.

Sirach 39:2
He will preserve the discourse of renowned men and enter into the turns of parables.
  Psalms 77:2-4 — related preservation of inherited discourse through parables: Sirach joins preserving renowned discourse with entering parables; the psalm likewise joins parables and ancient riddles with what the fathers recounted and transmission to another generation.

Sirach 39:3
He will seek out the hidden meanings of proverbs and dwell among the riddles of parables.
  Proverbs 1:5-6 — close understanding of proverbs and riddles: Both portray growing wisdom through understanding obscure sayings, wise utterances and riddles, matching Sirach's search into parables' hidden meanings.

Sirach 39:4
He will serve among dignitaries and appear before rulers. He will pass through the land of foreign nations, for he has experienced good and evil among human beings.
  Daniel 1:17-20 — narrative example of learned people serving before a king: Sirach places the experienced learned man before rulers; Daniel and his companions receive knowledge and understanding, are examined by the king and remain beside him.

Sirach 39:5
He will give his heart to rising early before the Lord his Maker and petition the Most High. He will open his mouth in prayer and petition concerning his sins.
  Psalms 5:2-4 — close morning petition before God: Sirach describes rising early to petition the Lord; the psalm likewise combines petition with standing before Him and being heard in the morning.

Sirach 39:6
If the great Lord is willing, he will be filled with a spirit of understanding. He himself will rain down words of his wisdom and give thanks to the Lord in prayer.
  1 Corinthians 12:8-11 — related divinely granted wisdom expressed in words: Sirach describes understanding granted if the Lord wills and resulting words of wisdom; Paul likewise describes words of wisdom and knowledge distributed through the Spirit as He wills.

Sirach 39:7
He will direct his counsel and knowledge rightly and consider His hidden things.
  Daniel 2:19-23 — related God-given understanding of hidden things: Sirach describes rightly directed knowledge considering God's hidden things; Daniel thanks God for wisdom and revelation of a mystery, expressly praising His disclosure of deep things.

Sirach 39:8
He will reveal the instruction of his teaching and boast in the law of the Lord’s covenant.
  Psalms 118:12-16 — parallel declaring divine instruction and delighting in it: Sirach's teacher reveals instruction and boasts in covenant law; the psalmist likewise declares God's judgments with his lips and delights in His testimonies.

Sirach 39:9

  Proverbs 6:25 — close warning against beauty capturing the eyes: Sirach forbids gazing at a person's beauty; Proverbs likewise warns against desire for beauty and capture through one's eyes, in a specific sexual warning. Sirach's broader wording and instruction about sitting among women are preserved.

Sirach 42:15
I will now remember the Lord’s works and recount what I have seen. By the Lord’s words are His works.
  Psalms 32:6-9 — close divine works accomplished by the Lord's word: Sirach introduces recollection of the Lord's works by saying they are through His words; the psalm likewise says the heavens were established by His word and creation came to be at His command.

Sirach 42:16
The shining sun has looked upon everything, and His work is full of the Lord’s glory.
  Psalms 18:2-7 — parallel sun and heavens displaying divine glory: Sirach joins the sun's comprehensive shining with the Lord's glory filling His work; the psalm likewise begins with heavens recounting God's glory and describes the sun's heaven-spanning course and heat.

Sirach 42:17
He has not enabled the Lord’s holy ones to recount all His wonders, which the Lord Almighty established so that everything might stand firm in His glory.
  Psalms 39:6 — close inability to enumerate all divine wonders: Sirach says even the holy ones cannot recount all the Lord's wonders; the psalm likewise speaks of announcing His wonders and finding them multiplied beyond number.

Sirach 42:18
He has traced out the abyss and the heart and considered their intricate workings. For the Most High knows all knowledge and has looked upon the sign of the age,
  Jeremiah 17:9-10 — close divine scrutiny of the heart's depth: Sirach says the Most High traces out the heart and its intricate workings; Jeremiah likewise calls the heart deep beyond all things and identifies the Lord as its examiner. Sirach also speaks of the abyss and universal knowledge.

Sirach 42:19
announcing what has passed and what will be, and uncovering the traces of hidden things.
  Isaiah 46:9-10 — close divine announcement of events before they occur: Sirach attributes announcement of past and future to God; Isaiah likewise identifies God as announcing last things before they came to be and bringing His counsel to fulfillment.
  Daniel 2:20-22 — close disclosure of hidden things by divine knowledge: Sirach says God uncovers traces of hidden things; Daniel's blessing likewise says He reveals deep and dark things and knows what is in darkness. This addresses the disclosure clause separately from the announcement of past and future.

Sirach 42:20
No thought has escaped Him; not even one word has been hidden from Him.
  Hebrews 4:12-13 — close divine discernment from which nothing is hidden: Sirach says no thought or word escapes God; Hebrews likewise describes God's word judging the heart's thoughts and intentions and denies that any creature is hidden before Him. The references to human speech and God's discerning word are distinct.

Sirach 42:21
He has set in order the great things of His wisdom, as He exists before the age and into the age. Nothing has been added or diminished, and He has had no need of any counselor.
  Isaiah 40:13-14 — close denial that God needs a counselor: Sirach says God has needed no counselor; Isaiah likewise asks who became His counselor, instructed Him or showed Him understanding.
  Ecclesiastes 3:14 — close permanence without addition or subtraction: Sirach says nothing is added or diminished in the ordering of divine wisdom; Ecclesiastes likewise says God's work endures forever and nothing can be added or taken from it.

Sirach 42:23
All these live and remain for ever for every need, and all obey.
  Psalms 148:5-8 — close enduring creation obeying divine command: Sirach says the works remain forever and all obey; the psalm likewise describes created things established for the age and earthly elements fulfilling God's word.

Sirach 42:24
All things are paired, one opposite another, and He has made nothing deficient.
  Sirach 33:14-15 — internal verbal parallel of paired opposites: Sirach 42:24 says all things are paired opposite one another; 33:14-15 likewise applies two-by-two opposition to the Most High's works, illustrating it with good and evil, life and death.

Sirach 42:25
One has confirmed the good things of another. Who will be filled with beholding His glory?
  Psalms 16:15 — related satisfaction in beholding divine glory: Sirach asks who will be filled by beholding God's glory; the psalmist confidently expects satisfaction when that glory appears.

Sirach 43:1
The pride of the heights is the clear firmament, the appearance of heaven in a vision of glory.
  Psalms 18:2 — close firmament displaying divine glory: Sirach presents the clear firmament and heavenly appearance as a vision of glory; the psalm likewise says the heavens recount God's glory and the firmament announces His handiwork.

Sirach 43:2

  Genesis 1:16-18 — creation background for the sun's appointed work: Sirach calls the rising sun a wondrous instrument made by the Most High; Genesis likewise describes God making and setting the greater light to rule the day and shine upon earth. The sun's announcement is Sirach's personification.

Sirach 43:3
At its noon it dries out the land; who will withstand its burning heat?
  Psalms 18:7 — close inescapable heat of the sun: Sirach asks who can withstand the sun's burning heat; the psalm likewise says no one is hidden from its heat as it crosses heaven.

Sirach 43:5
Great is the Lord who made it, and by His words it hastened on its course.
  Psalms 148:3-6 — parallel heavenly lights made and ordered by divine command: Sirach praises the Lord who made the sun and directs its course by His words; the psalm likewise summons the sun among the heavenly lights created and established by God's command.

Sirach 43:6
The moon too is always there at its appointed time, a display of seasons and a sign of the age.
  Psalms 103:19 — close moon appointed for seasons: Sirach describes the moon's appointed timing and display of seasons; the psalm likewise says God made the moon for seasons.

  Numbers 10:10 — related lunar timing of religious observance: Sirach connects the moon with the sign of a festival; Numbers places new moons alongside feasts among the occasions for offerings and trumpet remembrance. The moon's diminishing course is additional.

Sirach 43:9
The beauty of heaven is the glory of the stars, an ornament shining in the Lord’s heights.
  Psalms 8:2-4 — parallel contemplation of the glorious starry heavens: Sirach calls the stars heaven's shining ornament; the psalm likewise joins praise of divine magnificence above heaven with contemplation of the moon and stars founded by God's fingers.

Sirach 43:10
At the Holy One’s words they will stand according to His judgment and will never grow slack in their watches.
  Psalms 148:3-6 — parallel stars standing under enduring divine command: Sirach says the stars keep their watches at the Holy One's words; the psalm likewise includes stars among the works created and permanently established under His command. It does not use the watchkeeping image.

Sirach 43:11
Look at the rainbow and bless the One who made it, exceedingly beautiful in its radiance.
  Genesis 9:12-16 — creation-and-covenant counterpart to the rainbow: Sirach bids the observer bless the rainbow's Maker; Genesis likewise presents God setting His bow in the cloud, there as a covenant sign. Sirach emphasizes its beauty rather than rehearsing the covenant.

Sirach 43:12
It encircled heaven with a ring of glory; the hands of the Most High stretched it out.
  Ezekiel 1:28 — close encircling radiance of rainbow and glory: Sirach calls the rainbow a ring of glory around heaven; Ezekiel compares surrounding brightness to a bow in a rainy cloud and identifies the vision with the likeness of the Lord's glory.

Sirach 43:13
By His command He hastened the snow and speeds the lightning of His judgment.
  Job 37:5-6 — close snow brought by divine command: Sirach says God hastens snow by His command; Elihu likewise describes God commanding snow to come upon earth. Sirach additionally specifies the lightning of judgment.

Sirach 43:14
Therefore the storehouses were opened, and clouds flew out like birds.
  Job 38:22-23 — close storehouse image for heavenly weather: Sirach's opened storehouses stand between references to snow and hail; the Lord's speech in Job likewise describes stored treasures of snow and hail reserved for a time of battle. Sirach's flying-cloud comparison is additional.

Sirach 43:16
At His appearing the mountains will be shaken; at His will the south wind will blow.
  Psalms 17:8-10 — close mountains shaken at divine approach: Sirach says mountains shake at God's appearing; the psalm likewise describes shaken mountain foundations and earth as God bends heaven and descends. Sirach's south wind is an additional detail.

Sirach 43:17
The sound of His thunder rebuked the earth, as did the north wind’s storm and the whirling of wind.
  Psalms 28:3-8 — close divine thunder-voice shaking the land: Sirach attributes earth's rebuke to God's thunder; the psalm likewise joins the God of glory's thunder with His powerful voice shaking the wilderness. The particular winds are not repeated there.

Sirach 43:18
Like birds flying down, He sprinkles snow; like a settling locust is its descent. The eye will marvel at the beauty of its whiteness, and the heart will be astounded by its falling shower.
  Psalms 147:5 — parallel figurative description of God-given snow: Sirach compares descending snow to birds and a settling locust; the psalm likewise describes God giving snow through a comparison, to wool. The physical images differ rather than describing the same motion.

Sirach 43:19
He pours frost upon the earth like salt, and when frozen it becomes points of thorns.
  Job 37:10 — close frost attributed to divine action: Sirach says God pours frost upon earth; Elihu likewise says frost is given from the Strong One's breath, alongside His direction of water. Salt and thorn points are Sirach's additional images.

Sirach 43:20
A cold north wind will blow, and ice will freeze upon water. It will settle upon every gathering of water, and the water will put it on like a breastplate.
  Job 38:29-30 — related ice and the fixed surface of the abyss: Sirach describes ice settling over gathered water like a breastplate; God's questions in Job likewise concern ice's origin and who fixed the abyss's face. The north wind and armor image belong to Sirach.

Sirach 43:22
A mist quickly brings healing to everything; dew arriving from the scorching heat will bring gladness.
  Hosea 14:5-7 — related healing and renewed growth through dew: Sirach describes healing mist and gladness-bringing dew; Hosea likewise joins healing with God becoming dew to Israel, which blossoms and spreads branches. Physical weather becomes an image of Israel's restoration there.

Sirach 43:23
By His purpose He stilled the abyss and planted islands in it.
  Job 26:12 — close divine calming of the deep: Sirach says God stilled the abyss by His purpose; Job likewise says He calmed the sea by strength. Sirach's planting of islands is additional.

Sirach 43:24
Those who sail the sea recount its danger, and we marvel at what our ears hear.
  Psalms 106:23-32 — close sailors' testimony to danger and divine wonders: Sirach recounts wonder at sailors' reports of sea danger; the psalm likewise describes sailors seeing divine wonders and facing a terrifying storm, then calls for public praise for their rescue.

Sirach 43:25
There too are strange things and wondrous works, the variety of every living creature, the creation of sea monsters.
  Psalms 103:25-26 — close sea teeming with creatures and a sea monster: Sirach names the sea's varied living creatures and sea monsters among wonders; the psalm likewise describes countless small and great creatures and a dragon formed by God in the sea.

Sirach 43:26
Because of Him His messenger succeeds, and by His word all things hold together.
  Hebrews 1:2-3 — close all things sustained by divine word: Sirach says all things hold together by God's word; Hebrews likewise attributes upholding all things by the word of His power to the Son through whom God made the ages. Sirach 43:27
We will say much and still never reach the end. The conclusion of our words is this: He is everything.
  Romans 11:33-36 — related comprehensive conclusion to praise beyond full understanding: Sirach says speech cannot reach the end and concludes that God is everything; Romans likewise praises unsearchable divine wisdom and concludes that all things are from, through and to Him. The comprehensive praise is shared while the concluding formulations differ.

Sirach 43:28

  Wisdom of Solomon 13:3-4 — close Maker surpassing the beauty and power of His works: Sirach says God is greater than all His works; Wisdom likewise argues that the Master and Maker is better and more powerful than the beautiful and mighty things He created.

Sirach 43:29
The Lord is fearsome and exceedingly great, and His power is wondrous.
  Psalms 95:3-5 — close praise of the great and fearsome Creator: Sirach calls the Lord fearsome, exceedingly great and wondrous in power; the psalm likewise announces His wonders, calls Him great and fearsome, and identifies Him as Maker of the heavens.

Sirach 43:30
As you glorify the Lord, exalt Him as much as you can, for He will surpass even that. As you exalt Him, increase your strength; do not grow weary, for you will never reach the end.
  Nehemiah 9:5 — close exaltation beyond the measure of blessing and praise: Sirach urges the greatest possible glorification while saying God will surpass it; Nehemiah likewise calls for blessing the glorious divine name and exalting it above all blessing and praise.

Sirach 43:31
Who has seen Him and will recount Him? Who will magnify Him as He is?
  John 1:18 — related limit of human sight and recounting of God: Sirach asks who has seen God and can recount Him as He is; John likewise says no one has seen God, then identifies the only-begotten God as the One who makes Him known. John's specified revelation supplies a distinct development.

Sirach 43:32
Many hidden things are greater than these, for we have seen few of His works.

Sirach 43:33
For the Lord made all things, and He gave wisdom to the godly.
  Proverbs 2:6-8 — close wisdom given by God with care for the upright: Sirach says the Creator gives wisdom to the godly; Proverbs likewise says the Lord gives wisdom and guards those acting rightly and revering Him. The creation-of-all clause is not repeated there.

Sirach 44:1
Let us now praise glorious men, our fathers according to their generations.
  Hebrews 11:32-39 — parallel commemoration of earlier generations' notable lives: Sirach introduces praise of glorious forefathers; Hebrews likewise recalls judges, kings, prophets and others who received testimony through faith. The catalogues have distinct selections and emphases.

Sirach 44:3
There were those who ruled in their kingdoms, men renowned for power; those who counseled with their understanding, who made declarations in prophecies;
  1 Kings 3:28 — historical example of a renowned ruler's understanding: Sirach includes rulers and men of understanding among the praised ancestors; Israel's recognition of divine discernment in Solomon's judgment supplies a concrete example of wise rulership, not an identification of the entire list with him.

Sirach 44:4
leaders of the people in deliberations and in understanding the people’s writings, wise words being part of their instruction;
  Ezra 7:6-10 — historical example of learned instruction from sacred writings: Sirach praises leaders who understand writings and instruct wisely; Esdras is described as a ready scribe in Moses's law who seeks, practices and teaches its ordinances. He illustrates that activity without being named by Sirach here.

Sirach 44:5
those who sought out musical melodies and related verses in writing;
  1 Kings 5:12 — historical example of an ancestor's songs and sayings: Sirach recalls makers of melodies and written verses; Kings records Solomon's three thousand parables and five thousand songs. The target attests his compositions without specifying their writing process.

Sirach 44:6
wealthy men furnished with strength, living peacefully in their dwellings.
  1 Kings 5:1-4 — historical example of abundance, power and peace: Sirach includes powerful wealthy men living peacefully; Kings portrays Solomon's ample daily provisions, rule beyond the River and peace on every side. It illustrates the description without identifying all such men as Solomon.

Sirach 44:7
All these were honored in their generations and were a source of boasting in their days.
  Sirach 39:9-10 — internal parallel of honored understanding and public praise: Sirach 44:7 honors earlier people in their generations; 39:9-10 likewise promises lasting praise and public celebration to the person of understanding, developing a form of remembrance later applied to the ancestors.

Sirach 44:8
Some of them left a name, so that their praises might be recounted.

  Ecclesiastes 1:11 — close absence of remembrance across generations: Sirach describes people and descendants with no remembrance, as though they had not existed; Ecclesiastes likewise denies remembrance of earlier and later generations among those who follow.

Sirach 44:10

Sirach 44:11

  Proverbs 20:7 — close righteous conduct leaving children blessed: Sirach describes the merciful ancestors' offspring as a good inheritance; Proverbs likewise says a person living blamelessly in righteousness leaves children blessed.

Sirach 44:12

  Genesis 17:7-9 — covenant example of blessing extended to an ancestor's offspring: Sirach says the ancestors' offspring stood within covenants because of them; God establishes an everlasting covenant with Abraham and his descendants and commands its preservation through their generations.

Sirach 44:13

  Psalms 111:2-3 — parallel conjunction of blessed descendants and enduring righteousness: Sirach promises lasting offspring and unobliterated glory; the psalm likewise joins a strong seed and blessed upright generation with household glory and righteousness that remains forever. Its permanence clause concerns righteousness.

Sirach 44:14
Their bodies were buried in peace, and their name lives through generations.
  Isaiah 57:1-2 — close peaceful burial of the righteous: Sirach says the ancestors' bodies were buried in peace; Isaiah likewise says the righteous person's burial will be in peace. The lasting name is an additional Sirach theme.

Sirach 44:15
Peoples will recount their wisdom, and the assembly proclaims their praise.
  Sirach 39:10 — internal verbal repetition of wisdom recounted and praise proclaimed: Both passages say peoples or nations recount wisdom and the assembly proclaims praise; the earlier learned person's description becomes language for the honored ancestors.

Sirach 44:16
Enoch pleased the Lord and was taken away, an example of repentance to the generations.
  Genesis 5:22-24 — explicit Enoch narrative background: Both say Enoch pleased God and was taken or transferred away. Sirach's characterization of him as an example of repentance is additional to Genesis's account.

Sirach 44:17
Noah was found perfect and righteous; in a time of wrath he became an exchange. Through him a remnant was left to the earth when the flood came.
  Genesis 6:9 — explicit Noah narrative background for righteousness: Sirach calls Noah perfect and righteous; Genesis likewise names him righteous and blameless in his generation and says he pleased God. The later flood and the term exchange are not explained by this single target unit.
  Genesis 7:21-23 — explicit Noah narrative background for the surviving remnant: Sirach says a remnant remained through Noah when the flood came; Genesis likewise contrasts the destruction of living flesh with Noah and those with him remaining in the ark.

Sirach 44:18
Everlasting covenants were established with him, so that all flesh might not be wiped out by a flood.
  Genesis 9:8-17 — explicit Noahic covenant against another universal flood: Sirach recalls enduring covenants so that all flesh would not be wiped out by a flood; Genesis establishes the covenant with Noah, his seed and living creatures and promises that floodwaters will not again destroy all flesh.

Sirach 44:19
Abraham was a great father of a multitude of nations, and no one like him was found in glory.
  Genesis 17:4-6 — explicit Abrahamic fatherhood of many nations: Sirach calls Abraham a great father of a multitude of nations; Genesis uses the same many-nations promise in establishing his name and covenant. Sirach adds its comparison of his glory with others.

Sirach 44:20
He kept the law of the Most High and entered a covenant with Him. He established a covenant in his flesh, and under testing he was found faithful.
  Genesis 17:9-14 — explicit covenant in Abraham's flesh: Sirach says Abraham entered God's covenant and established it in his flesh; Genesis commands circumcision and explicitly calls it God's covenant upon the flesh.
  Genesis 22:1-12 — explicit Abrahamic testing and faithful response: Sirach says Abraham was found faithful under testing; Genesis explicitly introduces God's test and concludes the threatened offering with acknowledgment that Abraham fears God and has not withheld his beloved son.

Sirach 44:21
Therefore He assured him by an oath that nations would be blessed in his offspring, that He would multiply him like the dust of the earth, raise his offspring like the stars, and give them an inheritance from sea to sea and from the River to the end of the earth.
  Genesis 22:15-18 — explicit oath, stars and blessing of nations through Abraham's seed: Sirach combines the divine oath, multiplied offspring like stars and blessing of nations; Genesis joins these same elements in the oath following Abraham's test. Its additional multiplication image is sand by the sea.
  Genesis 13:14-17 — parallel land promise and innumerable earthly particles: Sirach recalls multiplied offspring like earth's dust and an inheritance; Genesis likewise promises land and offspring as uncountable as the sand of the earth. The supplied texts use dust and sand respectively.
  Psalms 71:8 — close sea-to-sea and river-to-world-end territorial formula: Sirach describes the promised inheritance from sea to sea and from the River to earth's end; the royal psalm uses the same territorial pattern for the king's rule. Abrahamic inheritance and royal dominion remain distinct applications.

Sirach 44:22
In Isaac He established it in the same way, because of Abraham his father.
  Genesis 26:3-5 — explicit renewal of Abraham's promise for Isaac: Sirach says the promise was established in Isaac because of Abraham; God likewise promises Isaac the land, multiplied seed and blessing of nations, expressly because Abraham obeyed Him.

Sirach 44:23
He caused the blessing of all people and the covenant to rest upon Jacob’s head. He acknowledged him with His blessings and gave him an inheritance. He distinguished his portions and divided them among twelve tribes.
  Genesis 35:9-12 — explicit blessing and inheritance given to Jacob: Sirach recalls blessing and inheritance resting on Jacob; Genesis likewise describes God blessing Jacob, naming him Israel and giving him and his descendants the land promised to Abraham and Isaac.
  Joshua 14:1-5 — historical counterpart to tribal division of the inheritance: Sirach recalls the division of portions among tribes; Joshua likewise describes tribal leaders allotting the inheritance and the people dividing the land as commanded. Its account specifies particular tribal arrangements rather than repeating Sirach's number twelve.

Sirach 45:1
From him He brought forth a man of mercy who found favor in the eyes of all flesh, beloved by God and people: Moses, whose remembrance is among blessings.
  Exodus 11:3 — explicit public honor of Moses: Sirach calls Moses favored and beloved among people; Exodus likewise says Moses became very great in the sight of the Egyptians, Pharaoh and his attendants. This particular public recognition is not expanded into a statement about every individual.

Sirach 45:2
He made him like the holy ones in glory and magnified him through the fears of his enemies.
  Exodus 34:29-30 — explicit Moses narrative background for glorification: Sirach says God made Moses glorious; Exodus describes the glorified appearance of his face after speaking with God. The people afraid there are Aaron and Israel's elders, while Sirach separately mentions enemies' fears.

Sirach 45:3
By his words He caused signs to cease; He glorified him before kings. He gave him commandments for His people and showed him something of His glory.
  Exodus 8:8-11 — explicit signs ceasing at Moses's words: Sirach says signs ceased through Moses's words; Exodus recounts Moses crying to the Lord concerning the frogs and the Lord doing as he said, bringing relief from that plague.
  Exodus 24:12 — explicit commandments entrusted to Moses: Sirach says God gave Moses commandments for His people; Exodus likewise promises him the written tablets, law and commandments. This addresses the law-giving clause separately from the cessation of signs and the sight of glory.
  Exodus 33:18-23 — explicit limited disclosure of divine glory to Moses: Sirach says Moses was shown something of God's glory; Exodus records his request to see it, God's passing glory, and the limits placed on what he could see.

Sirach 45:4
In faithfulness and gentleness He sanctified him; He chose him out of all flesh.
  Numbers 12:3-8 — close Moses described as both meek and faithful: Sirach joins Moses's gentleness and faithfulness with his distinctive selection; Numbers likewise calls him exceptionally meek and faithful in God's house, distinguishing his access to God from ordinary prophetic visions.

Sirach 45:5
He let him hear His voice, brought him into the darkness, and gave him commandments face to face, a law of life and knowledge, to teach Jacob the covenant and Israel His judgments.
  Exodus 20:18-21 — explicit Moses entering the darkness where God was: Sirach says God brought Moses into darkness; Exodus likewise contrasts the people standing far off with Moses entering the thick darkness where God was.
  Exodus 33:11 — explicit face-to-face speech with Moses: Sirach recalls divine speech and commandments face to face; Exodus explicitly says the Lord spoke to Moses face to face, as to a friend. That verse does not narrate the entire law-giving.
  Deuteronomy 32:44-47 — close Mosaic instruction described as life: Sirach calls Moses's instruction a law of life for Israel; Moses likewise commands Israel to preserve and teach the words of the law and says this word is their life.

Sirach 45:6
He exalted Aaron, a holy man like him, his brother from the tribe of Levi.
  Exodus 28:1 — explicit selection of Aaron, Moses's brother, for priesthood: Sirach says God exalted Moses's holy brother Aaron; Exodus commands bringing Aaron the brother and his sons forward to serve as priests. The tribe of Levi is an additional identification in Sirach, also confirmed in the separately read Numbers 18:1-2.

Sirach 45:7
He established him as an everlasting covenant and gave him the priesthood of the people. He blessed him with beautiful attire and girded him with a robe of glory.
  Exodus 29:8-9 — explicit enduring priesthood of Aaron and his sons: Sirach describes an everlasting covenant granting Aaron priesthood; Exodus likewise gives Aaron and his sons priesthood forever and orders their consecration.
  Exodus 28:2-4 — close holy priestly attire for honor and glory: Sirach says Aaron was blessed with beautiful attire and a robe of glory; Exodus likewise orders his holy robe for honor and glory and lists the priestly garments.

Sirach 45:8
He clothed him with the fullness of splendor and equipped him with mighty garments: breeches, a robe reaching to the feet, and a shoulder piece.
  Exodus 28:4-6 — explicit full-length robe and shoulder-piece: Sirach's list of Aaron's splendid garments includes a full-length robe and shoulder-piece; Exodus lists those garments and their crafted materials. The breeches are addressed in a separate target.
  Exodus 28:42-43 — explicit priestly linen undergarments: Sirach includes breeches among Aaron's garments; Exodus prescribes linen undergarments for Aaron and his sons to cover the flesh from waist to thighs during sanctuary service.

Sirach 45:9
He surrounded him with little pomegranates, with many golden bells all around, to sound with his footsteps and make an audible ringing in the sanctuary as a remembrance for the sons of His people;
  Exodus 28:33-35 — explicit pomegranate-and-bell hem with audible sanctuary service: Both describe pomegranates and bells around the robe and their sound in the sanctuary. Sirach specifies golden bells; the supplied Exodus wording explicitly mentions gold pomegranates and bells between them.

Sirach 45:10
with a holy robe of gold, blue, and purple, the work of an embroiderer; with an oracle-piece of judgment, tokens of truth, twisted scarlet, a craftsman’s work;
  Exodus 28:15-16 — explicit embroidered oracle-piece of judgment: Sirach's oracle-piece and colored precious fabric correspond to Exodus's embroidered oracle-piece of judgments, made with gold, blue, purple, scarlet and fine linen.
  Exodus 28:30 — explicit truth associated with the oracle-piece: Sirach includes tokens of truth with the oracle-piece of judgment; Exodus explicitly places Manifestation and Truth upon that oracle-piece, borne by Aaron before the Lord.

Sirach 45:11
with costly stones bearing engraved seals in settings of gold, a stoneworker’s work, as a remembrance in carved writing according to the number of Israel’s tribes;
  Exodus 28:17-29 — explicit engraved tribal stones in gold as a memorial: Sirach's precious stones, seal-like engravings, gold settings and tribal remembrance correspond to Exodus's twelve named tribal stones and Aaron bearing the names as a memorial. The target follows the actual native units, including the numbering gap before 28:29.

Sirach 45:12
with a golden crown upon the turban, the impression of a seal of holiness, an honor to boast of, a mighty work, beautifully adorned things desired by the eyes.
  Exodus 28:36-38 — explicit gold head ornament with a holy seal inscription: Sirach recalls a gold crown upon the turban and a seal of holiness; Exodus prescribes a gold plate stamped like a seal with The Lord's consecrated thing and fastened to the headband. Crown and plate are the respective descriptions.

Sirach 45:13
Before him there had never been such beautiful things. No outsider has ever worn them, but only his sons and his descendants continually.
  Exodus 29:29-30 — explicit Aaronic succession in the holy garments: Sirach reserves the garments to Aaron's sons and descendants; Exodus likewise gives Aaron's holy robe to his sons and to the priest succeeding him from among them. It does not repeat Sirach's retrospective claim that no outsider ever wore them.

Sirach 45:14
His sacrifices will be wholly offered twice every day, continually.
  Exodus 29:38-42 — explicit twice-daily continual offering: Sirach describes sacrifices wholly offered twice each day continually; Exodus prescribes two lambs each day, morning and evening, as a continual offering through the generations.

Sirach 45:15
Moses filled his hands and anointed him with holy oil. It became an everlasting covenant for him and his offspring, throughout the days of heaven, to minister to Him, serve as priests, and bless His people in the Name.
  Exodus 29:7-9 — explicit anointing and consecration of priestly hands: Sirach recalls oil anointing, filled hands and enduring priesthood; Exodus commands oil poured on Aaron's head, consecration of his and his sons' hands, and priesthood forever.
  Numbers 6:22-26 — explicit Aaronic blessing with the divine Name: Sirach includes blessing God's people in the Name among Aaron's duties; Numbers commands Moses to instruct Aaron and his sons in blessing Israel and placing God's name upon them. The name clause is within native 6:23.

Sirach 45:16
He chose him from every living person to present an offering to the Lord, incense and a pleasing fragrance as a remembrance, to make atonement for your people.
  Numbers 17:11-13 — explicit Aaronic incense making atonement for the people: Sirach describes Aaron's incense and atoning ministry; Numbers narrates Aaron carrying incense into the outbreak, making atonement and standing between dead and living as the outbreak subsides.

Sirach 45:17
In His commandments He gave him authority concerning covenants of judgments, to teach Jacob the testimonies and enlighten Israel through His law.
  Deuteronomy 33:8-10 — close Levitical teaching of Jacob's ordinances and Israel's law: Sirach describes Aaron teaching Jacob testimonies and enlightening Israel through law; Moses's blessing of Levi likewise commissions declaration of ordinances to Jacob and law to Israel, alongside incense ministry.

Sirach 45:18
Outsiders banded together against him and envied him in the wilderness: the men around Dathan and Abiram, and Korah’s assembly in anger and wrath.
  Numbers 16:1-3 — explicit named rebellion against Moses and Aaron: Sirach names Dathan, Abiram and Korah's assembly as opponents; Numbers likewise names those men and describes their congregation banding together against Moses and Aaron.
  Psalms 105:16-18 — poetic retelling of opposition to the Lord's holy Aaron: Sirach calls Aaron holy and names Dathan and Abiram among the rebels; the psalm likewise calls Aaron the Lord's holy one and names Dathan and Abiram in the ensuing judgment. Its stated action is angering Moses and Aaron; Sirach specifies envy.

Sirach 45:19
The Lord saw and was not pleased, and they were brought to their end in furious wrath. He worked portents upon them, consuming them in His flaming fire.
  Numbers 16:35 — explicit divine fire consuming rebellious incense-offerers: Sirach recalls rebels consumed by divine fire; Numbers identifies the two hundred fifty incense-offerers consumed by fire from the Lord. The earlier context separately narrates the earth swallowing the other rebels.

Sirach 45:20
He added glory to Aaron and gave him an inheritance. He allotted him the firstfruits of the first produce; He prepared bread in abundance first for them.
  Numbers 18:8-13 — explicit firstfruits allotted to Aaron as an honorable share: Sirach says Aaron received added glory, an inheritance and first produce for food; Numbers likewise grants him a share of honor from holy gifts and specifies firstfruits of oil, wine, grain and the land's produce.

Sirach 45:21
For they will eat the Lord’s sacrifices, which He gave both to him and to his offspring.
  Numbers 18:9-10 — explicit holy offerings eaten by Aaron and his sons: Sirach says Aaron and his offspring eat the Lord's sacrifices; Numbers likewise gives them the holy offerings and explicitly directs Aaron and his sons to eat them.

Sirach 45:22
But he will inherit nothing in the people’s land, and he has no portion among the people, for He Himself is your portion and inheritance.
  Numbers 18:20 — close Lord-as-inheritance formula for Aaron: Both deny Aaron a landed portion among Israel and identify the Lord Himself as his portion and inheritance.

Sirach 45:23
Phinehas son of Eleazar was third in glory, because he was zealous in the fear of the Lord and stood firm when the people turned away, with the goodness of his soul’s eagerness. He made atonement for Israel.
  Numbers 25:10-13 — explicit Phinehas's zeal and atonement: Sirach praises Phinehas's zeal and atonement for Israel; Numbers likewise names Phinehas son of Eleazar, credits his zeal with turning away wrath and says he made atonement for Israel.
  Psalms 105:28-31 — poetic counterpart to Phinehas standing and making atonement: Sirach says Phinehas stood firm amid the people's turning away and made atonement; the psalm likewise describes Baal-peor worship, Phinehas standing and making atonement, and the outbreak ceasing.

Sirach 45:24
Therefore a covenant of peace was established for him, to preside over holy things and His people, so that the majesty of the priesthood might belong to him and his offspring for ever.
  Numbers 25:12-13 — explicit covenant of peace and lasting priesthood for Phinehas: Both join God's covenant of peace for Phinehas with enduring priesthood for him and his descendants.

Sirach 45:25
There was also a covenant for David son of Jesse, of the tribe of Judah: the inheritance of a king passes from son to son alone; Aaron’s inheritance belongs also to his offspring.
  Psalms 88:4-5 — explicit Davidic covenant and dynastic succession: Sirach recalls the covenant for David and royal inheritance through sons; the psalm likewise joins the covenant and oath to David with enduring seed and a throne through generations. Sirach adds the comparison with Aaron's inheritance.

Sirach 45:26
May He give you wisdom in your heart to judge His people in righteousness, so that their good things may not disappear, and their glory may last through their generations.
  1 Kings 3:9-12 — close prayer for a wise heart to judge God's people rightly: Sirach asks for heart-wisdom to judge God's people in righteousness; Solomon likewise requests a heart to hear and judge the people in righteousness, and receives a discerning and wise heart. The royal request provides the parallel to Sirach's concluding wish.

Sirach 46:1
Mighty in war was Joshua son of Nun, Moses’ successor in prophecies. In keeping with his name he became great in the salvation of His chosen ones, taking vengeance on enemies who rose against them, so that he might give Israel its inheritance.
  Numbers 27:18-23 — historical recollection: Sirach identifies Joshua son of Nun as Moses' successor. Numbers records Moses presenting Joshua before Eleazar and the congregation, laying hands on him, and giving him authority so Israel will obey him.
  Joshua 11:23 — historical recollection: Sirach praises Joshua for giving Israel its inheritance after warfare. Joshua's narrative closes the conquest summary by saying that he gave the land to Israel as tribal inheritances and that the land ceased being at war.

Sirach 46:2
How glorious he was when he raised his hands and stretched out his sword against cities!
  Joshua 8:18-19 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls Joshua stretching out an armed hand against cities. At Ai, Joshua stretches his hand toward the city and the ambush rises to capture it; the target specifies a javelin, whereas Sirach calls the weapon a sword.

Sirach 46:3
Who before him stood so firm? For he himself brought on the Lord’s wars.
  Joshua 10:14 — historical recollection: Sirach characterizes Joshua's campaigns as the Lord's wars. Joshua's account of the exceptional day at Gibeon expressly says that the Lord fought alongside Israel.

Sirach 46:4
Was not the sun held back by his hand, and did not one day become as two?
  Joshua 10:12-14 — historical recollection: Sirach's extended day and restrained sun recall Joshua's request that the sun stand over Gibeon. The narrative says that the sun did not advance toward sunset for a full day and that God listened to Joshua.

Sirach 46:5
He called upon the Most High Ruler when enemies pressed him on every side, and the great Lord answered him with hailstones of mighty power.
  Joshua 10:10-14 — historical recollection: Sirach joins Joshua's appeal to the Lord with an answer through hailstones. Joshua's narrative describes the Lord hurling hailstones on the fleeing enemies, records Joshua speaking to the Lord, and concludes that God listened to a human being.

Sirach 46:6
He hurled war down upon a nation and destroyed those who resisted on the descent, so that the nations might know his full armament, because his war was before the Lord.
  Joshua 10:10-11 — historical recollection: Sirach places the enemies' destruction on a descent. Joshua locates the rout and heaven-sent hailstones on the descent of Horonin, giving a concrete setting for this recollection of the Lord's warfare.

Sirach 46:7
For he followed the Ruler, and in Moses’ days he showed mercy, he and Caleb son of Jephunneh, by standing against the assembly, restraining the people from sin, and stilling wicked grumbling.
  Numbers 14:5-10 — historical recollection: Sirach names Joshua and Caleb as the two who opposed the assembly's rebellious grumbling. Numbers records them tearing their garments, praising the land, and urging the congregation not to desert the Lord or fear its inhabitants.

Sirach 46:8
They alone, being two, were preserved from six hundred thousand foot soldiers, to bring them into an inheritance, into a land flowing with milk and honey.
  Numbers 14:28-31 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls the exceptional preservation of Joshua and Caleb for entry into the promised land. Numbers excludes the grumbling generation from entry but expressly excepts these two men; their survival is the shared point, without deriving Sirach's troop figure from this passage.
  Numbers 26:63-65 — historical confirmation: Sirach says that Joshua and Caleb alone survived from the earlier generation for the inheritance. The later census explicitly reports that none of the men registered in the wilderness remained except Joshua and Caleb.

Sirach 46:9
The Lord gave Caleb strength, and it remained with him until old age, so that he might mount the high places of the land; his offspring possessed an inheritance,
  Joshua 14:9-14 — historical recollection: Sirach praises Caleb's enduring strength, mountain inheritance, and descendants' possession. Caleb says that he is still strong at eighty-five, asks for the mountain, recalls the promise to his children, and receives Hebron.
  Numbers 14:24 — promise and subsequent recollection: Sirach's account of Caleb's offspring inheriting land recalls the Lord's promise that Caleb would enter the land and his seed would inherit it because he followed the Lord.

Sirach 46:10
so that all the sons of Israel might see that it is good to walk after the Lord.
  Joshua 14:14 — historical and ethical connection: Sirach draws the lesson that following the Lord is good from Caleb's experience. Joshua explains Caleb's lasting possession of Hebron by his following the command of the Lord, making the same connection between his loyalty and inheritance.

Sirach 46:11
The judges too, each by his own name, whose hearts did not prostitute themselves and who did not turn away from the Lord—may their remembrance be among blessings!
  Judges 2:16-19 — historical survey: Sirach honors judges who did not turn from the Lord. Judges describes the Lord raising judges and remaining with them to rescue Israel, while distinguishing the people's recurring abandonment of the Lord after a judge died.
  Hebrews 11:32-34 — parallel remembrance: Sirach commemorates faithful judges as a group. Hebrews names Gideon, Barak, Samson, and Jephthah among those remembered for faith and victories, offering another retrospective honoring of these leaders.

Sirach 46:12
May their bones flourish again from their place, and may their name pass onward to their honored sons.
  Isaiah 66:14 — shared imagery: Sirach wishes that the judges' bones would flourish again; Isaiah describes bones springing up like vegetation when the Lord's servants rejoice. The shared image expresses renewed flourishing without requiring both passages to describe the same event.
  Sirach 49:10 — literary refrain: The wish that honored predecessors' bones flourish again from their place recurs nearly word for word. Sirach applies it here to the judges and later to the twelve prophets.

Sirach 46:13
Beloved by his Lord, Samuel, the Lord’s prophet, established a kingdom and anointed rulers over His people.
  1 Samuel 10:1 — historical recollection: Sirach credits Samuel with anointing Israel's rulers. In the narrative whose preceding verses identify Saul, Samuel pours oil on him and declares that the Lord has anointed him ruler over Israel.
  1 Samuel 16:12-13 — historical recollection: Sirach's reference to Samuel anointing rulers also corresponds to David's anointing. The Lord instructs Samuel to anoint David, and Samuel anoints David among his brothers.

Sirach 46:14
By the Lord’s law he judged the assembly, and the Lord visited Jacob.
  1 Samuel 7:15-17 — historical recollection: Sirach describes Samuel judging the assembly. The narrative says that he judged Israel throughout his life and lists the circuit of places where he carried out that responsibility.

Sirach 46:15
By his faithfulness he was proved a prophet, and through his words he was known to be trustworthy in vision.
  1 Samuel 3:19-21 — historical recollection: Sirach joins Samuel's prophetic reliability with recognition of his words. The narrative says none of his words fell to the ground and that all Israel recognized him as a faithful prophet to the Lord.

Sirach 46:16
He called upon the Lord, the Ruler, when his enemies pressed him on every side, with an offering of a suckling lamb.
  1 Samuel 7:7-9 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls Samuel appealing to the Lord amid an enemy threat while offering a suckling lamb. The narrative supplies all three details: the enemy advance, Samuel's prayer for Israel, and the nursing lamb offered as a whole burnt offering.

Sirach 46:17
The Lord thundered from heaven and made His voice heard with a great sound.
  1 Samuel 7:10 — historical recollection: Sirach's heavenly thunder and great divine voice recall the Lord thundering with a great voice against the attacking foreigners while Samuel offered the sacrifice.

Sirach 46:18
He destroyed the leaders of the Tyrians and all the rulers of the Philistines.
  1 Samuel 7:7-13 — historical recollection with a qualification: Sirach attributes the defeat of hostile rulers to Samuel's period. The narrative describes enemy satraps advancing, their defeat during Samuel's intercession, and the Lord humbling the foreigners throughout Samuel's days; it does not name the Tyrian leaders mentioned by Sirach.

Sirach 46:19
Before the time of his everlasting sleep, he testified before the Lord and His anointed: “I have taken no property—not even sandals—from any human being.” And no person brought an accusation against him.
  1 Samuel 12:3-5 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls Samuel's public declaration that he had taken no property, not even sandals, and the absence of accusations. The narrative preserves his challenge before the Lord and His anointed, explicitly mentions a sandal, and records the people's confirmation that he had taken nothing.

Sirach 46:20
Even after he fell asleep, he prophesied and showed the king his end. He raised his voice from the earth in prophecy to wipe out the people’s lawlessness.
  1 Samuel 28:13-19 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls Samuel speaking from the earth after death to announce the king's end. The narrative identifies the figure rising from the earth as Samuel and records his warning that Saul and his sons would fall and Israel's camp would be handed to its enemies.

Sirach 47:1
After him Nathan arose to prophesy in David’s days.
  2 Samuel 7:2-5 — historical recollection: Sirach places Nathan's prophetic activity in David's reign. The narrative names Nathan as the prophet to whom David speaks and then records the Lord's word coming to Nathan with a message for David.

Sirach 47:2
As fat is set apart from a peace offering, so David was set apart from the sons of Israel.
  Leviticus 3:3-5 — cultic imagery: Sirach compares David's selection from Israel with fat set apart from a peace offering. Leviticus directs the removal of specified fat from the sacrifice of deliverance and its offering on the altar, supplying the sacrificial action used in the comparison.
  1 Samuel 16:10-13 — historical illustration: Sirach describes David as set apart from Israel. The anointing narrative distinguishes David from Jesse's other sons: the Lord has not chosen those presented earlier, but expressly directs Samuel to anoint David.

Sirach 47:3
He played with lions as with young goats, and with bears as with lambs of the flock.
  1 Samuel 17:34-37 — historical recollection: Sirach's image of David handling lions and bears recalls his account of protecting his father's flock. David tells Saul that he struck both lion and bear and rescued a sheep, crediting the Lord with his deliverance.

Sirach 47:4
In his youth did he not kill a giant and remove reproach from the people, when he raised his hand with a sling stone and brought down Goliath’s pride?
  1 Samuel 17:40-49 — historical recollection: Sirach names the youthful David, his sling stone, and Goliath's defeat. The narrative describes David choosing stones, Goliath despising him as a boy, and David slinging a stone into the warrior's forehead.

Sirach 47:5
For he called upon the Lord Most High, and He put strength in his right hand to remove a mighty warrior and raise the horn of His people.
  1 Samuel 17:45-47 — historical recollection: Sirach credits the Lord with David's victory over the mighty warrior. David's speech to Goliath expressly invokes the Lord's name and says the Lord will deliver the enemy into his hand because the battle belongs to the Lord.

Sirach 47:6
Thus they glorified him with tens of thousands and praised him with blessings of the Lord when a crown of glory was brought to him.
  1 Samuel 18:6-9 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls David being glorified with tens of thousands. The women's victory song assigns Saul thousands and David tens of thousands, and Saul's reaction confirms that this was praise of David's achievement.

Sirach 47:7
For he destroyed enemies all around and reduced his Philistine adversaries to nothing; he shattered their horn to this day.
  2 Samuel 5:17-25 — historical recollection: Sirach summarizes David's victories over his Philistine adversaries. The narrative describes two enemy advances after his anointing, his inquiries to the Lord, and the resulting defeats; this edition calls the opponents foreigners.
  2 Samuel 8:1 — historical recollection: Sirach's summary of David reducing his adversaries recalls the compact narrative statement that David struck and routed the foreigners and took territory from their hand.

Sirach 47:8
In every deed he gave thanks to the Holy Most High with a word of glory. With his whole heart he sang hymns and loved the One who made him.
  Psalms 17:1-4 — Davidic praise: Sirach praises David's love for his Maker and songs of thanksgiving. The psalm's heading identifies David's song after deliverance from his enemies, and its opening expresses love for the Lord and praise of Him as helper and salvation.
  1 Chronicles 29:10-13 — historical illustration: Sirach describes David giving thanks and glory to the Most High. Chronicles records David publicly blessing the Lord, acknowledging His power and glory, and explicitly giving thanks and praise to His glorious name.

Sirach 47:9
He stationed psalm singers before the altar, to sweeten the melodies with their sound.
  1 Chronicles 16:4-7 — historical recollection: Sirach credits David with stationing psalm singers. Chronicles records his appointment of Levites to give thanks and praise with instruments, then names David's first appointment of praise through Asaph and his brethren.
  1 Chronicles 16:39-42 — cultic and historical parallel: Sirach places singers before the altar to sweeten the melodies. Chronicles' account of David's arrangements associates the priests and continual altar offerings with named singers, cymbals, trumpets, and instruments for God's songs.

Sirach 47:10
He gave festivals splendor and adorned the appointed seasons to their completion, while they praised His holy name and made the sanctuary resound from early morning.
  1 Chronicles 23:30-31 — historical recollection: Sirach joins David's ordering of festivals with praise resounding from early morning. The Levitical duties in Chronicles explicitly include morning and evening praise and the offerings for sabbaths, new moons, and feasts.

Sirach 47:11
The Lord took away his sins, raised his horn for ever, and gave him a covenant of kings and a throne of glory in Israel.
  2 Samuel 12:13 — historical recollection: Sirach says the Lord removed David's sins. After David confesses to Nathan, the prophet expressly tells him that the Lord has put away his sin.
  2 Samuel 7:12-16 — covenant recollection: Sirach refers to a covenant of kings and a glorious throne given to David. Nathan's oracle promises David an established successor, enduring mercy, and a house, kingdom, and throne made sure for ever.
  Psalms 131:17-18 — shared Davidic imagery: Sirach describes the Lord raising David's horn for ever. The psalm uses the same royal image when the Lord promises to make a horn spring up for David, with a lamp for His anointed and shame for his enemies.

Sirach 47:12
After him arose a knowledgeable son, who because of him rested in spacious prosperity.
  1 Kings 2:10-12 — historical recollection: Sirach introduces the son who arose after David. Kings narrates David's death and Solomon's accession to his father's throne, adding that Solomon's kingdom was firmly prepared.

Sirach 47:13
Solomon reigned in days of peace; God gave him rest all around, so that he might establish a house for His name and prepare a sanctuary for ever.
  1 Kings 5:17-19 — historical recollection: Sirach connects Solomon's peace and rest on every side with building a house for God's name. Solomon's message to Hiram makes precisely that connection: David's wars prevented the work, but the Lord has now given rest and Solomon intends to build.
  1 Chronicles 22:9-10 — promise and recollection: Sirach's peaceful reign and sanctuary building correspond to the promise David recounts for Solomon. The promised son is a man of rest, Israel receives peace in his days, and he is to build a house for the Lord's name.

Sirach 47:14
How wise you became in your youth! You were filled with understanding like a river.
  1 Kings 3:7-12 — historical recollection: Sirach praises Solomon's wisdom in youth. Solomon calls himself a little child and asks for understanding to judge; the Lord answers by giving him a discerning and wise heart.
  2 Chronicles 1:10-12 — parallel historical account: Sirach describes Solomon as filled with understanding. Chronicles records his request for wisdom and understanding and God's explicit grant of both, supplying the parallel account of that gift.

Sirach 47:15
Your soul covered the earth, and you filled it with comparisons containing riddles.
  1 Kings 5:12-14 — historical recollection: Sirach links Solomon's wide influence with comparisons and riddles. Kings enumerates his parables and songs, describes his discourse about living things, and says peoples came to hear his wisdom.

Sirach 47:16
Your name reached distant islands, and you were loved for your peace.
  1 Kings 10:1-7 — narrative illustration: Sirach celebrates Solomon's name reaching distant places. The queen of Sheba hears of his name in her own land, travels to question him, and declares that his discernment surpasses the report she had heard; this is a particular example of his foreign renown.

Sirach 47:17
Countries marveled at you for songs, proverbs, comparisons, and interpretations.
  1 Kings 5:12-14 — historical recollection: Sirach associates the countries' admiration with Solomon's songs and wise sayings. Kings enumerates his songs and parables, says that peoples came to hear his wisdom, and adds that he received gifts from kings who had heard of it.
  2 Chronicles 9:1-6 — narrative illustration: Sirach says countries marveled at Solomon's interpretations. Chronicles describes the queen of Saba testing him with riddles, receiving explanations of every question, and declaring that the abundance of his wisdom exceeded its reputation.

Sirach 47:18
In the name of the Lord God, who is called the God of Israel, you gathered gold like tin and multiplied silver like lead.
  1 Kings 10:21-22 — historical recollection: Sirach portrays gold and silver as extraordinarily plentiful under Solomon. Kings describes golden vessels, silver having no value in his days, and ships bringing gold and silver, illustrating the abundance behind Sirach's metal comparisons.
  2 Chronicles 9:27 — parallel comparison: Sirach compares Solomon's plentiful gold and silver with tin and lead. Chronicles similarly describes those precious metals becoming as common as stones in Jerusalem; the comparison changes, but the asserted abundance is the same.

Sirach 47:19
You bent your loins toward women and were brought under power in your body.
  1 Kings 11:1-4 — historical recollection: Sirach addresses Solomon's submission to women. Kings names his many wives and concubines and says that his foreign wives turned his heart after their gods, explaining the compromise recalled by Sirach.
  Nehemiah 13:26 — parallel retrospective warning: Sirach recalls Solomon being brought under women's power. Nehemiah likewise appeals to Solomon's example, saying that despite his greatness and God's love for him, foreign women turned him aside.

Sirach 47:20
You put a blemish upon your glory and profaned your offspring, bringing wrath upon your children and anguish because of your folly,
  1 Kings 11:9-13 — historical recollection: Sirach connects Solomon's failure with wrath falling on his children. Kings records the Lord's anger and the announcement that the kingdom would be torn away from Solomon's son's hand, with an exception made for David's sake.

Sirach 47:21
so that the dominion became divided and a disobedient kingdom began from Ephraim.
  1 Kings 12:16-20 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls the divided dominion and a separate disobedient kingdom. Kings narrates Israel's rejection of David's house and its installation of Jeroboam as king, while Judah and Benjamin remain with David's house.

Sirach 47:22
But the Lord will never abandon His mercy or let any of His words fail. He will never wipe out His chosen one’s descendants or remove the offspring of the one who loved Him. He gave Jacob a remnant, and David a root from him.
  Psalms 88:29-38 — Davidic covenant parallel: Sirach insists that the Lord's mercy and word will not fail and that David's descendants will not be wiped out. The psalm promises enduring mercy, a faithful covenant, and David's seed and throne continuing despite correction of his sons' sins.
  1 Kings 11:11-13 — historical illustration: Sirach says the Lord preserved descendants and a remnant despite royal failure. The judgment on Solomon expressly leaves his son a scepter for David's sake and for Jerusalem, illustrating preservation within the announced division.

Sirach 47:23
Solomon rested with his fathers and left after him, from his offspring, the people’s foolishness: Rehoboam, deficient in understanding, who made the people revolt through his counsel.
  1 Kings 12:6-16 — historical recollection: Sirach attributes the revolt under Rehoboam to his deficient counsel. Kings shows him rejecting the elders' advice, following the young men's harsh counsel, and provoking Israel's declaration that it has no share in David's house.
  2 Chronicles 10:6-16 — parallel historical account: Sirach's charge against Rehoboam corresponds to Chronicles' account of the same consultation and revolt: the elders recommend kindness, he follows the younger men instead, answers harshly, and the people depart to their dwellings.

Sirach 47:24
There was also Jeroboam son of Nebat, who made Israel sin and gave Ephraim a path of sin. Their sins multiplied exceedingly, so that they were removed from their land.
  1 Kings 12:28-33 — historical recollection: Sirach says Jeroboam made Israel sin and provided a path of sin. Kings describes his golden heifers, shrines, unauthorized priestly appointments, and invented feast, explicitly calling the matter a sin.
  2 Kings 17:21-23 — historical recollection: Sirach connects Jeroboam's influence with Israel's eventual removal from its land. Kings explicitly says that Jeroboam thrust Israel away from the Lord, that Israel persisted in his sins, and that the nation was deported to Assyria.

Sirach 47:25
They sought out every kind of wickedness, until vengeance came upon them.
  2 Kings 17:13-18 — historical recollection: Sirach describes accumulating wickedness until vengeance came. Kings recounts the rejected prophetic warnings, the abandonment of the commandments and idolatrous practices, and the Lord's anger and removal of Israel.

Sirach 48:1
Then Elijah arose, a prophet like fire, and his word burned like a torch.
  Jeremiah 23:28-29 — prophetic imagery parallel: Sirach compares Elijah's prophetic word to a burning torch. Jeremiah contrasts dreams with the Lord's true word and compares that word to blazing fire; the parallel concerns the force of prophetic speech, while the prophets are different.

Sirach 48:2
He brought famine upon them and made them few through his zeal.
  1 Kings 18:1-2 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls Elijah bringing famine upon the people. Kings places a severe famine in Samaria within Elijah's ministry and records the Lord's instruction that Elijah appear before Ahab as rain is promised.
  Luke 4:25-26 — historical recollection: Sirach remembers the famine in Elijah's days. Jesus likewise recalls heaven being shut and a great famine during Elijah's ministry, adding the particular episode of his being sent to the widow in Sidon's territory.

Sirach 48:3
By the Lord’s word he held back heaven; in the same way he brought down fire three times.
  1 Kings 17:1 — historical recollection: Sirach says Elijah shut heaven by the Lord's word. Elijah's declaration to Ahab in Kings explicitly withholds dew and rain except through the utterance of his word, under oath by the living God of Israel.
  1 Kings 18:36-39 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls Elijah bringing down fire from heaven. Kings narrates one such occasion: Elijah appeals to the Lord with fire, fire consumes the offering and its surroundings, and the people confess the Lord as God.
  2 Kings 1:9-12 — historical recollection: Sirach's account of fire descending through Elijah is supported by Kings' two successive episodes in which fire consumes a captain and his fifty men.
  James 5:17-18 — historical recollection: Sirach associates Elijah with heaven being shut. James expressly names Elijah, describes his earnest prayer that rain cease, and contrasts the ensuing drought with the rain and fruitfulness following his later prayer.

Sirach 48:4
How glorious you became, Elijah, through your wonders! Who can boast of being like you?
  1 Kings 18:36-39 — narrative illustration: Sirach praises Elijah's remarkable wonders. The fire answering his appeal in Kings provides a concrete example of those wonders, with the people falling before God after the offering, water, stones, and dust are consumed.

Sirach 48:5
You raised a dead man from death and from Hades by the word of the Most High.
  1 Kings 17:17-24 — historical recollection: Sirach credits Elijah with raising a dead person. Kings recounts the widow's breathless son, Elijah's prayer that the child's soul return, and his presentation of the living child to his mother.

Sirach 48:6
You brought kings down to destruction and glorious men from their bed.
  2 Kings 1:15-17 — historical recollection: Sirach speaks of Elijah bringing down rulers from their beds. Kings records his message that the king will not leave the bed of illness but will die, followed by the king's death according to that word.
  1 Kings 20:17-22 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls Elijah's judgments against kings. In this edition's chapter 20, Elijah confronts Ahab over Naboth's vineyard and announces calamity and the destruction of his house; the passage records the judgment announced, rather than its later execution.

Sirach 48:7
You heard rebuke at Sinai and judgments of vengeance at Horeb.
  1 Kings 19:8-17 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls Elijah hearing rebuke and judgment at Horeb. Kings places Elijah at Mount Horeb, records the divine question about his presence there, and gives the ensuing commands concerning Hazael, Jehu, Elisha, and the ensuing judgments.

Sirach 48:8
You anointed kings for retribution, and prophets as successors after him.
  1 Kings 19:15-17 — historical recollection: Sirach associates Elijah with kings appointed for retribution and a prophetic successor. Kings records the Lord commissioning the anointing of Hazael and Jehu, naming Elisha as prophet in Elijah's place, and describing the succeeding agents of judgment.

Sirach 48:9
You were taken up in a whirlwind of fire, in a chariot with fiery horses.
  2 Kings 2:11-12 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls Elijah's fiery departure. Kings describes the chariot and horses of fire separating Elijah and Elisha, Elijah's ascent in a tumult, and Elisha watching until he sees him no more.

Sirach 48:10
You were recorded in rebukes for appointed times: to still anger before wrath, to turn a father’s heart toward a son, and to restore the tribes of Jacob.
  Malachi 3:22-23 — prophetic promise parallel: Sirach associates Elijah's appointed work with turning a father's heart toward a son before wrath. Malachi, at these native anchors, promises Elijah before the Lord's day and the restoration of a father's heart toward a son to avert the land's destruction.
  Luke 1:16-17 — prophetic mission parallel: Sirach connects Elijah with restored hearts and Israel's restoration. Luke describes John's mission in Elijah's spirit and power as turning Israel to God and fathers' hearts toward children; this relates the missions without identifying John as the same person as Elijah.
  Matthew 17:10-13 — prophetic interpretation parallel: Sirach recalls Elijah's appointed restorative task. Matthew records the expectation that Elijah restore all things and Jesus' interpretation concerning John the Baptist, preserving the Gospel's own distinction between the expectation and its interpretation.

Sirach 48:12
Elijah was covered by a whirlwind, and Elisha was filled with his spirit. In his days he was not shaken by a ruler, and no one overpowered him.
  2 Kings 2:11-15 — historical recollection: Sirach connects Elijah's departure with Elisha receiving his spirit. Kings narrates the departure, Elisha's subsequent division of the Jordan, and the prophetic community's declaration that Elijah's spirit has come to rest upon Elisha.
  2 Kings 3:11-14 — narrative illustration: Sirach says Elisha was not shaken by a ruler. Kings supplies a particular example: Elisha confronts Israel's king, directs him to his father's prophets, and says he regards him only on account of Jehoshaphat.

Sirach 48:13
No word surpassed him, and even in sleep his body prophesied.
  2 Kings 13:20-21 — historical recollection: Sirach describes Elisha's body as prophetic even after death. Kings relates a dead man's revival upon touching Elisha's bones, providing the bodily wonder behind that description without reporting a spoken prophecy from the corpse.

Sirach 48:14
In his life he performed portents, and at his end his works were wondrous.
  2 Kings 4:32-37 — narrative illustration: Sirach praises Elisha's portents during life. Kings gives the concrete example of his prayer and actions over the Shunammite woman's dead child, who opens his eyes and is returned to his mother.
