  2 Kings 13:20-21 — historical recollection: Sirach distinguishes Elisha's wonders during life from those at his end. Kings' account of a man revived through contact with Elisha's bones supplies the posthumous counterpart to that contrast.

Sirach 48:15
Through all these things the people did not repent or turn away from their sins, until they were plundered from their land and scattered throughout all the earth.
  2 Kings 17:13-23 — historical recollection: Sirach links refusal to repent with removal and scattering. Kings recounts Israel's refusal to heed prophetic warnings and its persistence in sins until the Lord removed Israel and it was deported to Assyria.
  Deuteronomy 28:63-64 — covenant consequence parallel: Sirach recounts the unrepentant people's removal and scattering over the earth. Deuteronomy warns of removal from the inherited land and dispersion among nations from one end of the earth to the other, supplying the corresponding covenant consequence.

Sirach 48:16
The people were left few in number, with a ruler in David’s house. Some of them did what was pleasing, but others multiplied sins.
  2 Kings 17:18-19 — historical recollection: Sirach describes a reduced remnant associated with David's ruling house and continuing disobedience. Kings says that only Judah remained after Israel's removal, then adds that Judah also failed to keep the Lord's commandments.

Sirach 48:17
Hezekiah fortified his city and brought water into its midst. With iron he dug through a steep rock and built reservoirs for water.
  2 Kings 20:20 — historical recollection: Sirach remembers Hezekiah's works bringing water into the fortified city. Kings' concluding notice expressly records his fountain and aqueduct and the bringing of water into the city.
  2 Chronicles 32:2-5 — historical recollection: Sirach combines Hezekiah's fortification and water works. Chronicles describes his response to the Assyrian threat through controlling nearby water, rebuilding the wall and towers, and adding an outer defensive wall.

Sirach 48:18
In his days Sennacherib came up and sent Rabshakeh, and he departed. He raised his hand against Zion and boasted greatly in his arrogance.
  2 Kings 18:13-17 — historical recollection: Sirach names Sennacherib's invasion and his envoy Rabshakeh. Kings places Sennacherib's attack in Hezekiah's reign and sends the officer named Rhapsakes in this edition, with other officers and a large force, to Jerusalem.
  Isaiah 36:1-4 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls the Assyrian king's envoy and his boastful challenge to Zion. Isaiah's parallel account names Sennacherib and the envoy Rapsakes, sends him to Jerusalem, and opens his challenge by invoking the authority of the great king.

Sirach 48:19
Then their hearts and hands were shaken, and they suffered pangs like women giving birth.
  2 Kings 19:1-4 — historical recollection: Sirach compares the people's terror to birth pains. In the same Assyrian crisis, Hezekiah's messengers describe affliction through the image of children reaching birth when the mother has no strength to deliver.
  Isaiah 37:1-4 — historical recollection: Sirach's birth-pain comparison recalls the crisis described in Isaiah: Hezekiah mourns and his messengers speak of a woman whose labor has come but who lacks strength to give birth.

Sirach 48:20
They called upon the merciful Lord, spreading their hands toward Him. The Holy One quickly heard them from heaven and redeemed them by Isaiah’s hand.
  2 Chronicles 32:20-22 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls prayer, God's hearing, and deliverance associated with Isaiah. Chronicles explicitly joins Hezekiah and Isaiah in prayer to heaven, then recounts the Lord's intervention and Jerusalem's deliverance from Sennacherib.
  2 Kings 19:14-20 — historical recollection: Sirach says God heard the threatened people's petition. Kings records Hezekiah's plea for deliverance and Isaiah's message that the Lord has heard his prayer concerning Sennacherib.

Sirach 48:21
He struck the Assyrians’ camp, and His angel destroyed them.
  2 Kings 19:35 — historical recollection: Sirach credits an angel with striking the Assyrian camp. Kings narrates the Lord's angel striking that camp by night and the discovery of its dead in the morning.
  Isaiah 37:36 — historical recollection: Sirach's destruction of the Assyrian camp through an angel has a parallel account in Isaiah, which names the angel of the Lord, the Assyrian camp, and the bodies discovered the following morning.

Sirach 48:22
For Hezekiah did what was pleasing to the Lord and stood strong in the ways of his father David, which Isaiah the prophet commanded, great and faithful in his vision.
  2 Kings 18:1-3 — historical recollection: Sirach commends Hezekiah for following David's ways. Kings identifies Hezekiah's accession and expressly assesses his conduct by everything his father David had done.

Sirach 48:23
In his days the sun went backward, and He added life to the king.
  2 Kings 20:4-11 — historical recollection: Sirach joins the backward movement of the sun with the extension of the king's life. Kings promises Hezekiah fifteen additional years and narrates Isaiah's prayer and the shadow's return ten steps as the healing sign.
  Isaiah 38:4-8 — historical recollection: Sirach's recollection of the reversed sun and prolonged life corresponds to Isaiah's account, where fifteen years are added to Hezekiah and the sun goes back up the ten steps as the promised sign.

Sirach 48:24
With a great spirit he saw the last things and comforted those mourning in Zion.
  Isaiah 61:1-3 — prophetic contents recalled: Sirach praises Isaiah's great spirit and comfort for Zion's mourners. Isaiah presents the Lord's spirit upon the commissioned speaker and explicitly promises comfort and transformed mourning for Zion.
  Isaiah 40:1-2 — prophetic contents recalled: Sirach recalls Isaiah comforting Zion's mourners. Isaiah's repeated call to comfort God's people is directed to Jerusalem, whose humiliation is fulfilled and whose sin is loosed.

Sirach 48:25
He showed what would happen to the end of the age, and hidden things before they came to pass.
  Isaiah 42:8-9 — prophetic contents recalled: Sirach describes Isaiah disclosing hidden things before they occur. Isaiah records the Lord announcing new things before they spring up; the foresight is presented in the target as God's declaration through the prophetic text.

Sirach 49:1
The remembrance of Josiah is like a blend of incense prepared by a perfumer’s skill. In every mouth it will be sweet as honey, and like music at a wine banquet.
  Ecclesiastes 7:1 — reputation imagery parallel: Sirach compares the memory of Josiah to finely prepared perfume. Ecclesiastes compares the value of a name with good oil, providing a related wisdom image in which personal reputation and good oil are brought together.

Sirach 49:2
He was rightly directed in bringing the people back, and he removed the abominations of lawlessness.
  2 Kings 23:3-6 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls Josiah turning the people back and removing abominations. Kings describes the renewed covenant, the people's participation, and his removal and destruction of objects associated with idolatrous worship.
  2 Chronicles 34:3-7 — historical recollection: Sirach's removal of abominations under Josiah corresponds to Chronicles' account of his seeking the Lord and cleansing Judah, Jerusalem, and other cities by destroying altars and sacred objects.

Sirach 49:3
He directed his heart toward the Lord; in days of lawless people he strengthened godliness.
  2 Kings 23:25 — historical recollection: Sirach praises Josiah's directed heart and strengthening of godliness. Kings similarly singles him out for returning to the Lord with his whole heart, soul, and strength according to Moses' law.
  2 Chronicles 34:31-33 — historical recollection: Sirach connects Josiah's heart toward God with the people's godliness. Chronicles records his whole-hearted covenant, the people's renewed commitment, and his making those present serve the Lord throughout his days.

Sirach 49:4
Except for David, Hezekiah, and Josiah, they all committed offenses. For they abandoned the law of the Most High; the kings of Judah came to an end.
  2 Chronicles 36:11-16 — historical recollection: Sirach links Judah's royal downfall with abandonment of the law. Chronicles gives the particular final-reign example of Zedekiah's refusal to return to God, the people's breaches of faith, and their rejection of prophetic messages until wrath came.

Sirach 49:5
For they gave their horn to others and their glory to a foreign nation.
  2 Chronicles 36:17-20 — historical recollection: Sirach describes Judah's strength and glory passing to foreigners. Chronicles recounts defeat by the Chaldean king, transfer of temple and royal treasures to Babylon, and the survivors' servitude there.

Sirach 49:6
They burned the chosen city of the sanctuary and made its streets desolate,
  2 Kings 25:8-11 — historical recollection: Sirach recalls the city of the sanctuary being burned and its streets made desolate. Kings records the burning of the Lord's house, the king's house, and Jerusalem's houses, followed by removal of the remaining people.
  2 Chronicles 36:19-20 — historical recollection: Sirach's burning and desolation of Jerusalem correspond to Chronicles' destruction of the Lord's house, city wall, and citadels, followed by deportation of the survivors.

Sirach 49:7
by Jeremiah’s hand. For they mistreated him, though he was sanctified in the womb as a prophet to uproot, afflict, and destroy, and likewise to build and plant.
  Jeremiah 1:4-10 — prophetic commission recalled: Sirach recalls Jeremiah's consecration in the womb and commission to destroy, build, and plant. Jeremiah's opening call explicitly places consecration before birth and appoints him to uproot, demolish, destroy, build, and plant.

Sirach 49:8
Ezekiel saw a vision of glory, which He showed him upon a chariot of cherubim.
  Ezekiel 1:26-28 — prophetic vision recalled: Sirach praises Ezekiel's vision of glory. Ezekiel describes the throne-like form, its radiance, and the rainbow-like brightness, explicitly identifying this as the appearance of the likeness of the Lord's glory.
  Ezekiel 10:15-20 — prophetic vision recalled: Sirach associates Ezekiel's glorious vision with the cherubim's chariot. Ezekiel describes the cherubim rising with wheels, the Lord's glory mounting them, and his recognition that these are the creatures seen at the river Chebar.

Sirach 49:9
For He remembered the enemies in a rainstorm, and remembered to do good to those who make their ways straight.
  Ezekiel 38:22-23 — storm-judgment imagery parallel: Sirach speaks of God remembering enemies in a rainstorm. Ezekiel's oracle against Gog describes divine judgment through flooding rain and hailstones. The supported connection is this specific storm-judgment image; the passage does not establish that Sirach is identifying this particular oracle.

Sirach 49:10
May the bones of the twelve prophets flourish again from their place! For they comforted Jacob and redeemed them with the faithfulness of hope.
  Zechariah 1:13-17 — prophetic contents recalled: Sirach honors the twelve prophets for comforting Jacob and sustaining hope of redemption. Zechariah supplies a particular example: the Lord answers with comforting words and promises compassion, rebuilding, and renewed mercy for Jerusalem and Zion.
  Sirach 46:12 — same-book literary refrain: Sirach prays that the twelve prophets' bones flourish from their place. The earlier praise of the judges likewise asks that their bones flourish from their place, repeating the memorial prayer for another group of Israel's honored ancestors.

Sirach 49:11
How shall we magnify Zerubbabel? He too was like a seal upon the right hand.
  Haggai 2:23 — prophetic comparison recalled: Sirach compares Zerubbabel to a signet on the right hand. Haggai's promise names Zerubbabel, calls him God's chosen servant, and says God will make him like a signet; Haggai does not specify the hand.

Sirach 49:12
So was Joshua son of Jozadak. In their days they built a house and raised up a holy sanctuary for the Lord, prepared for everlasting glory.
  Ezra 5:1-2 — historical recollection: Sirach credits Joshua son of Jozadak and Zerubbabel with rebuilding the Lord's house. Ezra names the corresponding leaders as Iesous son of Iosedek and Zorobabel son of Salathiel and records their beginning to rebuild God's house with prophetic assistance.

Sirach 49:13
Great also is the remembrance of Nehemiah, who raised our fallen walls, set up gates and bars, and rebuilt our home sites.
  Nehemiah 6:15-16 — historical recollection: Sirach remembers Nehemiah for restoring the fallen walls. Nehemiah records the wall's completion and the surrounding nations' recognition that God brought the work to completion.
  Nehemiah 7:1-3 — historical recollection: Sirach credits Nehemiah with restored walls and gates. Nehemiah records the built wall, the setting up of doors, and arrangements to close and guard them. This supports the wall and gate details; the adjacent verse 7:4 still says that houses had not been built.

Sirach 49:14
No one was created on earth like Enoch, for he was taken up from the earth.
  Genesis 5:21-24 — historical recollection: Sirach distinguishes Enoch by his being taken from the earth. Genesis says that Enoch pleased God and was not found because God transferred him.
  Hebrews 11:5 — historical recollection: Sirach praises Enoch's exceptional removal from the earth. Hebrews explicitly recalls Enoch being taken away so as not to see death and connects that removal with his testimony of pleasing God.

Sirach 49:15
Nor was a man born like Joseph, a leader of his brothers, a support for the people; and his bones were cared for.
  Genesis 50:24-26 — historical recollection: Sirach says that care was taken of Joseph's bones. Genesis records Joseph's oath requiring his bones to be carried from Egypt at God's visitation, followed by his burial preparations and placement in a coffin.
  Exodus 13:19 — historical recollection: Sirach remembers the care of Joseph's bones. Exodus records the carrying out of his oath when Moses takes those bones on Israel's departure from Egypt.
  Joshua 24:32 — historical recollection: Sirach's remembrance of Joseph's bones has a further narrative counterpart in Joshua, which records their transport from Egypt and burial in Joseph's inherited portion at Shechem.

Sirach 49:16
Shem and Seth were glorified among people, and Adam above every living creature in creation.
  Genesis 1:26-28 — creation dignity parallel: Sirach places Adam above the living creatures in his account of ancestral honor. Genesis gives humankind God's image and authority over fish, birds, cattle, the earth, and creeping things, supplying the corresponding distinction within creation.

Sirach 50:5
How glorious he was with the people surrounding him, when he came out from the house of the veil!
  Leviticus 16:15-17 — priestly-service parallel: Sirach admires Simon as he emerges from the house of the veil. Leviticus describes the priest's service within the veil and excludes others until he comes out, supplying a parallel pattern of restricted priestly entry and exit without identifying Simon's particular festival.

Sirach 50:6
He was like the morning star among clouds, like the full moon in its days,
  Song of Songs 6:9-10 — poetic imagery parallel: Sirach compares Simon's splendor to the full moon. The Song compares its praised woman to dawn and the beautiful moon; the shared celestial image describes beauty, while the persons and settings remain different.

Sirach 50:7
like the sun shining upon the sanctuary of the Most High, and like the rainbow gleaming in clouds of glory,
  Ezekiel 1:28 — poetic imagery parallel: Sirach compares Simon's splendor to a rainbow gleaming in glorious clouds. Ezekiel uses the rainbow in a rainy cloud to describe the brightness of the Lord's glory. The comparison concerns the luminous image, without equating the priest with God.

Sirach 50:8
like a rose blossom in the days of new growth, like lilies beside an outflow of water, like a shoot of Lebanon in summer days,
  Hosea 14:6-7 — poetic imagery parallel: Sirach portrays Simon through lilies near water and a shoot of Lebanon. Hosea describes restored Israel receiving dew, blossoming like a lily, and putting down roots like Lebanon; both use flourishing plants and Lebanon to portray renewed beauty.

Sirach 50:9
like fire and frankincense upon a firepan, like a vessel of beaten gold adorned with every costly stone,
  Leviticus 16:12-13 — cultic imagery parallel: Sirach likens Simon's splendor to fire and frankincense upon a firepan. Leviticus describes the priest taking a firepan of burning coals and putting incense on the fire before the Lord, providing the particular ritual image used in the simile.

Sirach 50:10
like an olive tree flourishing with fruit, and like a cypress rising into the clouds.
  Sirach 24:13-14 — same-book imagery parallel: Sirach compares Simon to a fruitful olive and a towering cypress. Wisdom's earlier self-description likewise uses the cypress and stately olive; the botanical images recur with different subjects.

Sirach 50:11
When he took up the robe of glory and clothed himself with the fullness of splendor, as he ascended the holy altar he glorified the sanctuary’s enclosure.
  Exodus 28:2-4 — priestly-vestment parallel: Sirach describes Simon's robe of glory and priestly splendor. Exodus orders Aaron's holy robe for honor and glory and enumerates garments made for priestly service, supplying the corresponding purpose of glorious priestly dress.

Sirach 50:12
When he received portions from the priests’ hands, standing himself beside the altar hearth, a crown of brothers surrounded him, like a growth of cedars in Lebanon; they encircled him like palm trunks,
  Psalms 91:13-14 — poetic imagery parallel: Sirach compares the priests surrounding the altar to Lebanon's cedars and palm trunks. The Psalm compares righteous people to palms and Lebanon's cedars and places their flourishing in the Lord's house and courts, joining the same trees with worship's setting.

Sirach 50:13
all the sons of Aaron in their glory, with the Lord’s offering in their hands before the whole assembly of Israel.
  Leviticus 9:5-9 — priestly-service parallel: Sirach depicts Aaron's sons with offerings before Israel's assembly. Leviticus places the congregation before the Lord and describes Aaron at the altar with his sons bringing him the sacrificial blood.

Sirach 50:14
As he completed his ministry at the altars, adorning the offering of the Most High Almighty,
  2 Chronicles 29:27-28 — temple-service parallel: Sirach describes the high priest completing his altar ministry and offering. Chronicles describes the offering on the altar and the accompanying assembly's worship, singing, and trumpets continuing until the burnt offering is finished.

Sirach 50:15
he stretched his hand over the libation vessel and poured a drink offering from the blood of the grape. He poured it at the altar’s foundations, a pleasing fragrance to the Most High, King of all.
  Numbers 28:7 — cultic instruction parallel: Sirach recounts the pouring of a drink offering before the Most High. Numbers prescribes pouring out a drink offering of strong drink to the Lord in the holy place; it supports the libation practice without specifying Sirach's altar-foundation detail.
  Deuteronomy 32:14 — wine imagery parallel: Sirach calls the libation wine the blood of the grape. Deuteronomy likewise names wine as the blood of the grape in its account of provision, sharing the particular image although the target describes drinking rather than a libation.

Sirach 50:16
Then the sons of Aaron cried out and sounded their hammered trumpets. They made a great sound heard as a remembrance before the Most High.
  Numbers 10:8-10 — cultic instruction parallel: Sirach describes Aaron's sons sounding trumpets as a remembrance before the Most High. Numbers assigns the trumpets to Aaron's priestly sons and expressly makes their sounding over offerings a remembrance before God.

Sirach 50:17
Then all the people together hurried and fell facedown upon the earth to worship their Lord, the Almighty God Most High.
  2 Chronicles 7:1-3 — public-worship parallel: Sirach's assembled people fall facedown to worship the Lord during the temple service. Chronicles recounts Israel's collective prostration on the pavement in response to the Lord's glory at the temple dedication; these are corresponding acts in different ceremonies.

Sirach 50:18
The psalm singers praised with their voices; amid a very great sound the melody grew sweet.
  2 Chronicles 5:12-13 — temple-music parallel: Sirach praises the psalm singers' voices and the great sound of their melody. Chronicles describes the assembled Levitical singers and instruments joining in one voice to give thanks and praise at the sanctuary.

Sirach 50:19
The people entreated the Lord Most High in prayer before the Merciful One, until the Lord’s ceremony was completed and they finished His service.
  Luke 1:8-10 — temple-prayer parallel: Sirach describes the people praying while the Lord's ceremony is completed. Luke likewise places the congregation's prayer alongside priestly service, with the multitude praying outside while the priest burns incense inside.

Sirach 50:20
Then he came down and raised his hands over the whole assembly of the sons of Israel, to give the Lord’s blessing from his lips and to glory in His name.
  Leviticus 9:22-23 — priestly-blessing parallel: Sirach describes Simon coming down and raising his hands over Israel to bless them. Leviticus describes Aaron lifting his hands over the people, blessing them, and coming down after the offerings; the correspondence concerns the priestly gesture and blessing.
  Numbers 6:23-26 — priestly-blessing instruction: Sirach associates the priest's spoken blessing with the Lord's name. Numbers instructs Aaron and his sons to bless Israel, explicitly placing God's name upon them in this edition's verse 23, and gives the words of the blessing.

Sirach 50:21
They bowed down a second time to receive the blessing from the Most High.
  Leviticus 9:22-24 — public-worship parallel: Sirach depicts the people bowing to receive the blessing after priestly service. Leviticus joins the priests' blessing, the appearance of divine glory, and the congregation's falling on their faces; the latter response also follows the fire consuming the offering.

Sirach 50:22
And now bless the God of all, who does great things everywhere, who exalts our days from the womb and deals with us according to His mercy.
  Psalms 70:5-6 — thanksgiving parallel: Sirach calls for blessing the God who exalts human days from the womb. The Psalm acknowledges support from the womb and shelter from the mother's belly, and joins that lifelong dependence with continual praise.

Sirach 50:23
May He give us gladness of heart, and may there be peace in Israel in our days, throughout the days of eternity.
  Romans 15:13 — prayer parallel: Sirach petitions God for gladness of heart and peace in Israel. Romans similarly prays that God fill its hearers with joy and peace in believing, within its own setting of hope shared by Jews and Gentiles.

Sirach 50:24
May He entrust His mercy to us, and in our days may He redeem us.
  Psalms 129:7-8 — mercy-and-redemption parallel: Sirach prays that God's mercy be entrusted to His people and that He redeem them. The Psalm connects the Lord's mercy with abundant redemption and explicitly promises the redemption of Israel.

Sirach 50:27
Jesus son of Sirach Eleazar, the Jerusalemite, inscribed instruction in understanding and knowledge in this book; he poured wisdom like rain from his heart.
  Deuteronomy 32:1-3 — teaching imagery parallel: Sirach describes the author's wisdom pouring like rain from his heart. Deuteronomy asks that the speaker's utterance be awaited like rain and his words descend like dew, using rainfall imagery for beneficial teaching without identifying the two speakers.

Sirach 50:28
Blessed is the one who will occupy himself with these things; by setting them upon his heart he will become wise.
  Psalms 118:97-100 — wisdom-through-meditation parallel: Sirach promises wisdom to the reader who occupies himself with these teachings and places them on his heart. The Psalm connects meditation on God's law and testimonies with becoming wiser and gaining understanding.

Sirach 50:29
For if he does them, he will be strong for everything, because the Lord’s light is his path.
  Psalms 118:105 — guidance imagery parallel: Sirach says that one practicing the instruction has the Lord's light as his path. The Psalm calls God's word a lamp to the feet and a light to the paths, sharing the image of divine guidance illuminating one's course.

Sirach 51:1
I will give thanks to Thee, Lord and King, and praise Thee, God my Savior. I give thanks to Thy name,
  Psalms 144:1-2 — thanksgiving parallel: Sirach addresses God as Lord and King and promises praise and thanksgiving to His name. The Psalm likewise addresses God as King and promises daily blessing and praise of His name.

Sirach 51:2
because Thou didst become my shelter and helper. Thou didst redeem my body from destruction and from the snare of a slanderous tongue, from lips that work falsehood. Before those standing around, Thou didst become my helper and redeem me,
  Psalms 119:1-2 — deliverance-prayer parallel: Sirach thanks God for rescue from a slanderous tongue and false lips. The Psalm recounts an answered cry in affliction and explicitly asks for rescue from unjust lips and a deceitful tongue.

Sirach 51:3
according to the abundance of Thy mercy and Thy name, from gnashing teeth when I was ready to be devoured, from the hand of those seeking my life, from the many afflictions I had,
  Psalms 34:16-17 — hostility imagery parallel: Sirach recalls gnashing teeth and the threat of being devoured by those seeking his life. The Psalm describes enemies gnashing their teeth and asks that the speaker's life be restored from their wickedness and from lions.

Sirach 51:4
from the choking of a fire all around, and from the midst of a fire that I did not kindle,
  Psalms 65:10-12 — deliverance imagery parallel: Sirach thanks God for rescue from surrounding fire. The Psalm recalls a community passing through fire and water before being brought to refreshment; it presents its ordeal as divine testing, while Sirach specifies a fire he did not kindle.

Sirach 51:5
from the depth of Hades’ belly, from an unclean tongue and a false word—
  Jonah 2:2-3 — deliverance-prayer parallel: Sirach describes rescue from the belly of Hades. Jonah's prayer from the sea creature likewise speaks of crying from the belly of Hades and being heard by God, sharing this particular image of extremity.

Sirach 51:6
slander to a king from an unjust tongue. My soul drew near to death, and my life was close to Hades below.
  1 Samuel 24:9-13 — narrative parallel: Sirach recounts slander reaching a king and threatening his life. David likewise challenges Saul's acceptance of people's accusations, demonstrates his innocence, and says that Saul seeks to take his life; the parallel concerns the situation, without identifying Sirach's unnamed king as Saul.

Sirach 51:7
They surrounded me on every side, and there was no helper. I looked for support from people, but there was none.
  Psalms 21:12 — distress-prayer parallel: Sirach describes being surrounded without any human helper. The Psalm joins approaching affliction with the explicit absence of anyone to help, turning that condition into an appeal for God's nearness.

Sirach 51:8
I remembered Thy mercy, Lord, and Thy work from of old: Thou rescuest those who wait for Thee and savest them from the hand of enemies.
  Psalms 24:3-7 — mercy-and-hope parallel: Sirach remembers God's ancient mercy and rescue of those who wait for Him. The Psalm assures those who wait for God, addresses Him as Savior, and asks Him to remember His compassions and mercies from eternity.

Sirach 51:9
I lifted my supplication from the earth and pleaded for deliverance from death.
  Psalms 114:3-4 — deliverance-prayer parallel: Sirach recalls pleading for deliverance from death. The Psalm describes death's pangs and Hades' dangers and gives the ensuing cry to the Lord to rescue the speaker's soul.

Sirach 51:10
I called upon the Lord, the Father of my Lord, not to abandon me in days of affliction, in a time of arrogance when there was no help. I will praise Thy name continually and sing hymns with thanksgiving.
  Psalms 37:22-23 — distress-prayer parallel: Sirach asks not to be abandoned in affliction when no help is available. The Psalm likewise asks the Lord not to abandon the speaker and to attend to his aid. This supports the petition, without interpreting Sirach's distinctive address, the Father of my Lord.
  Psalms 33:2-4 — continual-praise parallel: Sirach's petition ends with the resolve to praise God's name continually. The Psalm likewise promises blessing at every time, continual praise in the mouth, and the shared exaltation of the Lord's name.

Sirach 51:11
My petition was heard, for Thou didst save me from destruction and rescue me from an evil time.
  Psalms 33:7 — answered-prayer parallel: Sirach connects the hearing of his petition with rescue from destruction and an evil time. The Psalm similarly says that the Lord heard the poor man's cry and saved him from all his afflictions.

Sirach 51:12
Therefore I will give thanks to Thee and praise Thee and bless the Lord’s name.
  Psalms 29:12-13 — thanksgiving-after-deliverance parallel: Sirach responds to the rescue just recounted with thanksgiving and praise. The Psalm moves from mourning transformed into gladness to a promise of praise and everlasting thanks, supplying the corresponding response to deliverance.

Sirach 51:13
While I was still young, before I wandered, I openly sought wisdom in my prayer.
  1 Kings 3:7-12 — narrative illustration: Sirach remembers seeking wisdom in prayer while young. Solomon's prayer similarly describes him as a little child, asks for a discerning heart, and receives the Lord's gift of a wise heart; the accounts concern different petitioners.

Sirach 51:14
Before the sanctuary I asked for her, and until the end I will seek her out.
  Sirach 6:18 — same-book lifelong-learning parallel: After recalling his youthful search, Sirach promises to seek wisdom until the end. The earlier instruction likewise spans a lifetime, beginning the choice of instruction in youth and continuing the finding of wisdom into gray-haired age.

Sirach 51:15
From her blossom, as from a ripening grape, my heart rejoiced in her. My foot stepped in uprightness; from my youth I traced her path.
  Proverbs 4:11-13 — wisdom-path imagery parallel: Sirach describes his foot walking in uprightness as he traces wisdom's path. Proverbs joins instruction in wisdom with straight tracks, unhindered steps, and holding fast to discipline, sharing the portrayal of learning as a well-directed walk.

Sirach 51:16
I inclined my ear a little and received, and found much instruction for myself.
  Proverbs 2:1-5 — instruction parallel: Sirach describes inclining his ear, receiving, and finding much instruction. Proverbs likewise connects receiving the commandment, the ear's obedience to wisdom, and the application of the heart with finding the knowledge of God.

Sirach 51:17
I made progress in her; I will give glory to the One who gives me wisdom.
  Proverbs 2:6 — divine-gift parallel: Sirach gives glory to the One who gives him wisdom. Proverbs explicitly identifies the Lord as the giver of wisdom and the source of knowledge and understanding.

Sirach 51:18
For I resolved to practice her and was zealous for the good, and I will never be put to shame.
  Psalms 118:5-6 — obedience-and-confidence parallel: Sirach resolves to practice wisdom and declares that he will not be put to shame. The Psalm similarly wishes for ways directed to keeping God's ordinances and connects attention to the commandments with freedom from shame.

Sirach 51:19
My soul has struggled for her, and I was exact in practicing the law. I spread my hands toward the height and mourned my soul’s unwitting faults.
  Psalms 18:13-14 — penitential-prayer parallel: Sirach mourns his unwitting faults while seeking faithful practice of the law. The Psalm asks who can understand transgressions and requests cleansing of hidden faults, expressing the corresponding concern for sins beyond full awareness.

Sirach 51:20
I directed my soul toward her and found her in purity. From the beginning I gained a heart together with her; therefore I will never be abandoned.
  Wisdom of Solomon 1:1-5 — wisdom-and-purity parallel: Sirach directs his soul toward wisdom and describes finding her in purity. Wisdom of Solomon joins seeking God in simplicity of heart with the warning that wisdom does not enter an evil-devising soul or dwell in a body indebted to sin.

Sirach 51:21
My inward parts were stirred to seek her out; therefore I acquired a good possession.
  Proverbs 2:3-6 — wisdom-as-treasure parallel: Sirach's inward desire to seek wisdom results in acquiring a good possession. Proverbs likewise urges searching for wisdom like silver and treasure and describes the discovery of divine knowledge and the Lord's gift of wisdom.

Sirach 51:22
The Lord gave me a tongue as my reward, and with it I will praise Him.
  Psalms 50:17 — divinely-enabled-praise parallel: Sirach acknowledges a tongue given by the Lord and resolves to use it for praise. The Psalm likewise connects the Lord's opening of the lips with the mouth's announcement of His praise.

Sirach 51:23
Draw near to me, you who are uninstructed, and lodge in a house of instruction.
  Proverbs 9:1-6 — wisdom-invitation parallel: Sirach invites the uninstructed to lodge in a house of instruction. Proverbs personifies Wisdom as building a house and summoning those lacking understanding to come, leave foolishness, and seek understanding.

Sirach 51:24
Why do you say you are lacking in these things, while your souls thirst exceedingly?
  Isaiah 55:1-3 — instruction-invitation parallel: Sirach addresses souls that thirst while lacking instruction. Isaiah likewise addresses the thirsty, invites them to freely provided nourishment, and connects listening with the soul's life and delight; the nourishment functions within an invitation to hear God.

Sirach 51:25
I opened my mouth and said, “Acquire for yourselves without silver.
  Isaiah 55:1-3 — instruction-invitation parallel: Sirach urges hearers to acquire instruction without silver. Isaiah similarly invites those without silver to buy without silver or price and to listen for the soul's life, joining the free-acquisition image with receptive hearing.

Sirach 51:26
“Put your neck beneath the yoke, and let your soul receive instruction. She is near at hand to be found.
  Matthew 11:28-30 — teaching-yoke imagery parallel: Sirach joins putting the neck under a yoke with receiving instruction. Jesus likewise invites hearers to take His yoke and learn from Him, coupling the teaching image with rest and a light burden; this is a comparison of the invitations, not an identification of their speakers.
  Sirach 6:24-28 — same-book instruction imagery parallel: Sirach's final invitation places the neck beneath wisdom's yoke. The earlier exhortation likewise places the neck in her collar, urges whole-soul pursuit, and promises the discovery of her rest.

Sirach 51:27
“See with your own eyes that I labored a little and found much rest for myself.
  Matthew 11:28-30 — labor-and-rest parallel: Sirach contrasts the little labor of seeking wisdom with the much rest he found. Jesus addresses those laboring and burdened with a promise of rest and describes His burden as light. Both connect the teacher's invitation with rest, while describing its demands as little labor or a light burden.

Sirach 51:28
“Take part in instruction at the cost of much silver, and acquire much gold through her.
  Proverbs 8:10-11 — wisdom-value parallel: Sirach urges costly participation in instruction using silver and gold as its value imagery. Proverbs places discipline and knowledge above silver and tested gold; the comparison concerns wisdom's worth and does not turn Sirach's image into a guarantee of financial profit.

Sirach 51:29
“May your soul rejoice in His mercy, and may you not be ashamed in His praise.
  Psalms 30:8-9 — joy-in-mercy parallel: Sirach exhorts the soul to rejoice in God's mercy. The Psalm likewise promises exultation and rejoicing in divine mercy and grounds that response in rescue from humiliation and an enemy's grasp.

Sirach 51:30
“Do your work before the appointed time, and He will give you your reward in His time.”
  Galatians 6:7-10 — work-and-reward parallel: Sirach urges work before the appointed time and trusts God to give the reward in His time. Galatians similarly joins doing good while opportunity remains with the assurance of reaping in the proper season if one does not give up.
